.
The graph after removal of the pendant edges and the edges adjacent to them which are never covered.

Finally, gauge transform at each white vertex by the factor
(a_{i,j}^{[n+1]}+b_{i,j}^{[n+1]}),2\leq i\leq n+1,1\leq j\leq n+1
necessary to make the Northeast and Southeast edges incident to it have weight
1
. The resulting graph has weights
\{a_{i,j}^{[n]},b_{i,j}^{[n]}:1\leq i,j\leq n\}
defined as in (
2.11
) and its partition function is therefore
Z_{n}
. We have changed the partition function
Z_{n+1}
by a factor of

(2.14)

\frac{\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}2\leq i\leq n+1\\
1\leq j\leq n+1\end{subarray}}(a_{i,j}^{[n+1]}+b_{i,j}^{[n+1]})}{\prod_{1\leq i,j\leq n+1}(a_{i,j}^{[n+1]}+b_{i,j}^{[n+1]})}=\frac{1}{\prod_{1\leq j\leq n+1}(a_{1,j}^{[n+1]}+b_{1,j}^{[n+1]})}

through the spider move and then the gauge transformation, which exactly yields (
2.12
).
∎

The recurrence of
2.4
of course gives an explicit formula for the whole partition function
Z_{n}
when applied repeatedly. Since the proof relates the Aztec diamond graphs of sizes
n
and
n+1
via the spider move and vertex-dilation,
2.2
and
2.3
give not just a relation between partition functions but a coupling between matchings of the Aztec diamonds of size
n
and size
n+1
. This coupling, viewed as a random transition map from matchings of size
n
to matchings of size
n+1
, is exactly a step of the shuffling algorithm discussed in the Introduction. As noted in
2.2
, the three cases of the coupling correspond to the deletion, slide, and creation steps of the algorithm; for a longer discussion of the equivalence of the two descriptions see e.g.
[
2
, Section 12.3]
.

2.3.
Relevant random variables

The Gamma and Beta distributions are characterized by certain invariance properties which make them natural from the perspective of the spider move on the Aztec diamond. We give background on these random variables now.

Definition 2.2
.

The
Gamma distribution

\Gamma(\chi,s)
with
shape parameter

\chi>0
and
scale parameter

s>0
is the probability distribution on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
with density

(2.15)

\frac{1}{\Gamma(\chi)s^{\chi}}x^{\chi-1}e^{-x/s}

with respect to the Lebesgue measure
dx
, where
\Gamma(\chi)
is the Gamma function. We write
X\sim\Gamma(\chi,s)
for Gamma random variables
X
.

We write
\Gamma^{-1}(\chi,s)
for the distribution of
1/X
where
X\sim\Gamma(\chi,s)
.

Note that the scale parameter just multiplies the random variable by a constant
s
. This does not affect the dimer measure as mentioned in
1.2
.

Definition 2.3
.

The
Beta distribution

\mathrm{Beta}(\alpha,\beta)
is the probability distribution on
[0,1]
with density

(2.16)

\frac{1}{\beta(\alpha,\beta)}x^{\alpha-1}(1-x)^{\beta-1}

with respect to the Lebesgue measure
dx
, where
\beta(\alpha,\beta)
is the beta function.

We write
\mathrm{Beta}^{-1}(\alpha,\beta)
for the distribution of
1/X
where
X\sim\mathrm{Beta}(\alpha,\beta)
.

We rely on the following classical result due to Lukacs
[
57
]
5
5

5

Note that the original version was stated for independence of
X+Y
with
Y/X
instead of
X/(X+Y)
, but these are equivalent since
X/(X+Y)=1/(1+Y/X)
.
.

Theorem 2.5
.

Let
X,Y
be independent, positive, and nonconstant random variables. Then the random variables
X+Y
and
X/(X+Y)
are independent if and only if there exist
x,y,s\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that

(2.17)

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\sim\Gamma(x,s)

(2.18)

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle\sim\Gamma(y,s).

In this case,

(2.19)

\displaystyle X+Y

\displaystyle\sim\Gamma(x+y,s)

(2.20)

\displaystyle\frac{X}{X+Y}

\displaystyle\sim\mathrm{Beta}(x,y).

Corollary 2.6
.

Let
A,B
be two independent, nonconstant random variables such that
B
is supported in
[0,1]
and
A
is positive. Then
BA
and
(1-B)A
are independent if and only if there exist
x,y,s\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}

(2.21)

\displaystyle\begin{split}A&\sim\Gamma(x+y,s)\\
B&\sim\mathrm{Beta}(x,y).\end{split}

In this case,

(2.22)

\displaystyle BA

\displaystyle\sim\Gamma(x,s)

(2.23)

\displaystyle(1-B)A

\displaystyle\sim\Gamma(y,s).

Proof.

(
\Rightarrow
) Let
X=BA,Y=(1-B)A
. Then these are both positive, nonconstant random variables, and the random variables
A=X+Y
and
B=X/(X+Y)
are independent. Hence the forward direction of
2.5
yields that there exist
x,y,s\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that

(2.24)

\displaystyle\begin{split}X&\sim\Gamma(x,s)\\
Y&\sim\Gamma(y,s).\end{split}

Deducing (
2.21
) is a standard computation.

(
\Leftarrow
) One has equality in joint distribution
(A,B)\sim(X+Y,X/(X+Y))
for
X,Y
independent and distributed as in (
2.24
), and it is a standard computation that

(2.25)

(X+Y,X/(X+Y))\sim\Gamma(x+y,s)\times\mathrm{Beta}(x,y).

∎

3.
Characterization of shuffle-independent full-plane weights

Let us recall and expand upon the motivation given in the Introduction for considering a full plane worth of weights. The recursion used in
2.4
gives the weights of a size
n
Aztec diamond in terms of those of a size
n+1
Aztec diamond. If considering random weights, it is natural to ask what families of independent random weights on the size
n
Aztec diamond have the property that the weights of each Aztec diamond of size
n-1,n-2,\ldots
, expressed in terms of them via

(3.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{i,j}^{[n-1]}&=\frac{a_{i,j}^{[n]}}{a_{i,j}^{[n]}+b_{i,j}^{[n]}}(a_{i+1,j}^{[n]}+b_{i+1,j}^{[n]})\\
b_{i,j}^{[n-1]}&=\frac{b_{i,j+1}^{[n]}}{a_{i,j+1}^{[n]}+b_{i,j+1}^{[n]}}(a_{i+1,j+1}^{[n]}+b_{i+1,j+1}^{[n]})\end{split}

(given previously in (
2.11
)) are also independent.

We will, however, answer a slightly different version of this question in order to characterize the Gamma weights treated here. It is natural, particularly from the perspective of sampling via the shuffling algorithm, to invert these recurrences (
3.1
) and define the weights of the size
n+1
Aztec diamond in terms of the one of size
n
. The formula (
3.1
) directly yields

(3.2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{i,j}^{[n+1]}&=\frac{a_{i,j}^{[n]}}{a_{i,j}^{[n]}+b_{i,j-1}^{[n]}}(a_{i-1,j}^{[n]}+b_{i-1,j-1}^{[n]})\\
b_{i,j}^{[n+1]}&=\frac{b_{i,j-1}^{[n]}}{a_{i,j}^{[n]}+b_{i,j-1}^{[n]}}(a_{i-1,j}^{[n]}+b_{i-1,j-1}^{[n]}).\end{split}

However, there is a problem: to get weights
a_{i,j}^{[n+1]}
or
b_{i,j}^{[n+1]}
when
i
or
j
is
1
, (
3.2
) expresses them in terms of weights
a_{0,j}^{[n]}
and
b_{i,0}^{[n]}
, which do not appear in the size
n
Aztec diamond. Similarly, the weights
a_{i,j}^{[n+2]}
or
b_{i,j}^{[n+2]}
with
i
or
j
equal to
1
will require weights
a_{-1,j}^{[n]}
and
b_{i,-1}^{[n]}
. Hence it is really most natural to consider a full plane worth of random weights

(3.3)

\{a_{i,j}^{[0]},b_{i,j}^{[0]}:i,j\in\mathbb{Z}\},

which ‘preprocesses’ all randomness needed to generate the random weights of the Aztec diamond of every size
n
. All weights
a_{i,j}^{[n]},b_{i,j}^{[n]}
for any
n\geq 1
may be expressed in terms of these through repeated application of the recurrences (
3.2
), and conversely each weight in (
3.3
) appears in the formula for
a_{i,j}^{[n]},b_{i,j}^{[n]}
for some
n
. One can also define weights with
n\leq 0
via (
3.1
). This full-plane perspective, which is also taken e.g. in
[
22
]
, is the one we take in this section.

Definition 3.1
.

We say that real parameter sequences
(\psi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}},(\phi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}},(s_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}},(\theta_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
are
admissible
if

(3.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}s_{i}&>0\\
\psi_{j}+\theta_{i}&>0\\
\phi_{j}-\theta_{i}&>0\\
\end{split}

for each
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
(\psi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}},(\phi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}},(s_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}},(\theta_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
be admissible parameter sequences in the sense of
3.1
, and
\{a_{i,j}^{[0]},b_{i,j}^{[0]}:i,j\in\mathbb{Z}\}
be a collection of mutually independent random variables with distribution

(3.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{i,j}^{[0]}&\sim\Gamma(\psi_{j}+\theta_{i},s_{i})\\
b_{i,j}^{[0]}&\sim\Gamma(\phi_{j}-\theta_{i},s_{i}).\end{split}

For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, define the random variables
\{a_{i,j}^{[n]},b_{i,j}^{[n]}:i,j\in\mathbb{Z}\}
recursively by the recurrences (
3.2
) for
n\geq 1
and (
3.1
) for
n\leq 1
. Then for each
n
, the random variables
\{a_{i,j}^{[n]},b_{i,j}^{[n]}:i,j\in\mathbb{Z}\}
are mutually independent, with distribution given by

(3.6)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{i,j}^{[n]}&\sim\Gamma(\psi_{j}+\theta_{i},s_{i-n})\\
b_{i,j}^{[n]}&\sim\Gamma(\phi_{j-n}-\theta_{i},s_{i-n}).\end{split}

Proof.

The proof proceeds by induction, using the recurrence relation (
3.2
). We prove the case of
n\geq 1
, and the case of
n\leq-1
is the same argument.

Assume that (
3.6
) holds for some fixed
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Consider the collection of variables
\{A_{i,j},B_{i,j}:(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\}
defined by

(3.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}A_{i,j}&=a_{i,j}^{[n]}+b_{i,j-1}^{[n]}\sim\Gamma(\psi_{j}+\phi_{j-n-1},s_{i-n})\\
B_{i,j}&=\frac{a_{i,j}^{[n]}}{a_{i,j}^{[n]}+b_{i,j-1}^{[n]}}\sim\mathrm{Beta}(\psi_{j}+\theta_{i},\phi_{j-n-1}-\theta_{i}).\end{split}

This collection is mutually independent, because the only pairs in this collection which depend on the same randomness are pairs
A_{i,j},B_{i,j}
for a given
i,j
, and these are independent by
2.5
.

Now, the recurrence (
3.2
) in terms of the
A_{i,j}
and
B_{i,j}
reads

(3.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{i,j}^{[n+1]}&=B_{i,j}A_{i-1,j}\sim\Gamma(\psi_{j}+\theta_{i},s_{i-n-1})\\
b_{i,j}^{[n+1]}&=(1-B_{i,j})A_{i-1,j}\sim\Gamma(\phi_{j-n-1}-\theta_{i},s_{i-n-1}),\end{split}

where the
\sim\Gamma(\cdots)
comes from the ‘in this case’ part of
2.6
. It remains to show that the variables
\{a_{i,j}^{[n+1]},b_{i,j}^{[n+1]}:(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\}
are mutually independent.

For each
i_{0},j_{0}
, by (
3.8
) the variables
a_{i_{0},j_{0}}^{[n+1]}
and
b_{i_{0},j_{0}}^{[n+1]}
are the only random variables in the collection
\{a_{i,j}^{[n+1]},b_{i,j}^{[n+1]}:(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\}
which depend on
B_{i_{0},j_{0}}
, and they are also the only random variables which depend on
A_{i_{0}-1,j}
. So, the collection of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-valued random variables
\{(a_{i,j}^{[n+1]},b_{i,j}^{[n+1]}):(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\}
is mutually independent. By
2.6
,
a_{i,j}^{[n+1]}
is independent of
b_{i,j}^{[n+1]}
, hence the collection
\{a_{i,j}^{[n+1]},b_{i,j}^{[n+1]}:(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\}
is mutually independent.

Hence, the claim holds for all
n\geq 0
. The case
n\leq 0
follows by a similar argument, using (
3.1
).
∎

Theorem 3.2
.

Let

(3.9)

\{a_{i,j}^{[0]},b_{i,j}^{[0]}:i,j\in\mathbb{Z}\}

be a collection of mutually independent, positive, nonconstant random variables, such that each of the collections of random variables

(3.10)

\{a_{i,j}^{[1]},b_{i,j}^{[1]}:i,j\in\mathbb{Z}\}

defined by (
3.2
) and

(3.11)

\{a_{i,j}^{[-1]},b_{i,j}^{[-1]}:i,j\in\mathbb{Z}\}

defined by (
3.1
) is also mutually independent. Then there exist admissible parameter sequences
(\psi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}},(\phi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}},(s_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}},(\theta_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
(in the sense of
3.1
) such that the variables
\{a_{i,j}^{[0]},b_{i,j}^{[0]}:i,j\in\mathbb{Z}\}
are distributed according to (
3.5
).

Proof of
3.2
.

We first show that the random variables
\{a_{i,j}^{[0]},b_{i,j}^{[0]}:i,j\in\mathbb{Z}\}
have Gamma distribution, then pin down the parameters.

If the random variables
\{a_{i,j}^{[1]},b_{i,j}^{[1]}:i,j\in\mathbb{Z}\}
are mutually independent, then in particular for each
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
we have that

(3.12)

a^{[1]}_{i,j}=\frac{a_{i,j}^{[0]}}{a_{i,j}^{[0]}+b_{i,j-1}^{[0]}}(a_{i-1,j}^{[0]}+b_{i-1,j-1}^{[0]})

is independent of

(3.13)

a_{i+1,j}^{[1]}=\frac{a_{i+1,j}^{[0]}}{a_{i+1,j}^{[0]}+b_{i+1,j-1}^{[0]}}(a_{i,j}^{[0]}+b_{i,j-1}^{[0]}).

Since all the variables with superscript
[0]
are independent, the above implies that the laws of
a_{i,j}^{[1]}
and
a_{i+1,j}^{[1]}
are conditionally independent after conditioning on
a_{i+1,j}^{[0]},b_{i+1,j-1}^{[0]},a_{i-1,j}^{[0]},b_{i-1,j-1}^{[0]}
, hence

(3.14)

\frac{a_{i,j}^{[0]}}{a_{i,j}^{[0]}+b_{i,j-1}^{[0]}}\quad\quad\quad\text{is independent of}\quad\quad\quad a_{i,j}^{[0]}+b_{i,j-1}^{[0]}.

By
2.5
, since
a_{i,j}^{[0]}
and
b_{i,j}^{[0]}
are assumed to be nonconstant, it follows that for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(3.15)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{i,j}^{[0]}&\sim\Gamma(\alpha_{i,j},s_{i,j})\\
b_{i,j-1}^{[0]}&\sim\Gamma(\beta_{i,j-1},s_{i,j})\end{split}

for some
\alpha_{i,j},\beta_{i,j-1},s_{i,j}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
; note that the scale parameters
s_{i,j}
are the same for both.

It remains to show that the parameters have the structure as stated in the theorem. That is, we need to show that the scale parameters
s_{i,j}
are independent of
j
,
\alpha_{i,j}+\beta_{i,j}
is independent of
i
and
\alpha_{i,j}-\beta_{i,j}
is independent of
j
.

We start with the statement on the scale parameter and begin by writing

a^{[-1]}_{i,j}=BA,\quad\text{and}\quad b^{[-1]}_{i,j-1}=(1-B)A,

where

(3.16)

A=a^{[0]}_{i+1,j}+b^{[0]}_{i+1,j},

and

(3.17)

B=\frac{a^{[0]}_{i,j}}{a^{[0]}_{i,j}+b^{[0]}_{i,j}}.

Since the variables
a^{[0]}_{i,j},b^{[0]}_{i,j}
for
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
are mutually independent, we see that
A
and
B
are independent. By assumption, we also have that
a^{[-1]}_{i,j}
and
b^{[-1]}_{i,j}
are independent, and thus by using
2.6
, we find that there exist parameters
x,y,s
such that

(3.18)

A\sim\Gamma(x+y,s),\quad B\sim\mathrm{Beta}(x,y)

which by
2.5
gives
a^{[0]}_{i,j}\sim\Gamma(x,s)
and
b_{i,j}^{[0]}\sim\Gamma(y,s)
, and thus they must have the same scale parameters. By (
3.15
) this implies that
s_{i,j}=s_{i,j+1}
. Since this holds for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
we thus indeed find that
s_{i,j}=s_{i}
does not depend on
j
.

This argument also shows that
\alpha_{i,j}+\beta_{i,j}
does not depend on
i
. Indeed, by (
3.17
) and (
3.15
), we find that
x,y
in (
3.18
) satisfies
x+y=\alpha_{i,j}+\beta_{i,j}
. However, from (
3.18
) and (
3.16
) we also find
x+y=\alpha_{i+1,j}+\beta_{i+1,j}
, and thus

(3.19)

\alpha_{i+1,j}+\beta_{i+1,j}=\alpha_{i,j}+\beta_{i,j},

for
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
,
which implies that
\alpha_{i,j}+\beta_{i,j}
does not depend on
i
, but only on
j
.

Similarly, from (
3.15
),

(3.20)

\frac{a_{i,j}^{[0]}}{a_{i,j}^{[0]}+b_{i,j-1}^{[0]}}\sim\mathrm{Beta}(\alpha_{i,j},\beta_{i,j-1})

and

(3.21)

a_{i,j}^{[0]}+b_{i,j-1}^{[0]}\sim\Gamma(\alpha_{i,j}+\beta_{i,j-1},s_{i,j}).

So since

(3.22)

\hat{A}:=a_{i-1,j}^{[0]}+b_{i-1,j-1}^{[0]}\sim\Gamma(\alpha_{i-1,j}+\beta_{i-1,j-1},s_{i-1,j-1})

and

(3.23)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{B}:=\frac{a_{i,j}^{[0]}}{a_{i,j}^{[0]}+b_{i,j-1}^{[0]}}\sim\mathrm{Beta}(\alpha_{i,j},\beta_{i,j-1})\end{split}

are independent, and also
a_{i,j}^{[1]}=\hat{B}\hat{A}
is independent of
b_{i,j}^{[1]}=(1-\hat{B})\hat{A}
,
2.6
tells us that

(3.24)

\alpha_{i,j}+\beta_{i,j-1}=\alpha_{i-1,j}+\beta_{i-1,j-1}

(indeed, the sum of the Beta variable parameters
x,y
is equal to the Gamma variable’s shape parameter
x+y
in that result). By starting from a reordering of (
3.24
) and then inserting (
3.19
) (with
i
replaced by
i-1
), we find, for
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\beta_{i,j-1}-\beta_{i-1,j-1}=\alpha_{i-1,j}-\alpha_{i,j}=\beta_{i,j}-\beta_{i-1,j}.

Hence

(3.25)

\alpha_{i-1,j}-\alpha_{i,j}=\beta_{i,j-1}-\beta_{i-1,j-1}=:\kappa_{i}

is independent of
j
. We may choose (non-uniquely, but unique up to an overall constant shift) parameters
\theta_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

(3.26)

\kappa_{i}=\theta_{i-1}-\theta_{i}.

With this notation, (
3.25
) implies

(3.27)

\alpha_{i,j}-\theta_{i}=\alpha_{i-1,j}-\theta_{i-1},

hence the right hand side of (
3.27
) is independent of
i
and we may define

(3.28)

\psi_{j}:=\alpha_{i,j}-\theta_{i}.

Similarly,
\beta_{i,j}+\theta_{i}
is independent of
i
and hence we may define

(3.29)

\phi_{j}:=\beta_{i,j}+\theta_{i}.

With this notation,

(3.30)

\displaystyle\alpha_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\psi_{j}+\theta_{i}

(3.31)

\displaystyle\beta_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\phi_{j}-\theta_{i}.

Finally, note that (
3.15
) in our new parameters gives

(3.32)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{i,j}^{[0]}&\sim\Gamma(\psi_{j}+\theta_{i},s_{i})\\
b_{i,j}^{[0]}&\sim\Gamma(\phi_{j}-\theta_{i},s_{i}),\end{split}

and this proves the statement.∎

Corollary 3.3
.

Let

(3.33)

\{a_{i,j}^{[0]},b_{i,j}^{[0]}:i,j\in\mathbb{Z}\}

be a collection of mutually independent nonconstant random variables, such that each of the collections of random variables

(3.34)

\{a_{i,j}^{[1]},b_{i,j}^{[1]}:i,j\in\mathbb{Z}\}

defined by (
3.2
) and

(3.35)

\{a_{i,j}^{[-1]},b_{i,j}^{[-1]}:i,j\in\mathbb{Z}\}

defined by (
3.1
) is also mutually independent. Then for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the random variables
\{a_{i,j}^{[n]},b_{i,j}^{[n]}:i,j\in\mathbb{Z}\}
are mutually independent. Furthermore, there is an admissible (in the sense of
3.1
) parameter collection
(\psi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}},(\phi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}},(s_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}},(\theta_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
such that for every
i,j,n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the weights have distribution as in (
3.6
).

Proof.

Follows directly from
3.2
and
3.1
.
∎

Remark 3.1
.

Rather than passing to a full
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
worth of weights, one might ask if simply requiring independence of the weights
\{a_{i,j}^{[n]},b_{i,j}^{[n]}:0\leq i,j\leq n-1\}
and
\{a_{i,j}^{[n-1]},b_{i,j}^{[n-1]}:0\leq i,j\leq n-2\}
at two consecutive steps of the shuffling algorithm for an actual finite-size Aztec diamond is enough to conclude that all weights are
\Gamma
variables. This fails: for instance, if one chooses any independent weights for an Aztec diamond of size two, then the corresponding weights

(3.36)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{0,0}^{[1]}&=\frac{a_{0,0}^{[2]}}{a_{0,0}^{[2]}+b_{0,0}^{[2]}}(a_{1,0}^{[2]}+b_{1,0}^{[2]})\\
b_{0,0}^{[1]}&=\frac{b_{0,1}^{[2]}}{a_{0,1}^{[2]}+b_{0,1}^{[2]}}(a_{1,1}^{[2]}+b_{1,1}^{[2]})\end{split}

are independent simply because they depend on different variables. In general, one can show that the subset of weights
a_{i,j}^{[n]},b_{i,j}^{[n]}
for
0\leq i\leq n-2
and
1\leq j\leq n-2
are
\Gamma
-distributed if they are independent and the corresponding weights of the size
n-1
Aztec diamond are independent, but cannot conclude this for all weights.

Remark 3.2
.

Similarly, since
3.3
requires independence of weights at
3
steps of the shuffling algorithm, one might ask if only two steps suffice. While this is enough to obtain that the weights are Gamma variables distributed by (
3.18
), if the parameters
s_{i,j}
there actually depend nontrivially on
j
then the weights will become dependent for
n\geq 2
.

As mentioned in the Introduction,
3.3
makes it natural to define the multi-parameter Gamma weights of
1.7
on the Aztec diamond of size
n
. Before ending this section, we prove the key independence of factors in the partition function for these weights, which will be used to prove asymptotic results on free energy in
Section

9
. It also comes as a corollary of some of the more involved graph manipulations in
Section

4
, and we give a second proof there, but for the sake of exposition we give a direct proof here as well.

Corollary 3.4
.

The partition function of an Aztec diamond of size
n
, with weights
a_{i,j}^{[n]},b_{i,j}^{[n]},1\leq i,j\leq n
as in
Figure

11
is

(3.37)

Z_{G_{n}^{\Az}}=\prod_{k=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{k}(a_{1,j}^{[k]}+b_{1,j}^{[k]})

Furthermore, if the weights
a_{i,j}^{[n]},b_{i,j}^{[n]},1\leq i,j\leq n
are Gamma variables with parameters as in
1.7
, then the random variables
a_{1,j}^{[k]}+b_{1,j}^{[k]}
appearing in (
3.37
) are mutually independent, with distributions

(3.38)

a_{1,j}^{[k]}+b_{1,j}^{[k]}\sim\Gamma(\psi_{j}+\phi_{j-k},1).

Proof.

The explicit form (
3.37
) follows immediately from
2.4
, and the marginal distributions (
3.38
) likewise follow from
3.1
, so it suffices to show they are mutually independent. By
3.1
, for each fixed
k
the collection of variables
\{a_{1,j}^{[k]}+b_{1,j}^{[k]}:1\leq j\leq k\}
are mutually independent, but
a priori
one would expect that there exist dependencies between them for different
k
. To show that they do not, we will prove something stronger:

Claim:
For each
k
, the weights
\{a_{i,j}^{[k]},b_{i,j}^{[k]}:1\leq i,j\leq k\}
are independent of
\{a_{1,j}^{[k+1]}+b_{1,j}^{[k+1]}:1\leq j\leq k+1\}
.

By (
3.1
), the only weights in the collection
\{a_{i,j}^{[k]},b_{i,j}^{[k]}:1\leq i,j\leq k\}
which might depend on weights
a_{1,j}^{[k+1]}
or
b_{1,j}^{[k+1]}
at all are those with
i=1
, since all others are expressed in terms of
a_{i,j}^{[k+1]}
s and
b_{i,j}^{[k+1]}
s with
i>1
. However,

(3.39)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{1,j}^{[k-1]}&=\frac{a_{1,j}^{[k]}}{a_{1,j}^{[k]}+b_{1,j}^{[k]}}(a_{1+1,j}^{[k]}+b_{1+1,j}^{[k]})\\
b_{1,j}^{[k-1]}&=\frac{b_{1,j+1}^{[k]}}{a_{1,j+1}^{[k]}+b_{1,j+1}^{[k]}}(a_{1+1,j+1}^{[k]}+b_{1+1,j+1}^{[k]}),\end{split}

and
\frac{a_{1,j}^{[k]}}{a_{1,j}^{[k]}+b_{1,j}^{[k]}}
is independent of
a_{1,j}^{[k]}+b_{1,j}^{[k]}
by
2.5
. This proves the claim.

Because all weights
\{a_{1,j}^{[k]}+b_{1,j}^{[k]}:1\leq j\leq k,1\leq k\leq n-1\}
can be expressed in terms of the weights
\{a_{i,j}^{[n-1]},b_{i,j}^{[n-1]}:1\leq i,j\leq n-1\}
, our claim (with
k=n-1
) immediately yields that all variables
\{(a_{1,j}^{[k]}+b_{1,j}^{[k]}):1\leq k\leq n-1,1\leq j\leq k\}
are independent of
\{(a_{1,j}^{[n]}+b_{1,j}^{[n]}):1\leq j\leq n\}
. Similarly, all variables
\{(a_{1,j}^{[k]}+b_{1,j}^{[k]}):1\leq k\leq n-2,1\leq j\leq k\}
are independent of
\{(a_{1,j}^{[n-1]}+b_{1,j}^{[n-1]}):1\leq j\leq n\}
, and so on; this completes the proof.
∎

4.
The
\beta
-
\Gamma
polymer and matching along a vertical slice

Having singled out the Gamma-disordered Aztec diamond as special from the perspective of shuffling in the previous section, we begin investigating its other properties. The main result of this section is
4.10
, which is a general version of
1.5
for the weights of
1.7
which arose in the previous section. Because the parameters
s_{i}
do not affect the dimer measure, we will set them to
1
throughout this section and the rest of the paper except for
Section

9
and
Appendix

B
.

The structure of the section is as follows. In
Section

4.1
, we show a distributional equality (
4.7
) between the dimer measure on
G_{n}^{\Az}
on the
\ell^{th}
vertical slice and the dimer measure on a related graph
\bar{G}_{n,\ell}^{v-swap}
, obtained from the former by a sequence of local moves. In
Section

4.2
we relate dimer covers on the latter graph to the
\beta
-
\Gamma
polymer (
Lemma

4.9
).

One feature of these results is that we obtain not just a distributional equality between matchings of the Aztec diamond and an independent
\beta
-
\Gamma
polymer paths, but also an explicit mapping from the weights of the Aztec diamond to the weights in the
\beta
-
\Gamma
polymer.

4.1.
Distributional equalities between the Aztec diamond and related graphs

The local urban renewal move (
2.2
), together with the more trivial vertex-expansion move (
2.3
), gives distributional equalities between dimer models on related graphs. The goal of this subsection is to prove
4.7
, which gives such a distributional equality between the Aztec diamond and a dimer model on a certain graph
\bar{G}^{v-swap}_{n,\ell}
which is transparently related to the
\beta
-
\Gamma
polymer (as we will see next). Along the way, by keeping track of the independence properties of random weights under these transformations, we also give another proof of the independence statement in
3.4
from earlier.

We first alter the Aztec diamond slightly, defining a related graph
G_{n}^{vert}
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Denote by
G_{n}^{vert}
the graph as in
Figure

15
, with weights related to those on the original Aztec graph
G_{n}^{\Az}
(recall from
Figure

11
) as shown there. Then there is a weight-preserving bijection between perfect matchings of the two graphs, which furthermore does not change the edges surrounding the square faces
6
6

6

The correspondence between the square faces on
G_{n}^{\Az}
and
G_{n}^{vert}
is clear.
.

\gamma_{11}^{[3]}
\gamma_{12}^{[3]}
\gamma_{13}^{[3]}
\bar{\beta}_{11}^{[3]}
\bar{\beta}_{12}^{[3]}
\bar{\beta}_{13}^{[3]}
\beta_{11}^{[3]}
\beta_{12}^{[3]}
\beta_{13}^{[3]}
\gamma_{21}^{[3]}
\gamma_{22}^{[3]}
\gamma_{23}^{[3]}
\bar{\beta}_{21}^{[3]}
\bar{\beta}_{22}^{[3]}
\bar{\beta}_{23}^{[3]}
\beta_{21}^{[3]}
\beta_{22}^{[3]}
\beta_{23}^{[3]}
\gamma_{31}^{[3]}
\gamma_{32}^{[3]}
\gamma_{33}^{[3]}
\bar{\beta}_{31}^{[3]}
\bar{\beta}_{32}^{[3]}
\bar{\beta}_{33}^{[3]}
\beta_{31}^{[3]}
\beta_{32}^{[3]}
\beta_{33}^{[3]}
\gamma_{41}^{[3]}
\gamma_{42}^{[3]}
\gamma_{43}^{[3]}

Figure 15
.
The graph
G_{n}^{vert}
for
n=3
. Here we use compressed notation
\gamma_{i,j}^{[n]}=a_{i,j}^{[n]}+b_{i,j}^{[n]},\beta_{i,j}^{[n]}=\frac{a_{i,j}^{[n]}}{a_{i,j}^{[n]}+b_{i,j}^{[n]}}
and
\bar{\beta}_{ij}^{[n]}=1-\beta_{ij}^{[n]}.

Proof.

First apply
2.3
to expand each black vertex into a pair of black vertices connected by a white one. At the rightmost black vertex in each such pair, gauge transform by
1/(a_{i,j}^{[n]}+b_{i,j}^{[n]})
(with
i,j
as appropriate), and then gauge transform by
a_{i,j}^{[n]}+b_{i,j}^{[n]}
at the white vertex immediately to its left
7
7

7

Note that for the rightmost vertices, this gauge transform adds new weights
a_{n+1,j}^{[n]}
which were not present in the original Aztec diamond. However, every matching of the resulting graph does not include the edges with these weights, so the partition function and dimer measure are still independent of them—we simply add them as a notational convenience to make this and later constructions more uniform and clear.
. After a cosmetic vertical shear, the resulting graph is as in
Figure

15
.

The vertex-expansion moves do not change the partition function, by
2.3
, and furthermore by the same result there is a bijection between dimer covers of
G_{n}^{\Az}
and
G_{n}^{vert}
which fixes the edges around the square faces. While gauge transforms do change the partition function, the
1/(a_{i,j}^{[n]}+b_{i,j}^{[n]})
and
a_{i,j}^{[n]}+b_{i,j}^{[n]}
factors cancel.
∎

Let us now treat the graph
G_{n}^{vert}
a bit more formally; for the rest of this section, all graphs will be drawn such that the vertex sets are subsets of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and all edges are either horizontal edges from
(x,y)
to
(x+1,y)
or up-right edges from
(x,y)
to
(x+1,y+1)
. Looking at
G_{n}^{vert}
, one can note that it is composed naturally of columns as in the following definition.

Definition 4.1
.

Given a bipartite graph
G
drawn on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
as above, we refer to a subgraph as in
Figure

16
(left) as a
(+)
-column
. In other words, it has white vertices at
(x,y-1),(x,y-2),\ldots,(x,y-m)
, connected horizontally to black vertices at
(x+1,y-1),(x+1,y-2),\ldots,(x+1,y-m)
, then each of these connected to the white vertices at
(x+2,y),(x+2,y-1),\ldots,(x+2,y-m)
by both horizontal and up-right edges. If the weights on adjacent pairs of right and up-right edges are given by
\beta_{i}
and
1-\beta_{i}
as in the figure, we will sometimes refer to it as a
(+)
-column with weights
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{m}
when emphasizing the weights. We allow the degenerate case
m=0
, for which the
(+)
-column is just a single white vertex at
(x+2,y)
with no black vertices.

We refer to a subgraph as in
Figure

16
(right), i.e. white vertices at
(x,y),(x,y-1),\ldots,(x,y-m)
connected horizontally and up-right to black vertices at
(x+1,y),(x+1,y-1),\ldots,(x+1,y-m+1)
, then these connected horizontally to white vertices at
(x+2,y),(x+2,y-1),\ldots,(x+2,y-m+1)
, as a
(-)
-column
. If the weights on the black-to-white edges are
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{m}
as shown, we will sometimes refer to it as a
(-)
-column with weights
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{m}
to emphasize the weights. We allow the degenerate case
m=0
, for which the
(-)
-column is just a single white vertex at
(x,y)
.

\bar{\beta}_{m-1}
\beta_{m-1}
\bar{\beta}_{m-2}
\beta_{m-2}
\bar{\beta}_{m}
\beta_{m}
\bar{\beta}_{1}
\beta_{1}
\bar{\beta}_{2}
\beta_{2}

Definition 4.2
.

Denote by
G_{n}^{vert}
the weighted graph with vertex set contained in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
defined as follows. It has black vertices at
(0,0),(0,-1),\ldots,(0,-n+1)
which are connected to white vertices at
(1,0),(1,-1),\ldots,(1,-n+1)
by edges with weights
a_{1,1}^{[n]}+b_{1,1}^{[n]},\ldots,a_{1,n}^{[n]}+b_{1,n}^{[n]}
(top to bottom). These white vertices then form the leftmost white vertices in a
(+)
-column with weights

\frac{a_{1,1}^{[n]}}{a_{1,1}^{[n]}+b_{1,1}^{[n]}},\ldots,\frac{a_{1,n}^{[n]}}{a_{1,n}^{[n]}+b_{1,n}^{[n]}}.

The rightmost white vertices of this
(+)
-column are the leftmost white vertices of a
(-)
-column with weights
a_{2,1}^{[n]}+b_{2,1}^{[n]},\ldots,a_{2,n}^{[n]}+b_{2,n}^{[n]}
. The alternating
(+)
- and
(-)
-columns with weights

\frac{a_{i,1}^{[n]}}{a_{i,1}^{[n]}+b_{i,1}^{[n]}},\ldots,\frac{a_{i,n}^{[n]}}{a_{i,n}^{[n]}+b_{i,n}^{[n]}}

for the
(+)
-column and
a_{i+1,1}^{[n]}+b_{i+1,1}^{[n]},\ldots,a_{i+1,n}^{[n]}+b_{i+1,n}^{[n]}
repeat until
i=n
, at which point there are horizontal edges connecting the rightmost white vertices to
n
black vertices at coordinates
(4n+2,1),\ldots,(4n+2,n)
.

The reason why
4.1
is a useful definition is the following lemma. This is also why we have added the pendant edges on the right and left of
G_{n}^{vert}
, which might have seemed strange above, as they allow us to write the graph as an alternating sequence of
(+)
- and
(-)
-columns sandwiched between two ‘trivial’ strips of black and white vertices connected by horizontal edges.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
m\geq 1
and
G
be a weighted bipartite graph containing a local configuration with a
(+)
and then a
(-)
-column with weights
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{m}
and
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{m}
respectively as in the left hand side of
Figure

17
. Let
G^{\prime}
be the graph where the local configuration is replaced by the one on the right hand side with a
(-)
and then a
(+)
-column with weights
\hat{\gamma}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\gamma}_{m-1}
and
\hat{\beta}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\beta}_{m-1}
respectively
8
8

8

In the case
m=1
,
G^{\prime}
will have a degenerate
(-)
-column and a degenerate
(+)
-column, so no updated weights are necessary.
, related to the initial weights via

(4.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\gamma}_{j}&:=\beta_{j}\gamma_{j}+(1-\beta_{j+1})\gamma_{j+1}\\
\hat{\beta}_{j}&:=\frac{\beta_{j}\gamma_{j}}{\beta_{j}\gamma_{j}+(1-\beta_{j+1})\gamma_{j+1}}.\end{split}

Then the marginal distribution (with respect to the dimer measure) of edges outside this local configuration is the same for both
G
and
G^{\prime}
. Additionally, both graphs have the same partition function.

Figure 17
.
The portion of
G
(left) and
G^{\prime}
(right) which is changed under
Lemma

4.2
. Here we use shorthand
\bar{\beta}_{j}=1-\beta_{j}
to declutter notation, and edges without weights pictured have weight
1
.

Proof.

First apply urban renewal (
2.2
) inside each square. The top and bottom of the graph now have
1
-valent white vertices, so remove these together with the black vertex they are incident to. Now gauge transform by
\gamma_{j}^{-1}
at the black vertex with the
\gamma_{j}
edge incident, and then gauge transform by
\gamma_{j}
at the white vertex to its left, for each
j
. Finally, contract all
2
-valent vertices with both incident edges having weight
1
. The result is the graph on the right hand side of
Figure

17
. Furthermore, the partition function is unchanged, since each urban renewal move changed it by
\beta_{j}\cdot 1+(1-\beta_{j})\cdot 1=1
and the contribution of each gauge transform by
\gamma_{j}
was canceled by the gauge transform by
\gamma_{j}^{-1}
.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

In the setup of
Lemma

4.2
, suppose the weights
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{m}
and
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{m}
are mutually independent random variables distributed as

(4.2)

\displaystyle\gamma_{j}

\displaystyle\sim\Gamma(x_{j}+y_{j},1)

(4.3)

\displaystyle\beta_{j}

\displaystyle\sim\mathrm{Beta}(x_{j},y_{j})

for some positive real parameters
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}
. Then the weights
\hat{\gamma}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\gamma}_{m-1}
and
\hat{\beta}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\beta}_{m-1}
are mutually independent with distributions

(4.4)

\displaystyle\hat{\gamma}_{j}

\displaystyle\sim\Gamma(x_{j}+y_{j+1},1)

(4.5)

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{j}

\displaystyle\sim\mathrm{Beta}(x_{j},y_{j+1})

Proof.

First, by
2.6
the collection of random variables
\{\beta_{j}\gamma_{j},(1-\beta_{j})\gamma_{j}:1\leq j\leq m\}
are mutually independent with distributions

(4.6)

\displaystyle\beta_{j}\gamma_{j}

\displaystyle\sim\Gamma(x_{j},1)

(4.7)

\displaystyle(1-\beta_{j})\gamma_{j}

\displaystyle\sim\Gamma(y_{j},1).

It follows by
2.5
that the random variables

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\gamma}_{j}&:=\beta_{j}\gamma_{j}+(1-\beta_{j+1})\gamma_{j+1}\\
\hat{\beta}_{j}&:=\frac{\beta_{j}\gamma_{j}}{\beta_{j}\gamma_{j}+(1-\beta_{j+1})\gamma_{j+1}}\end{split}

for
j=1,\ldots,m-1
are mutually independent with distributions as in the statement.
∎

The column-swap move of
Lemma

4.2
is very similar to zipper arguments coming from the Yang-Baxter equation for solvable lattice models. Using it, we will be able to transform
G_{n}^{vert}
into graphs as in
Figure

19
, which we define more formally below (though it may be easier to just look at
Figure

19
, at least at a first pass). The construction may remind the reader familiar with
[
22
]
with matrix commutations in that paper, and we will present an alternative construction in the spirit of
[
22
]
in
Appendix

A
.

For such column-swap arguments, we find it helpful to schematically visualize sequences of
(+)
- and
(-)
-columns by ‘staircase’ diagrams, where each down step corresponds to a
(-)
-column and each right step corresponds to a
(+)
-column. The Aztec diamond of size
n
, or rather the dilated graph
G_{n}^{vert}
of
4.2
, corresponds to a staircase of
n
alternating horizontal and vertical steps, see
Figure

18
(left).

Figure 18
.
The diagrams of
G_{7}^{vert}
(purple) and
G^{v-swap}_{7,3}
(orange). The latter graph is pictured later in
Figure

19
.

The same diagrams are often used for Schur processes, which indeed are a deterministic limit of our dimer model. The commutation of a
(+)
- and
(-)
-column then corresponds to one of the moves

on this diagram, i.e. adding or subtracting a square. Note that this diagram does not keep track of either weights or matchings. We will use these commutations to turn
G_{n}^{vert}
into another graph, which we now define and which is schematically represented in
Figure

18
(orange).

Definition 4.3
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
\ell\in[1,n+1]
be integers. Let
G^{v-swap}_{n,\ell}
be the weighted bipartite graph drawn on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
as follows. It has black vertices at
(0,-1),(0,-2),\ldots,(0,-n)
which are connected to white vertices at
(1,-1),(1,-2),\ldots,(1,-n)
by edges with weights
a_{1,1}^{[n]}+b_{1,1}^{[n]},\ldots,a_{1,n}^{[n]}+b_{1,n}^{[n]}
(recall from
4.1
that we always enumerate weights top to bottom). These white vertices then form the leftmost white vertices of a series of
\ell-1

(-)
-columns where the
i^{th}

(-)
-column (starting from
i=1
at the left) has weights
a_{1,1}^{[n-i]}+b_{1,1}^{[n-i]},\ldots,a_{1,n-i}^{[n-i]}+b_{1,n-i}^{[n-i]}
. The rightmost white vertices of the
(\ell-1)^{th}

(-)
-column, which are located at coordinates
(2\ell-1,-1),\ldots,(2\ell-1,-n+\ell-1)
, form the leftmost white vertices of a series of
\ell

(+)
-columns where the
i^{th}
column (starting from
i=1
at the left) has weights

\frac{a^{[n-\ell+i]}_{i,1}}{a^{[n-\ell+i]}_{i,1}+b^{[n-\ell+i]}_{i,1}},\ldots,\frac{a^{[n-\ell+i]}_{i,n-\ell+i}}{a^{[n-\ell+i]}_{i,n-\ell+i}+b^{[n-\ell+i]}_{i,n-\ell+i}}.

The rightmost white vertices of the
\ell^{th}

(+)
-column in this series, which are at
(4\ell-1,\ell-1),\ldots,(4\ell-1,-n+\ell-1)
, form the leftmost white vertices of a series of
n-\ell+1

(-)
-columns, where the
i^{th}
column (starting from
i=1
at the left) has weights
a^{[n-i+1]}_{\ell+1,1}+b^{[n-i+1]}_{\ell+1,1},\ldots,a^{[n-i+1]}_{\ell+1,n-i+1}+b^{[n-i+1]}_{\ell+1,n-i+1}
. The rightmost white vertices of the
(n-\ell+1)^{th}

(-)
-column, which are at
(2n+2\ell+1,\ell-1),\ldots,(2n+2\ell+1,0)
, form the leftmost white vertices of a series of
n-\ell

(+)
-columns, where the
i^{th}
column (starting from
i=1
at the left) has weights

\frac{a^{[\ell+i-1]}_{\ell+i,1}}{a^{[\ell+i-1]}_{\ell+i,1}+b^{[\ell+i-1]}_{\ell+i,1}},\ldots,\frac{a^{[\ell+i-1]}_{\ell+i,\ell+i-1}}{a^{[\ell+i-1]}_{\ell+i,\ell+i-1}+b^{[\ell+i-1]}_{\ell+i,\ell+i-1}}.

Finally, the rightmost white vertices of the last such
(+)
-column are connected by horizontal edges of weight
1
to
n
black vertices, which are located at
(4n+2,n-1),\ldots,(4n+2,0)
.

An example is given in
Figure

19
. In the case
\ell=n+1
the graph
G^{v-swap}_{n,n+1}
has two connected components, as pictured in
Figure

20
.

Figure 19
.
The graph
G^{v-swap}_{7,3}
. It was obtained from an Aztec diamond
G_{7}^{\Az}
by the graph moves described above, which did not alter the
3^{rd}
column of the original Aztec diamond (highlighted in green).

Figure 20
.
The graph
G^{v-swap}_{2,3}
, which has two connected components, shown with its unique dimer cover. In the language of
4.1
it is composed of two
(-)
-columns followed by two
(+)
-columns, where the second
(-)
-column and first
(+)
-column are degenerate and consist of a single white vertex.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
\ell\in[1,n+1]
be integers, and let
G_{n}^{vert}
be as in
4.2
and
G_{n,\ell}^{v-swap}
be as in
4.3
, with weights given in terms of the ones in
G_{n}^{vert}
via the recurrence (
3.1
). Then

(1)

There is equality of partition functions
Z_{G_{n}^{vert}}=Z_{G_{n,\ell}^{v-swap}}
.

(2)

If furthermore
\ell\leq n
, then there is also an equality of marginal distributions of edges in (a) the
\ell^{th}
column from the left of
n
squares in
G_{n}^{vert}
, and (b) the only column of
n
squares in
G_{n,\ell}^{v-swap}
.

Proof.

To the left of the
\ell^{th}
column from the left of
n
squares in
G_{n}^{vert}
, there are
\ell-1

(-)
-columns and
\ell

(+)
-columns in alternating order, including the one which intersects this column of squares. Use
Lemma

4.2
to swap the order of these to match the order
-,\ldots,-,+,\ldots,+
; note that the rightmost
(+)
-column does not have to be swapped with anything. Now, to the right of our column of squares there are
n-\ell+1

(-)
-columns (including the one which intersects this column of squares) and
n-\ell

(+)
-columns in alternating order. Again use
Lemma

4.2
to swap the order
-,\ldots,-,+,\ldots,+
, and similarly note that the leftmost
(-)
-column did not have to get swapped with anything. By
Lemma

4.2
, these swaps did not change the partition function, and since neither the
\ell^{th}

(+)
-column nor the
\ell^{th}

(-)
-column were swapped, the marginal distribution of edges in our desired column of squares did not change. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.1
.

The upshot of the proof of
4.4
is that one can take the modified Aztec graph
G_{n}^{vert}
and swap
(+)
- and
(-)
-columns via
Lemma

4.2
to obtain related graphs with explicit relations between certain marginals of the dimer measures for the two graphs. We only showed this in the case where we modify
G_{n}^{\Az}
to a graph
G_{n,\ell}^{v-swap}
with columns of the form
-,\ldots,-,+,\ldots,+,-,\ldots,-,+,\ldots,+
. However, at the cost of slightly more notation and keeping track of indices, one can get any arbitrary sequence of
(+)
- and
(-)
-columns by the same argument as the proof of
4.4
. These would correspond to different mixed polymers (see remainder of this section) where the columns of Beta and strict-weak environments interleave in an arbitrary way.

In fact, dimer covers of
G_{n,\ell}^{v-swap}
are deterministically ‘frozen’ on the edges. These frozen portions change the partition function, but have no effect on the dimer measure in the middle portion.

Lemma 4.5
.

Any dimer cover
M
of the graph
G_{n,\ell}^{v-swap}
of
4.3
has the following properties:

(1)

For each of the first block of
\ell-1

(-)
-columns, every horizontal edge with left vertex black and right vertex white is contained in
M
.

(2)

For each of the second block of
n-\ell

(+)
-columns, every horizontal edge with left vertex white and right vertex black is contained in
M
.

Proof.

The leftmost black vertices are
1
-valent and hence in any dimer cover they must be matched to the white vertices to their right. After removing these vertices and all other edges connected to them, we again have
1
-valent black vertices which must be matched to the white vertices to their right, and so on. This proves the first claim, and the second follows by the same argument proceeding from the right of
G_{n,\ell}^{v-swap}
.
∎

In particular, this lets us give another proof of independence of factors in the partition function of the Aztec diamond.

Another proof of
3.4
.

By the first part of
4.4
together with
Lemma

4.5
,
Z_{G_{n}^{\Az}}
is equal to the weight of the single legal matching of
G^{v-swap}_{n,n+1}
, and from the explicit description of weights in
4.3
this is given by the right hand side of (
3.37
).

For the mutual independence, note that in the proof of
4.4
we obtained
G^{v-swap}_{n,n+1}
from
G_{n}^{vert}
by a sequence of swaps of
(+)
- and
(-)
-columns. By
Lemma

4.3
, it follows that the weights in
G^{v-swap}_{n,n+1}
are mutually independent. The marginal laws in (
3.38
) come
3.1
and the fact that a sum of two Gamma variables is another Gamma variable (see
2.5
).
∎

Corollary 4.6
.

For any
n\geq\ell\geq 1
, there is a bijection between matchings of
G_{n,\ell}^{v-swap}
and
\bar{G}^{v-swap}_{n,\ell}
which preserves weights up to an overall factor of

(4.8)

\prod_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\prod_{j=1}^{n-i}(a_{1,j}^{[n-i]}+b_{1,j}^{[n-i]}).

Consequently,

(1)

The dimer measure on the subgraph of
G_{n,\ell}^{v-swap}
corresponding to
\bar{G}^{v-swap}_{n,\ell}
is the same as the dimer measure on
\bar{G}^{v-swap}_{n,\ell}
.

(2)

The partition function of
\bar{G}^{v-swap}_{n,\ell}
is

(4.9)

Z_{\bar{G}^{v-swap}_{n,\ell}}=\prod_{k=1}^{n-\ell}\prod_{j=1}^{k}(a_{1,j}^{[k]}+b_{1,j}^{[k]})=Z_{G_{n-\ell}^{\Az}}.

Proof.

The first sentence is a direct consequence of
Lemma

4.5
. The equality of dimer measures in (
1
) follows directly from this. For the partition function, we use the same fact together with the explicit partition function of
G_{n}^{\Az}
from
3.4
, which is the same as that of
G_{n}^{vert}
by
Lemma

4.1
and the same as that of
G_{n,\ell}^{v-swap}
by
4.4
.
∎

Theorem 4.7
.

Let
n\geq\ell\geq 1
be integers, and let
G_{n}^{\Az}
be the weighted Aztec graph as in
Figure

11
and
\bar{G}^{v-swap}_{n,\ell}
be as in
4.4
, with weights given in terms of the ones in
G_{n}^{\Az}
via the recurrence (
3.1
). Then there is an equality of marginal distributions of edges in (a) the
\ell^{th}
column from the left of
n
squares in
G_{n}^{\Az}
, and (b) the only column of
n
squares in
\bar{G}^{v-swap}_{n,\ell}
.

Proof.

Combining
Lemma

4.1
,
4.4
Part (
2
), and
4.6
Part (
1
), we obtain distributional equalities along the
\ell^{th}
vertical slice from
G_{n}^{\Az}
to
G_{n}^{vert}
, then to
G_{n,\ell}^{v-swap}
, then to
\bar{G}^{v-swap}_{n,\ell}
, respectively.
∎

4.2.
Distributional equality between
\bar{G}^{v-swap}_{n,\ell}
and the
\beta
-
\Gamma
polymer

The following is the general version of
1.5
earlier.

Definition 4.5
.

Let
p,m\geq 1
be integers, and let
(\tau_{i})_{1\leq i\leq m},(\eta_{j})_{1\leq j\leq m+p},(\xi_{j})_{1\leq j\leq m+p}
be real parameters such that
\eta_{j}+\tau_{i},\xi_{j}-\tau_{i}
, and
\eta_{j}+\xi_{j^{\prime}}
are always positive.

Consider the weighted, directed graph
G^{\beta\Gamma}_{p,m}
(
Figure

22
(bottom)) with all vertices lying in the set

(4.10)

\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:-m\leq x\leq-1,-m-p\leq y\leq-1,x+y\geq-m-p\}\\
\cup\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:-m-p\leq y\leq-1,0\leq x\leq\min(p-1,y+m+p)\}

and edges given as follows:

(1)

For each vertex at
(x,y)
with
x\leq-1
, there are right and down-right directed edges to vertices
(x+1,y)
and
(x+1,y-1)
, with weights
\beta_{x,y}
and
1-\beta_{x,y}
respectively, where
\beta_{x,y}\sim\mathrm{Beta}(\eta_{-y}+\tau_{-x},\xi_{-x-y-1}-\tau_{-x})
are independent Beta variables.

(2)

For each vertex at
(x,y)
with
x\geq 0
lying in the set (
4.10
), there are down and right directed edges to vertices
(x,y-1)
and
(x+1,y)
(if they also lie in the set (
4.10
)). The downward edges have weight
1
, while the rightward edges have independent Gamma weights
\gamma_{x,y}\sim\Gamma(\eta_{-y}+\xi_{x-y},1)
.

Then the associated
\beta
-
\Gamma
polymer partition function
is

(4.11)

Z^{\beta\Gamma}_{p,m}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi_{j}:(-m,-j)\to(p-j,-m-j)\\
1\leq j\leq p\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{p}\wt(\pi_{j}),

where the sum is over
p
-tuples of nonintersecting
9
9

9

Meaning that the vertex sets of any two paths, not just the edge sets, are disjoint.
paths
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{p}
on
G^{\beta\Gamma}_{p,m}
, where
\pi_{j}
has start point
(-m,-j)
and end point
(p-j,-m-j)
, and
\wt(\pi_{j})
denotes the product of edge weights over the edges in
\pi_{j}
.

The associated
\beta
-
\Gamma
polymer measure
is a probability measure on such
p
-tuples
(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{p})
of nonintersecting paths which assigns to each one the probability

(4.12)

\frac{1}{Z^{\beta\Gamma}_{p,m}}\prod_{j=1}^{p}\wt(\pi_{j}).

We recall the following definitions from the Introduction.

Definition 4.6
.

Each of the polymer paths
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{p}
in
4.5
contains at least one vertex
(0,-y)
, and sometimes several. Let

\pi_{j}(m)=\min(\{y:(0,-y)\in\pi_{j}\})

be the distance of the closest such vertex to the
x
-axis. Then we define

(4.13)

X^{poly}(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{p})=\{\pi_{j}(m):1\leq j\leq p\}.

Recall that
|X^{poly}(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{p})|=p
since the paths are non-intersecting. We wish to match the distribution of this set with the following analogue for the Aztec diamond.

Definition 4.7
.

For an Aztec diamond
G_{n}^{\Az}
of size
n
, let
\mathcal{M}
be the set of perfect matchings of
G_{n}^{\Az}
. For any
1\leq\ell\leq n
, define functions

(4.14)

X_{\ell}^{\Az}:\mathcal{M}\to\binom{[n+1]}{n-\ell+1}

as follows. There are
n
columns of white vertices in
G_{n}^{\Az}
; consider the
\ell^{th}
one from left to right. For each perfect matching
M\in\mathcal{M}
, there will be exactly
n-\ell+1
white vertices in the
\ell^{th}
column which are matched to a black vertex to their left (either up-left or down-left). Labeling the white vertices in this column by
1,\ldots,n+1
from top to bottom, we let
X_{\ell}^{\Az}(M)
be the set of labels of the white vertices which are matched with a black vertex to their left (again, either up-left or down-left).

Recall that the graph
\bar{G}^{v-swap}_{n,\ell}
has a single column of
n
faces of degree
4
, which comes from the
\ell^{th}
column of such faces in the Aztec diamond
G_{n}^{\Az}
. For a matching
\tilde{M}
of
\bar{G}^{v-swap}_{n,\ell}
, we similarly define
\bar{X}(\tilde{M})
to be the set of labels of white vertices matched with a black vertex to their left (either horizontally left or down-left).

See
Figure

4
(left) for an example of
X_{\ell}^{\Az}
.

Lemma 4.8
.

Fix integers
n\geq p\geq 1
. Let
\bar{G}^{v-swap}_{n,n-p+1}
be the weighted bipartite graph of
4.4
, with deterministic weights. Let
G_{p,n-p+1}^{\beta\Gamma}
be the directed graph of

, but with deterministic weights defined in terms of those of
\bar{G}^{v-swap}_{n,n-p+1}
via

(4.15)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\gamma_{x,y}&=a^{[n-x]}_{\ell+1,-y}+b^{[n-x]}_{\ell+1,-y}\\
\beta_{x,y}&=\frac{a^{[n+x+1]}_{\ell+x+1,-y}}{a^{[n+x+1]}_{\ell+x+1,-y}+b^{[n+x+1]}_{\ell+x+1,-y}},\end{split}

where
\ell=n-p+1
. Then there is a bijection
M\mapsto(\pi_{1}(M),\ldots,\pi_{\ell}(M))
between perfect matchings
M
on

and

on

, which is weight-preserving and satisfies

\bar{X}(M)=X^{poly}(\pi_{1}(M),\ldots,\pi_{p}(M)).

Proof.

The bijection is as follows. The graph
\bar{G}^{v-swap}_{n,n-p+1}
can be divided into two halves, the left half consisting of
+

-

. There are four types of edges which, when present in a matching

, yield a corresponding segment of path
\pi_{j}(M)

(1)

Each up-right edge with lower-left vertex black and upper-right vertex white
10
10

10

Such edges occur only in the left half of
\bar{G}^{v-swap}_{n,n-p+1}
.
, corresponds to a horizontal path segment

(

) in the

,

(2)

Each horizontal edge

(black vertex on the left) in the

,

(3)

Each horizontal edge

(black vertex on the left) in the

corresponds to a horizontal path segment
(x,y)\to(x+1,y)
(

) in the

,

(4)

Each up-right edge with lower-left vertex white and upper-right vertex black
11

11

Such edges occur only in the right half of

See

(4.17)

\frac{a^{[n+x+1]}_{\ell+x+1,-y}}{a^{[n+x+1]}_{\ell+x+1,-y}+b^{[n+x+1]}_{\ell+x+1,-y}},\frac{b^{[n+x+1]}_{\ell+x+1,-y}}{a^{[n+x+1]}_{\ell+x+1,-y}+b^{[n+x+1]}_{\ell+x+1,-y}},a^{[n-x]}_{\ell+1,-y}+b^{[n-x]}_{\ell+1,-y},\text{ and }1

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
G_{n}^{\Az}
be an Aztec diamond graph with deterministic positive real weights

. Fix

and let

for a random matching

, and let

be the directed graph of
4.5
, with weights

(4.18)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\gamma_{x,y}&=a^{[n-x]}_{\ell+1,-y}+b^{[n-x]}_{\ell+1,-y}\\
\beta_{x,y}&=\frac{a^{[n+x+1]}_{\ell+x+1,-y}}{a^{[n+x+1]}_{\ell+x+1,-y}+b^{[n+x+1]}_{\ell+x+1,-y}},\end{split}

given in terms of the updated weights of the Aztec defined via (

), and let

(4.19)

X_{\ell}^{\Az}(M)=X^{poly}(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{p})\quad\quad\quad\quad\text{ in distribution.}

Proof.

By

, there is a distributional equality
X_{\ell}^{\Az}(M)=\bar{X}(\tM)
between the point configurations defined in
4.7
. Combining this with

4.8

∎

Theorem 4.10
.

Fix
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and let
(\phi_{j-n})_{1\leq j\leq n},(\psi_{j})_{1\leq j\leq n},(\theta_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
be real parameters such that
\psi_{j}+\theta_{i}
and
\phi_{j-n}-\theta_{i}
are positive for each
1\leq i,j\leq n
. Consider a Gamma-disordered Aztec diamond with these parameters (
1.7
), and let
M
be a random perfect matching distributed by the corresponding dimer measure.

Consider also an independent mixed
\beta
-
\Gamma
polymer on
G^{\beta\Gamma}_{p,m}
with
p=n-\ell+1
paths and
m=\ell
layers in the notation of
4.5
, with independent weights
12
12

12

One can of course match these parameters
\psi_{\cdots},\phi_{\cdots},\theta_{\cdots}
in the weights to the parameters
\eta_{j},\xi_{j},\tau_{i}
in the notation of
4.5
.

(4.20)

\displaystyle\beta_{x,y}

\displaystyle\sim\mathrm{Beta}(\psi_{-y}+\theta_{\ell+1+x},\phi_{-y-n-x-1}-\theta_{\ell+1+x})

(4.21)

\displaystyle\gamma_{x,y}

\displaystyle\sim\Gamma(\psi_{-y}+\phi_{-y+x-n},1).

Let
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{p}
be paths (ordered from top to bottom as in
4.5
) distributed according to the corresponding polymer measure. Then

(4.22)

X_{\ell}^{\Az}(M)=X^{poly}(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{p})\quad\quad\quad\quad\text{ in distribution.}

Proof of
4.10
.

Defining updated weights in terms of
a_{i,j}^{[n]},b_{i,j}^{[n]}
via (
3.1
) as usual, we define random variables
\gamma_{x,y},\beta_{x,y}
in terms of these updated weights via (
4.18
). Then
Lemma

4.9
gives an equality in distribution of the desired form. So it remains to show that the weights
\gamma_{x,y},\beta_{x,y}
are all independent and have distributions as in (
4.20
), (
4.21
). Independence of the weights in
\bar{G}^{v-swap}_{n,\ell}
follows by
Lemma

4.3
, and the weights in
G^{\beta\Gamma}_{n-\ell+1,\ell}
have the same joint distribution so the latter weights are independent. To obtain the exact distributions, we note that

(4.23)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{i,j}^{[k]}&\sim\Gamma(\psi_{j}+\theta_{i},1)\\
b_{i,j}^{[k]}&\sim\Gamma(\phi_{j-k}-\theta_{i},1)\end{split}

by (
3.6
). Hence

(4.24)

\displaystyle\beta_{x,y}

\displaystyle:=\frac{a^{[n+x+1]}_{\ell+x+1,-y}}{a^{[n+x+1]}_{\ell+x+1,-y}+b^{[n+x+1]}_{\ell+x+1,-y}}\sim\mathrm{Beta}(\psi_{-y}+\theta_{\ell+1+x},\phi_{-y-n-x-1}-\theta_{\ell+1+x})

(4.25)

\displaystyle\gamma_{x,y}

\displaystyle:=a^{[n-x]}_{\ell+1,-y}+b^{[n-x]}_{\ell+1,-y}\sim\Gamma(\psi_{-y}+\phi_{-y+x-n},1)

by
2.5
. This completes the proof.
∎

5.
Dynamical matching: shuffling trajectories and polymer paths

The previous section exhibited a distributional equality between vertical slices of an Aztec matching and a multi-path polymer model
G^{\beta\Gamma}_{p,m}
, namely
4.10
. Now that we have this polymer model, it is natural to ask if the path trajectories on
G^{\beta\Gamma}_{p,m}
outside of this single slice mean anything for the Aztec diamond. It turns out that they do: they track the trajectory of our chosen slice of the matching as it grows via the shuffling algorithm. We first describe this algorithm, then formulate the main result,
5.1
.

5.1.
Shuffling algorithm from column-swapping

The shuffling algorithm is an algorithm to generate a random perfect matching of a size
n
Aztec diamond with arbitrary weights. In the process of doing so, it actually generates a coupled sequence of matchings of the Aztec diamonds of sizes
1,2,\ldots,n
. We are interested not just in the size
n
Aztec matching, but in the coupled sequence. Our formulation here will be in terms of commutation of
(+)
- and
(-)
-columns (
4.1
), by contrast with the discussion in the Introduction and in
Section

2
.

As in
Section

4
we draw all graphs on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. All graphs considered in this section will be sequences of
(+)
- and
(-)
-columns in some order, capped by columns of
1
-valent black vertices on the right and left, as with
G_{n}^{vert}
and
G^{v-swap}_{n,\ell}
in the previous section.

Because the commutation of
(+)
- and
(-)
-columns (
Lemma

4.2
) involves only the spider move and vertex-contraction,
2.2
and
2.3
give a natural coupling between dimer covers before the commutation and after the commutation. Hence there is a natural coupling—or, equivalently, transition kernel between—matchings of two graphs such as those represented in
Figure

23
(middle and bottom), which are copies of
G_{n}^{vert}
and
G_{n+1}^{vert}
with extra frozen ends.

This transition kernel is the basic step of the shuffling algorithm. Note that one gets two transition kernels, the ‘up-shuffle’ from size
n
to size
n+1
, and the ‘down-shuffle’ from size
n+1
to size
n
. These are both equivalent ways of viewing the coupling between the two. The down-shuffle is a deterministic map on tilings, in contrast to the up-shuffle.

To couple multiple matchings of the Aztec diamond at different
n
, we will augment the Aztec diamond by additional
(-)
-columns on the left and
(+)
-columns on the right. These additional columns are frozen (there is only one way to tile them in a perfect matching) as in the proof of
Lemma

4.5
. We may then repeat the above transition map multiple times.

So, it is natural to begin with the graph
G^{v-swap}_{n,n+1}
as defined in
4.3
, see
Figure

23
(top). This graph has two connected components and is composed of
n

(-)
-columns, followed by
n

(+)
-columns, with a column of pendant edges on each side as in
G_{n}^{vert}
or
G^{v-swap}_{n,\ell}
in the previous section. The weights are as described in
4.3
.

G^{vert}_{1}
G^{vert}_{2}

Figure 23
.
The graph
G^{v-swap}_{7,8}
before shuffling begins (top). Commuting the trivial columns in the middle of
G^{v-swap}_{7,8}
yields a graph
G^{v-swap}_{7,7}
with a copy of
G_{1}^{vert}
in the middle and frozen regions outside (middle). Commuting the rightmost pair of
(-)
-columns with their right neighbors yields a graph with a copy of
G_{2}^{vert}
in the middle (bottom) and frozen regions outside. Weights not pictured, but see
4.3
for those of
G^{v-swap}_{7,8}
, from which all others are derived.

Figure 24
.
The diagrams for the pre-shuffling graph
G^{v-swap}_{7,8}
(red), the size
1
Aztec diamond (green), the size
2
Aztec diamond (blue), all depicted in
Figure

23
. Also depicted is the size
7
Aztec diamond
G^{vert}_{7}
(purple). Note that the paths intersect the
y
- and
x
-axis in the first three cases; these portions correspond to frozen blocks of
(-)
- and
(+)
-columns on the corresponding graphs.

As in the previous section, we will typically take the weights of the Gamma-disordered Aztec diamond with general parameters (
1.7
), but our distributional matchings also hold for arbitrary choices of deterministic real weights on the Aztec diamond, where the polymer weights are given as (deterministic) functions of these. Our random weights simply have the feature that both sets of weights are independent collections of random variables.

Commuting the rightmost
(-)
-column with the leftmost
(+)
-column in
Figure

23
(top), we obtain a graph
G_{1}^{vert}
sandwiched between two frozen blocks of
n-1

(-)
- and
(+)
-columns on the left and right respectively, see
Figure

23
(middle). Commuting the rightmost two
(-)
-columns with the leftmost two
(+)
-columns then yields
G_{2}^{vert}
sandwiched between two frozen regions (
Figure

23
(bottom)). And so on, until the
(+)
and
(-)
-columns are in alternating order after the
n^{th}
such step. As mentioned, the coupling in
2.2
yields transition kernels between the dimer measures of each such graph. This is the shuffling algorithm.

The above description actually gives more than just a coupling of matchings on the sequence of graphs
G_{j}^{vert},1\leq j\leq n
. It gives a coupling of matchings on all graphs coming from interchange of
(+)
- and
(-)
-columns on the original
G^{v-swap}_{n,n+1}
. This is because the coupling in
2.2
is local, so for any given pair of adjacent
(+)
- and
(-)
-columns, it does not matter which other columns are swapped before vs. after the given pair is swapped.

For instance, two column pairs must be commuted to get between the middle and bottom graph in
Figure

23
, and locality guarantees that it does not matter which order these swaps happen in. There is actually a coupling between matchings not just on those two graphs, but on those two graphs and the two ‘intermediate’ graphs where either pair of columns is swapped but not the other.

5.2.
Main result

Sample a coupled sequence of matchings
M_{1},M_{2},\ldots
of the Aztec diamond graphs of size
1,2,\ldots
according to the shuffling algorithm as described above
13
13

13

As phrased above, the shuffling algorithm must stop at the graph
G_{n}^{vert}
, because there are no more
(-)
-columns on the left or
(+)
-columns on the right. However, by either noting that this algorithm is consistent for different
n
or by simply defining an infinite graph, one may run the shuffling algorithm for all time
n
. Because we will only ever run it for a finite number of steps in what follows, we ignore this detail and simply choose the ambient graph large enough.
. Let
X^{\Az}_{\ell}(M_{\tau})
be the
\ell^{th}
-column point configuration as defined in
4.7
, and for convenience extend that definition by the convention

(5.1)

X^{\Az}_{0}(M_{n}):=\{1,\ldots,n+1\}

for any matching
M_{n}
of
G^{\Az}_{n}
or
G_{n}^{vert}
. Fix also a positive integer
k
, which will index the slice of the Aztec. We are interested in the random sequence

(5.2)

X^{\Az}_{\tau}(M_{\tau+k-1}),\quad\quad\tau\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},

which we view as a stochastic process in time
\tau
. Its state space is the set
\binom{\mathbb{N}}{k}
of
k
-element subsets of
\mathbb{N}
, and our convention for
\tau=0
simply makes
X^{\Az}_{0}(M_{k-1})=[k]
a deterministic initial condition.

Then the first random step of this process corresponds to the first matching
M_{k}
which is big enough have a
k^{th}
column, and since the
\tau^{th}
column from the left on
M_{\tau+k-1}
is the
k^{th}
column from the right, this process describes the evolution of the
k^{th}
column
from the right
of the matching. Our main result of this section,
5.1
, matches these trajectories with the polymer path trajectories, which we define formally as follows.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{p}
be the paths of a
\beta
-
\Gamma
polymer on the directed graph
G_{p,m}^{\beta\Gamma}
from
4.5
. For each path
\pi_{j}
, let
\pi_{j}(0),\pi_{j}(1),\ldots,\pi_{j}(m-1)
be the negative
y
-coordinates of the vertices
(-m,\cdot),(-m+1,\cdot),\ldots,(-1,\cdot)
on the path, and let
\pi_{j}(m)
be as already defined in
4.6
14
14

14

Here recall that there are several
y
-coordinates because the paths may move down, and
4.6
tells us how to select one.
. For each
0\leq\tau\leq m
, let
\Pi(\tau)=(\pi_{1}(\tau),\ldots,\pi_{p}(\tau))
. For later use in
Section

7
, we allow this notation on any directed graph with the same underlying graph as
G_{p,m}^{\beta\Gamma}
even if the weights are different.

Note that
\Pi(m)=X^{poly}(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{p})
as defined in
4.6
.

Example 5.1
.

In the path configuration of

,

(5.3)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Pi(0)&=(1,2,3,4,5)\\

was already discussed in

.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
(\phi_{j})_{j\leq 0},(\psi_{j})_{j\geq 1},(\theta_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be real parameters such that
\psi_{j}+\theta_{i}
and
\phi_{j}-\theta_{i}
are positive for all
i,j
.
Sample a coupled sequence of matchings
M_{1},M_{2},\ldots
of the Aztec diamonds of size
1,2,\ldots
according to the shuffling algorithm with these parameters (see
Section

5.1
).

Then the ‘
k^{th}
column from the right’ stochastic process
X^{\Az}_{\tau}(M_{\tau+k-1}),\tau\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, described above, has the following description. Fix any ‘final time’
T>0
, and let
G_{k,T}^{\beta\Gamma}
be a
\beta
-
\Gamma
polymer with weights defined in terms of the Aztec weights as in
Lemma

4.8
. Let
(\Pi(\tau))_{0\leq\tau\leq T}
be the joint polymer path trajectories as in
5.1
. Then

(5.4)

(X^{\Az}_{\tau}(M_{\tau+k-1}))_{0\leq\tau\leq T}=(\Pi(\tau))_{0\leq\tau\leq T}

in distribution, for any fixed realization of the weights
a_{i,j}^{[n]},b_{i,j}^{[n]}
.

Proof.

X_{\ell}^{\Az}(M)
is defined on matchings
M
of
G_{n}^{\Az}
. However, these are in bijection with matchings on
G_{n}^{vert}
, so
X_{\ell}^{\Az}
gives a function on the latter. This function only depends on the
\ell^{th}

(+)
-column from the left in such a graph, because
X_{\ell}^{\Az}
only depends on the edges in the left part of the
\ell^{th}
column of
G_{n}^{\Az}
.

The random variables
X_{\tau}^{\Az}(M_{\tau+k-1})
(when
\tau\geq 1
) thus depend on the first
(+)
-column of
M_{k}
, second
(+)
-column of
M_{k+1}
, etc. up until the
T^{th}

(+)
-column of
M_{T+k-1}
, as depicted in
Figure

25
.

Figure 25
.
The previous discussion in the case
k=2
,
T=3
. Purple, orange, and blue lines correspond to Aztec diamonds (embedded in frozen columns as in
Section

5.1
) of sizes
2
,
3
and
4
respectively. Horizontal segments corresponding to
(+)
-columns on which the relevant observables
X_{\tau}^{\Az}(M_{\tau+k-1})
depend are labeled with those observables.

Figure 26
.
The depiction of
G^{v-swap}_{4,3}
corresponding to
Figure

25
. Its non-frozen
(+)
-columns are exactly the same labeled ones in that figure. The segments of the dotted green line intersecting the axes correspond to the two frozen regions.

However, these
(+)
-columns exactly correspond to the first block of
(+)
-columns of
G^{v-swap}_{T+k-1,T}
, see
Figure

26
. Furthermore, by the discussion of
Section

5.1
, shuffling gives a coupling between matchings of
G^{v-swap}_{T+k-1,T}
(or any other intermediate graph) and the matchings
M_{1},\ldots,M_{T+k-1}
. Because the coupling in
2.2
is local, the (non-frozen)
(+)
-columns of
G^{v-swap}_{T+k-1,T}
have exactly the same configurations of edges as the corresponding ones from Aztec shuffling, namely the first
(+)
-column of
M_{k}
, second
(+)
-column of
M_{k+1}
, etc. up until the
T^{th}

(+)
-column of
M_{T+k-1}
.

We thus obtain a distributional equality between
(X_{\tau}^{\Az}(M_{\tau+k-1}))_{1\leq\tau\leq T}
and the same statistics for
G^{v-swap}_{T+k-1,T}
. Matching the latter graph with the polymer
G^{\beta\Gamma}_{k,T}
via the combinatorial bijection of
Lemma

4.8
concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.1
.

By exactly the same argument as in the proof of
5.1
, one may also match the right (strict-weak) half of
G^{\beta\Gamma}_{\cdots}
with the distribution of the
\ell^{th}

(-)
-columns from the left in the Aztec diamond. We omit this only to avoid setting up more cumbersome notation; it is no more difficult.

6.
Exact matching of East and West turning points with stationary polymers

Two cases of
4.10
which are of particular interest to us are
\ell=1
and
\ell=n
, the leftmost and rightmost vertical slices of the Aztec diamond. These are the West and East turning points defined in
1.2
.

6.1.
The East turning point

We begin with the second case
\ell=n
, which is slightly easier. The below random walk model was to our knowledge first considered by Korotkikh
[
51
]
, deforming the case introduced by Barraquand-Corwin
[
3
]
.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
(\psi_{j})_{j\geq 1},(\phi_{j})_{j\leq 0},(\theta_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be real numbers such that
\psi_{j}+\theta_{i}
and
\phi_{1-j}-\theta_{i}
are positive for all
i,j\geq 1
. Then the
deformed Beta-random walk in random environment (RWRE)

X_{t},t\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
(with the above deformation parameters) is the random walk on the negative integers with initial condition
X_{0}=-1
and transition probabilities

(6.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{P}(X_{t+1}=y|X_{t}=y)&=B_{y,t}\\
\mathbb{P}(X_{t+1}=y-1|X_{t}=y)&=1-B_{y,t},\end{split}

where

(6.2)

B_{y,t}\sim\mathrm{Beta}(\psi_{-y}+\theta_{t+1},\phi_{-y-t-1}-\theta_{t+1})

are mutually independent Beta variables.

t
y
X_{t}
(0,-1)
B_{t,y}
1-B_{t,y}
(t,y)
\psi
\phi
\theta

Figure 27
.
The Beta-RWRE of
6.1
(right), with step distributions (left, upper) and directions along which the
\psi,\phi
and
\theta
parameters are constant (left, lower).

Remark 6.1
.

The Beta-RWRE is usually defined as a sheared version of the above with steps
\pm 1
and state space
\mathbb{Z}
in works such as
[
3
]
and
[
51
]
; the reason we shear as above is to match with our previous notation on the Aztec diamond and
\beta
-
\Gamma
polymer. Our parameters
\psi,\theta,\phi
correspond to
\tilde{\sigma},-\tilde{\rho},-\tilde{\omega}
in the notation of
[
51
, Section 1.3]
, with indices translated appropriately.

The following result follows from
5.1
.

Corollary 6.1
.

Let
(\phi_{j})_{j\leq 0},(\psi_{j})_{j\geq 1},(\theta_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be real parameters such that
\psi_{j}+\theta_{i}
and
\phi_{1-j}-\theta_{i}
are positive for all
i,j\geq 1
. Sample a coupled sequence of matchings
M_{1},M_{2},\ldots
of the Aztec diamonds of size
1,2,\ldots
according to the shuffling algorithm with these parameters (see
Section

5.1
). Then for any
T\geq 0
,

(6.3)

T^{East}(M_{\tau})=-X_{\tau}-1\quad\quad\quad\text{in joint distribution over $0\leq\tau\leq T$,}

where
X_{\tau}
is an independent Beta-RWRE as in
6.1
with the same parameters
\psi_{j},\phi_{j},\theta_{i}
as the Aztec diamond, and we recall the notation
T^{East}
from
1.2
.

Proof.

T^{East}(M_{\tau})+1
is the unique element of the set
X_{\tau}^{\Az}(M_{\tau})
in the notation of
4.7
. Hence the
k=1
case of
5.1
yields that

(6.4)

(T^{East}(M_{\tau})+1)_{0\leq\tau\leq T}=(\Pi(\tau))_{0\leq\tau\leq T}

in distribution, where
\Pi(\tau)
gives the negative
y
-coordinate of a path on
G_{1,T}^{\beta\Gamma}
(recall
5.1
). Since the strict-weak (right) part of
G_{1,T}^{\beta\Gamma}
consists of a single column, all weights on edges that our directed path may encounter are
1
. So the path in
G_{1,T}^{\beta\Gamma}
moves through
T
columns of Beta-distributed directed edges, and then exits the bottom. Furthermore, the parameters on these Beta-distributed edges exactly match those of
6.1
, and this proves (
6.3
).

∎

6.2.
The West turning point

We proceed to the
\ell=1
case, which gives the turning point at the West boundary of the Aztec diamond and proves
1.4
. This case is harder than the East turning point because
G_{n,1}^{\beta\Gamma}
has
n
paths rather than a single path as in the previous case. However, it turns out that we can relate it to a model with only a single path through a certain complementation bijection. We first restate
1.3
for convenience, slightly generalized to points
(m,n)
rather than
(n,n)
but otherwise identical.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>0
. Place an independent random weight
Y_{i,j}
on every point
(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
, where
Y_{0,0}=1
and those with
(i,j)\neq(0,0)
have distributions

(6.5)

Y_{i,j}\sim\begin{cases}\Gamma^{-1}(\beta,1)&j=0\\
\Gamma^{-1}(\alpha,1)&i=0\\
\Gamma^{-1}(\alpha+\beta,1)&i,j>0\end{cases}.

These are known as
stationary log-Gamma polymer
weights.

Let
\Pi_{m,n}
be the set of up-right paths with steps
(i,j)\to(i+1,j)
and
(i,j)\to(i,j+1)
, beginning at
(0,0)
and ending at
(m,n)
. Then the associated partition function is

(6.6)

Z^{stat-\Gamma}_{m,n}:=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{m,n}}\prod_{(i,j)\in\pi}Y_{i,j},

where the product is over the weights on all lattice points in the path. The associated
quenched
polymer measure on
\Pi_{m,n}
is

(6.7)

Q^{stat-\Gamma}_{m,n}(\pi)=\frac{\prod_{(i,j)\in\pi}Y_{i,j}}{Z^{stat-\Gamma}_{m,n}},

a random probability measure depending on the weights. The associated
annealed
polymer measure on
\Pi_{m,n}
is

(6.8)

\mathbb{P}_{m,n}^{stat-\Gamma}(\pi)=\mathbb{E}[Q^{stat-\Gamma}_{m,n}(\pi)].

The annealed probability measure just gives the distribution of a random path given by first sampling the polymer environment of Gamma variables, then sampling a path with respect to the quenched polymer measure
Q^{stat-\Gamma}_{m,n}
associated to those weights. The following result is the
raison d’être
of the above weights.

Proposition 6.2

(Burke property)
.

In the notation of
6.2
,
let

(6.9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}U_{m,n}&=\frac{Z^{stat-\Gamma}_{m,n}}{Z^{stat-\Gamma}_{m-1,n}}\\
V_{m,n}&=\frac{Z^{stat-\Gamma}_{m,n}}{Z^{stat-\Gamma}_{m,n-1}}\end{split}

whenever
m\geq 1,n\geq 0
(for
U_{m,n}
) or
m\geq 0,n\geq 1
(for
V_{m,n}
) so both partition functions are defined. Then

(6.10)

\displaystyle\begin{split}U_{m,n}&\sim\Gamma^{-1}(\beta,1)\\
V_{m,n}&\sim\Gamma^{-1}(\alpha,1)\end{split}

for every
(m,n)
. Furthermore, associating
U_{m,n}
and
V_{m,n}
to the directed horizontal edge
(m-1,n)\to(m,n)
and vertical edge
(m,n)\to(m,n-1)
respectively, we have that for any down-right path in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
, the
U
and
V
variables associated to its right and down segments are all mutually independent.

Proof.

This is a special case of
[
74
, Theorem 3.3]
, which also treats independence of certain additional bulk weights
X_{m,n}
which we ignore here.
∎

The source of the name ‘stationary’ in
6.2
is the following result, which follows directly from the Burke property. We will not use it directly until
Section

6
, but it is natural to prove it here.

Corollary 6.3
.

Let
1\leq m\leq M
and
1\leq n\leq N
be integers. Let

(6.11)

s_{M-m,N-n}(x,y)=(x-(M-m),y-(N-n))

be the shift map. Let
\pi\in\Pi_{M,N}
and
\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi_{m,n}
be distributed by two independent copies of
\mathbb{P}^{stat-\Gamma}_{M,N}
and
\mathbb{P}^{stat-\Gamma}_{m,n}
respectively. Then the marginals
\pi^{\prime}\cap([1,m]\times[1,n])
and
s_{M-m,N-n}(\pi\cap[M-m+1,M]\times[N-n+1,N])
are equal in distribution.

Proof.

By
6.2
, the ratios of partition functions
Z_{i,N-n}/Z_{i-1,N-n}
and
Z_{M-m,j}/Z_{M-m,j-1}
in the larger rectangle are independent and have the same distributions as the boundary weights
Z_{i,0}/Z_{i-1,0}
and
Z_{0,j}/Z_{0,j-1}
in the smaller
m\times n
rectangle.
6.3
follows immediately. ∎

(M,0)
(0,0)
(0,N)
(M,N)
(M-m,N-n)

Figure 28
.
A path
\pi\sim\mathbb{P}^{stat-\Gamma}_{M,N}
starting at
(0,0)
(black).
6.3
says that the portion inside the green rectangle (red) as the same (annealed) distribution as the interior portion of a path
\pi^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}^{stat-\Gamma}_{m,n}
in an independent environment.

The quantity of interest to us is the point at which a polymer path intersects the line
x+y=n
.

Definition 6.3
.

For paths
\pi
as in
6.2
with
n\leq m
, let
x_{mid}(\pi)
be the unique
x
-value such that
(x,n-x)\in\pi
(see
Figure

3
).

Remark 6.2
.

We only prove the ‘single-time’ matching in
1.4
because that is all we use in our asymptotics, though we note that it is not hard to prove a dynamical version similar to
6.1
.

We begin on the Aztec diamond side, first matching the West turning point (with fully general parameters) with a related single-path log-Gamma polymer model, given in
6.4
and depicted in
Figure

29
, via a path-complementation bijection. We will then specialize parameters and show how to match this polymer with the version in
6.2
. However, for the time being we use a coordinate system for this new polymer which is shifted and does not match the one in
6.2
, as this one is more suited to the coordinates on the Aztec diamond.

Definition 6.4
.

Fix
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, two sequences of positive real parameters
(\psi_{j})_{1\leq j\leq n},(\phi_{j-n})_{1\leq j\leq n}
, and
\theta_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\psi_{j}+\theta_{1}>0
and
\phi_{j-n}-\theta_{1}>0
for all
1\leq j\leq n
. Equip each shifted lattice point in
\{(x+1/2,y):y\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq-1},x\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},y-x\geq-n\}
with a random weight
1/\gamma_{x,y}
, where

(6.12)

\gamma_{x,y}\sim\Gamma(\psi_{-y}+\phi_{-y+x-2n},1)

are independent. Define another independent set of boundary weights

(6.13)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{j}&\sim\Gamma(\psi_{j}+\theta_{1},1)\\
b_{j}&\sim\Gamma(\phi_{j-n}-\theta_{1},1).\end{split}

Given a path
\tilde{\pi}:(-1/2,y)\to(n-1/2,-1)
from a point
(-1/2,y)
(where
-1\leq y\leq-n-1
) to
(n-1/2,-1)
which consists only of horizontal
(x,y)\to(x+1,y)
and up-right
(x,y)\to(x+1,y+1)
steps, we define its bulk weight to be

(6.14)

\wt(\tilde{\pi})=\prod_{(x+1/2,y)\in\tilde{\pi}}1/\gamma(x,y)

where the product over
(x+1/2,y)\in\tilde{\pi}
is over all points of
(\mathbb{Z}+1/2)\times\mathbb{Z}
contained in
\tilde{\pi}
, excluding the two endpoints. We also use the notation
Y(\tilde{\pi})
for the coordinate
y
of its left endpoint.

Then
Q^{edge-\Gamma}_{n}(\cdot)
is the probability measure (random, depending on the above weights) on the set of paths defined above, defined by

(6.15)

Q^{edge-\Gamma}_{n}(\tilde{\pi})\propto\wt(\tilde{\pi})\prod_{j=1}^{-Y(\tilde{\pi})-1}a_{j}/b_{j}.

Lemma 6.4
.

Fix
(\psi_{j})_{1\leq j\leq n},(\phi_{j-n})_{1\leq j\leq n},(\theta_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
with
\psi_{j}+\theta_{i}>0
and
\phi_{j-n}-\theta_{i}>0
for all
i,j
and let
M
be a perfect matching from the dimer measure on
G_{n}^{\Az}
with random weights given by these parameters. Let
\tilde{\pi}
be distributed by an independent copy of
Q^{edge-\Gamma}_{n}
with the same parameters
(\psi_{j})_{1\leq j\leq n},(\phi_{j-n})_{1\leq j\leq n},\theta_{1}
as the Aztec diamond, and
Y(\tilde{\pi})
is as in
6.4
. Then

(6.16)

T^{West}(M)=-Y(\tilde{\pi})-1\quad\quad\quad\text{in distribution,}

Proof.

If
a_{i,j}^{[n]},b_{i,j}^{[n]}
are the weights of our Aztec diamond, then defining

(6.17)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\gamma_{x,y}&=a_{2,-y}^{[n-x]}+b_{2,-y}^{[n-x]}\\
a_{j}&=a_{1,j}^{[n]}\\
b_{j}&=b_{1,j}^{[n]}\end{split}

(where the weights defining
\gamma_{x,y}
are defined recursively via shuffling through (
3.1
)), these will be distributed as in (
6.12
) and (
6.13
). As before, we will show distributional equality by showing a finer statement, which is that for any deterministic nonnegative weights
a_{i,j}^{[n]},b_{i,j}^{[n]}
, there is a deterministic bijection between path ensembles
(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n})
on
G_{n,1}^{\beta\Gamma}
and paths
\tilde{\pi}
on the the free-weight log-Gamma polymer with weights as in (
6.17
), which preserves weights up to an overall factor of

(6.18)

C:=\prod_{j=1}^{n}b_{1,j}^{[n]}/(a_{1,j}^{[n]}+b_{1,j}^{[n]}).

Let us show this bijection. Consider the
n
paths in
G_{n,1}^{\beta\Gamma}
. Each path takes either a single downward step on the right portion of the graph, or a down-right step on the left portion, as can be seen in
Figure

29
. Call these E-paths and SE-paths, after the type of step they take in the leftmost column.

If a given path
\pi_{j}
is an E-path, then all paths
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{j-1}
above it are also E-paths, as if any of them took a down-right step there would be a collision. For each place in which one of the paths
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n}
makes a downward step
(x,y)\to(x,y-1)
, draw a segment of dual path
(x+1/2,y)\to(x-1/2,y-1)
. For each vertex
(x,y)
that has no path incident to it, draw a segment of dual path
(x+1/2,y)\to(x-1/2,y)
. This produces a dual path
\tilde{\pi}
from
(n-1/2,-1)
to
(-1/2,-k-1)
, where
k
is the number of E-paths.

Because
\tilde{\pi}
picks up exactly the weights
\gamma_{x,y}=a_{2,-y}^{[n-x]}+b_{2,-y}^{[n-x]}
which the paths
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n}
do not pick up, we see that for any
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n}
and
\tilde{\pi}
the corresponding complement path as above, we have

(6.19)

\frac{1}{\wt(\tilde{\pi})}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{n}\wt(\pi_{j})=\frac{a_{1,1}^{[n]}}{a_{1,1}^{[n]}+b_{1,1}^{[n]}}\cdots\frac{a_{1,-Y(\tilde{\pi})-1}^{[n]}}{a_{1,-Y(\tilde{\pi})-1}^{[n]}+b_{1,-Y(\tilde{\pi})-1}^{[n]}}\frac{b_{1,-Y(\tilde{\pi})}^{[n]}}{a_{1,-Y(\tilde{\pi})}^{[n]}+b_{1,-Y(\tilde{\pi})}^{[n]}}\cdots\frac{b_{1,n}^{[n]}}{a_{1,n}^{[n]}+b_{1,n}^{[n]}}

where
Y(\tilde{\pi})
is the coordinate of the endpoint as in the end of
6.4
. The factors on the right hand side of (
6.19
) come from the
\beta
RWRE portion of
G_{n,1}^{\beta\Gamma}
(which is just a single column). We see that

(6.20)

\text{RHS\eqref{eq:paths_and_complements}}=C\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{-Y(\tilde{\pi})-1}a_{1,j}^{[n]}/b_{1,j}^{[n]}

where
C
is the overall factor in (
6.18
). Using this and multiplying both sides by
\wt(\tilde{\pi})
, we have that

(6.21)

C\wt(\tilde{\pi})\prod_{j=1}^{-Y(\tilde{\pi})-1}a_{1,j}^{[n]}/b_{1,j}^{[n]}=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\wt(\pi_{j}).

This shows that our bijection is weight-preserving up to the overall factor
C
.

(0,-8)
(1,-7)
(2,-6)
(3,-5)
(4,-4)
(5,-3)
(6,-2)
(6\frac{1}{2},-1)
