.
An instance of paths
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{7}
(black) on the graph
G_{7,1}^{\beta\Gamma}
(gray), and the free-weight log-Gamma polymer path
\tilde{\pi}
obtained by the complementation bijection in the proof of
Lemma

6.4
(red). Here
Y(\tilde{\pi})=-5
, and
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{4}
are E-paths while
\pi_{5},\pi_{6},\pi_{7}
are SE-paths in the notation of the proof.

Now let us use this bijection to prove the desired distributional equality. The
\ell=1
case of
4.10
relates the Aztec diamond to a polymer model with
n
paths on
G_{n,1}^{\beta\Gamma}
. More specifically, the random
n
-element set
X_{1}^{\Az}(M)
in the notation of
4.7
is just
[n+1]\setminus\{T^{West}(M)+1\}
.
4.10
then gives a distributional equality

(6.22)

[n+1]\setminus\{T^{West}(M)+1\}=X^{poly}(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n}),

so
T^{West}(M)
is the (negative of) the unique
y
-coordinate between
-1
and
-n-1
which does
not
have a path entering it from the left (recall
4.6
). The weight-preserving bijection we just showed furthermore proves

(6.23)

X^{poly}(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n})=[n+1]\setminus\{-Y(\tilde{\pi})\}\quad\quad\quad\quad\text{ in distribution,}

where as usual
Y(\tilde{\pi})
is the
y
-coordinate of the left endpoint of a path
\tilde{\pi}
of the free-weight log-Gamma polymer. Combining (
6.22
) with (
6.23
) completes the proof.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

The path-complementation/particle-hole bijection that we apply to go from a polymer model with
n
paths on the graph
G_{n,1}^{\beta\Gamma}
to one with
1
path as in
6.4
may also be applied to
G_{p,m}^{\beta\Gamma}
more generally. It yields a polymer model with
m
paths, with a random environment which is partially log-Gamma polymer as in
6.4
(corresponding to the strict-weak portion after path-complementation), and partially the
inverse-Beta polymer
of Thiery-Le Doussal
[
80
]
(corresponding to the Beta-RWRE portion after path-complementation). The full matching will be discussed in upcoming work.

Now we have related the West turning point of the Aztec diamond to a log-Gamma polymer model with extra weighting, but this is still not obviously the same as the log-Gamma path midpoint featured in
1.4
. The key to matching them is the Burke property. The following lemma, a corollary of the Burke property, shows that the partition functions along the antidiagonal
x+y=n
differ from one another by quotients of independent Gamma variables. These will match with the
a_{j}/b_{j}
boundary weight factors in
6.4
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\alpha,\beta>0
and
Z^{stat-\Gamma}_{i,j}
be as in
6.2
. Then there exist random variables
\{U_{i-1,n-i+1},V_{i,n-i}:1\leq i\leq n\}
, which are independent of each other and of the above-antidiagonal weights
\{Y_{i,j}:i+j\geq n+1\}
, satisfying

(6.24)

\displaystyle\begin{split}U_{i,n-i}&\sim\Gamma^{-1}(\beta,1)\\
V_{i,n-i}&\sim\Gamma^{-1}(\alpha,1),\end{split}

and

(6.25)

Z^{stat-\Gamma}_{i+1,n-i-1}=\frac{U_{i,n-i}}{V_{i+1,n-i-1}}\cdot Z^{stat-\Gamma}_{i,n-i}

for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
.

Proof.

(
6.24
) and (
6.25
) follow directly from applying
6.2
along the down-right path composed of the edges
(i,n-i)\to(i,n-i-1)
and
(i,n-i-1)\to(i+1,n-i-1)
, for
i=0,1,\ldots,n-1
. The independence from
\{Y_{i,j}:i+j\geq n+1\}
is trivial since the partition functions defining
U_{i,n-i}
and
V_{i,n-i}
by their quotients do not involve paths passing through any of the weights
\{Y_{i,j}:i+j\geq n+1\}
.
∎

Lemma 6.6
.

Fix
n
and
\alpha,\beta>0
, and set the
\psi,\phi,\theta
parameters of
6.4
to the homogeneous case

(6.26)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\theta_{i}&\equiv 0\\
\psi_{j}&\equiv\alpha\\
\phi_{j}&\equiv\beta.\end{split}

Let
\tilde{\pi}
be distributed according to the polymer measure
Q^{edge-\Gamma}_{n}
of
6.4
with these weights
\gamma_{x,y},a_{j},b_{j}
. Let
\pi
be distributed by a copy of the measure
Q^{stat-\Gamma}_{n,n}
with weights independent of those of
Q^{edge-\Gamma}_{n}
. Then

(6.27)

-Y(\tilde{\pi})=x_{mid}(\pi)+1\quad\quad\quad\quad\text{in distribution.}

Proof.

The basic idea is portrayed in
Figure

30
. The paths
\tilde{\pi}
correspond to the portion of paths
\pi
which lies up-right from the line
i+j=n
, and the boundary weights of the paths
\tilde{\pi}
correspond to point-to-point partition functions from
(0,0)
to a point on the line
i+j=n
in the stationary log-Gamma model.

(0,0)
(0,n)
(n,0)
(n,n)
f\circ\nu
\longrightarrow
(c,n-c)
(-\frac{1}{2},-n-1)
(-\frac{1}{2},-1)
(-\frac{1}{2},-c-1)
(n-\frac{1}{2},-1)

Figure 30
.
A sample polymer path from
(0,0)
to
(n,n)
(left), and its image under
f\circ\nu
where we recall
f(x,y)=(x+y-n-\frac{1}{2},y-n-1)
. Here
n=10
and
c=6
.

More specifically, identifying paths
\pi
with their vertex set in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
, we let

(6.28)

\upsilon(\pi)=\pi\cap\{(i,j):i+j\geq n\}.

This is a path from any point on the antidiagonal
i+j=n
to the point
(n,n)
. Let
\hat{\pi}
denote any such path, with starting point
(c,n-c)
. Under the pushforward measure
\upsilon_{*}(Q^{stat-\Gamma}_{n,n})
, we have

(6.29)

(\upsilon_{*}(Q^{stat-\Gamma}_{n,n}))(\hat{\pi})\propto Z^{stat-\Gamma}_{c,n-c}\cdot\prod_{(i,j)\in\hat{\pi}}Y_{i,j},

with the convention that the product of weights does not include the starting weight
Y_{c,n-c}
since it already contributes to
Z^{stat-\Gamma}_{c,n-c}
. By
Lemma

6.5
,

(6.30)

Z^{stat-\Gamma}_{c,n-c}\cdot\prod_{(i,j)\in\hat{\pi}}Y_{i,j}=Z^{stat-\Gamma}_{0,n}\prod_{i=1}^{c}\frac{U_{i-1,n-i+1}}{V_{i,n-i}}\prod_{(i,j)\in\hat{\pi}}Y_{i,j}.

Combining (
6.29
) with (
6.30
) yields

(6.31)

(\upsilon_{*}(Q^{stat-\Gamma}_{n,n}))(\hat{\pi})\propto\prod_{i=1}^{c(\hat{\pi})}\frac{U_{i-1,n-i+1}}{V_{i,n-i}}\prod_{(i,j)\in\hat{\pi}}Y_{i,j},

for any up-right path
\hat{\pi}
with starting point
(c(\hat{\pi}),n-c(\hat{\pi}))
on the diagonal
i+j=n
, and ending point
(n,n)
.

Let

(6.32)

f(x,y)=(x+y-n-\frac{1}{2},y-n-1),

so
f
—which is just the composition of a standard shear and a translation—takes the triangle

(6.33)

\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:x+y\geq n,x\leq n,y\leq n\}

(on which the paths
\hat{\pi}
live) to the triangle

(6.34)

\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:x\geq 0,y\leq-1,y-x\geq-n-1/2\}

on which the paths
\tilde{\pi}
live. See
Figure

30
.

Again identifying paths with their vertex sets, given any up-right path
\pi
from
(0,0)
to
(n,n)
,
f\circ\upsilon(\pi)
is a valid path in the support of
Q^{edge-\Gamma}_{n}
. For a diagonal point
(c,n-c)
, we have
f(c,n-c)=(-1/2,-c-1)
, which is a valid endpoint of a path
\tilde{\pi}
in the support of
Q^{edge-\Gamma}_{n}
.

Furthermore,

(6.35)

\prod_{i=1}^{c}\frac{U_{i-1,n-i+1}}{V_{i,n-i}}=\prod_{j=1}^{-(-c-1)-1}\frac{a_{j}}{b_{j}}

in distribution, in fact in joint distribution as
c
varies over
[0,n]
, since
U_{i-1,n-i+1}=b_{i}^{-1}
and
V_{i,n-i}=a_{i}^{-1}
in distribution and all these variables are independent. Hence the boundary weights of the paths in the two models, identified via
f
, are equal in distribution. Additionally, the bulk weights

\{Y_{i,j}:(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},i+j>n,i\leq n,j\leq n\}

are equal in joint distribution to

\{\gamma_{x,y}:(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},x\geq 0,y\leq-1,y-x\geq-n\}

with the index sets identified by
f(i,j)=(x,y)
. Hence paths
\hat{\pi}
and
f(\hat{\pi})
have weights which are the same in distribution, and this is true for the joint distribution over all paths
\hat{\pi}
. This is exactly the statement that the two random measures
\upsilon_{*}(Q^{stat-\Gamma}_{n,n})
and
Q^{edge-\Gamma}_{n}
are equal in distribution as random measures. In particular, the marginal distributions of their endpoints are equal. Since

(6.36)

x_{mid}(\pi)=-Y(f\circ\upsilon(\pi))-1

for any
\pi
, this yields the desired claim.

∎

Remark 6.4
.

The above proof yields not just a distributional matching between
Y(\tilde{\pi})
and
y_{mid}(\pi)
, given in
Lemma

6.6
, but a full distributional matching the whole path
\tilde{\pi}
and the portion of the path
\pi
lying above the line
x+y=n
, after an appropriate shear transformation
f
. In particular, courtesy of
5.1
, this means that the evolution of the West turning point under shuffling is exactly described by a stationary log-Gamma polymer path.

Proof of
1.4
.

Combine
Lemma

6.4
with
Lemma

6.6
(with homogeneous weights
\psi_{j}\equiv\alpha,\phi_{j}\equiv\beta,\theta_{i}\equiv 0
).
∎

Remark 6.5
.

Barraquand-Le Doussal
[
4
]
give a
k
-path generalization of the stationary polymer model in
6.2
, which should similarly have a distributional match along antidiagonals with the
k^{th}
slice from the left of a Gamma-disordered Aztec diamond, generalizing
1.4
; this will be treated in upcoming work. However, probabilistic asymptotics on path trajectories like the ones in
[
74
]
do not seem to be worked out yet in the literature for this multi-path model. Hence, at the moment, this match does not appear to buy any asymptotic information about the Aztec diamond.

7.
Horizontal slices and the
\Gamma\log\Gamma
polymer

This section contains the analogues of Sections
4
,
5
, and
6
, except that we slice the Aztec diamond horizontally rather than vertically. This results in a different notion of
(+)
- and
(-)
-column and a matching with a different polymer model. We call it the
\Gamma\log\Gamma
polymer since it consists of a strict-weak (also known as Gamma) polymer grafted to a log-Gamma polymer along the boundary.

Most arguments for these results are quite similar to those for the corresponding results with vertical slices. Because of the similarity, we will be somewhat more terse in this section, and recommend the reader to have finished the previous ones mentioned before starting this one.

7.1.
Definition of
\Gamma\log\Gamma
polymer and statement of matching

Definition 7.1
.

Let
p,m\geq 1
be integers, and let
(\psi_{j})_{1\leq j\leq m},(\phi_{j})_{1-p\leq j\leq 0},(\theta_{i})_{1\leq i\leq m+p}
be parameters such that all sums
\psi_{j}+\theta_{i}
and
\phi_{j}-\theta_{i}
are positive.

Consider the weighted, directed graph
G^{\Gamma\log\Gamma}_{p,m}
with the same underlying graph as
G^{\beta\Gamma}_{p,m}
from
4.5
(see
Figure

22
(bottom)), which we recall means that all vertices lie in the set

(7.1)

\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:-m\leq x\leq-1,-m-p\leq y\leq-1,x+y\geq-m-p\}\\
\cup\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:-m-p\leq y\leq-1,0\leq x\leq\min(p-1,y+m+p)\}.

Define the edges and their weights as follows

(1)

For each vertex at
(x,y)
with
x\leq-1
, there are right and down-right directed edges to vertices
(x+1,y)
and
(x+1,y-1)
, with weights
\rho_{x,y}
and
1
respectively, where
\rho_{x,y}\sim\Gamma(\psi_{m+x+1}+\theta_{-y},1)
are independent Gamma variables.

(2)

For each vertex at
(x,y)
with
x\geq 0
lying in the set (
7.1
), there are down and right directed edges to vertices
(x,y-1)
and
(x+1,y)
(if they also lie in the set (
7.1
)). Both edges have the same weight
\kappa_{x,y}\sim\Gamma^{-1}(\phi_{1-p+x}-\theta_{-y})
, where the variables
\kappa_{x,y}
are independent from each other and from the
\rho_{x,y}
variables.

Then the associated
\Gamma\log\Gamma
polymer partition function
is

(7.2)

Z^{\Gamma\log\Gamma}_{p,m}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi_{j}:(-m,-j)\to(p-j,-m-j)\\
1\leq j\leq p\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{p}\wt(\pi_{j}),

where the sum is over
p
-tuples of nonintersecting
15
15

15

Meaning that the vertex sets of any two paths, not just the edge sets, are disjoint.
paths
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{p}
on
G^{\Gamma\log\Gamma}_{p,m}
, where
\pi_{j}
has start point
(-m,-j)
and end point
(p-j,-m-j)
, and
\wt(\pi_{j})
denotes the product of edge weights over the edges in
\pi_{j}
.

The associated
\Gamma\log\Gamma
polymer measure
is a probability measure on such
p
-tuples
(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{p})
of nonintersecting paths which assigns to each one the probability

(7.3)

\frac{1}{Z^{\Gamma\log\Gamma}_{p,m}}\prod_{j=1}^{p}\wt(\pi_{j}).

Note that because the weights on both outgoing edges from any vertex in the right half of the polymer are equal, one may view these weights as associated to the vertex rather than the edges. This polymer environment is then a multi-parameter generalization of that of the log-Gamma polymer of
[
74
]
, recalled in
6.2
; such deformed log-Gamma polymers arise from Whittaker processes, see
[
10
, Definition 5.16]
.

Remark 7.1
.

Recall that
4.6
defined a point configuration
X^{poly}(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{p})
in terms of the intersections of the paths
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{p}
with the
y
-axis in
G^{\beta\Gamma}_{p,m}
. Since the underlying graph is the same as
G^{\Gamma\log\Gamma}_{p,m}
, we will freely use
X^{poly}(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{p})
for paths
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{p}
on
G^{\Gamma\log\Gamma}_{p,m}
as well. We will similarly use
\Pi(0),\ldots,\Pi(m)
as in
5.1
.

This time, we will match
X^{poly}
with horizontal slices of the Aztec diamond, which we denote with similar notation to
X_{\ell}^{\Az}
before (cf.
4.7
):

Definition 7.2
.

For an Aztec diamond
G_{n}^{\Az}
of size
n
, let
\mathcal{M}
be the set of perfect matchings of
G_{n}^{\Az}
. For any
1\leq\ell\leq n
, define functions

(7.4)

Y_{\ell}^{\Az}:\mathcal{M}\to\binom{[n+1]}{n-\ell+1}

as follows. There are
n
rows of black vertices in
G_{n}^{\Az}
; consider the
\ell^{th}
one from top to bottom. For each perfect matching
M\in\mathcal{M}
, there will be exactly
n-\ell+1
black vertices in the
\ell^{th}
row which are matched to a white vertex above them (either up-right or up-left). Labeling the black vertices in this row by
1,\ldots,n+1
from left to right, we let
Y_{\ell}^{\Az}(M)
be the set of labels of the black vertices which are matched with a white vertex above them (again, either up-right or up-left).

Theorem 7.1
.

Fix
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and let
(\phi_{j-n})_{1\leq j\leq n},(\psi_{j})_{1\leq j\leq n},(\theta_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
be real parameters such that
\psi_{j}+\theta_{i}
and
\phi_{j-n}-\theta_{i}
are positive for each
1\leq i,j\leq n
. Consider a Gamma-disordered Aztec diamond with these parameters (
1.7
), and let
M
be a random perfect matching distributed by the corresponding dimer measure.

Consider also an independent
\Gamma\log\Gamma
polymer on
G^{\Gamma\log\Gamma}_{p,m}
with
p=n-\ell+1
paths and
m=\ell
layers in the notation of
7.1
, and the same parameters
\phi_{j-n},\psi_{j},\theta_{i}
. Let
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{p}
be paths (ordered from top to bottom as in
7.1
) distributed according to the corresponding polymer measure. Then

(7.5)

Y_{\ell}^{\Az}(M)=X^{poly}(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{p})\quad\quad\quad\quad\text{ in distribution.}

Furthermore, this distributional equality holds for the dimer measure associated to any fixed realization of the Aztec weights, if the polymer weights of the corresponding polymer measure are given in terms of them via

(7.6)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\rho_{x,y}&\longleftrightarrow a_{-y,\ell+x+1}^{[n-\ell+(\ell+x+1)]}=a_{-y,\ell+x+1}^{[n+x+1]}\\
\kappa_{x,y}&\longleftrightarrow 1/b_{-y,\ell}^{[n-x]}.\end{split}

Theorem 7.2
.

Let
(\phi_{j})_{j\leq 0},(\psi_{j})_{j\geq 1},(\theta_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be real parameters such that
\psi_{j}+\theta_{i}
and
\phi_{j}-\theta_{i}
are positive for all
i,j
.
Sample a coupled sequence of matchings
M_{1},M_{2},\ldots
of the Aztec diamonds of size
1,2,\ldots
according to the shuffling algorithm with these parameters (see
Section

5.1
).

Then the ‘
k^{th}
row from the top’ stochastic process
Y^{\Az}_{\tau}(M_{\tau+k-1}),\tau\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, where
Y_{\tau}^{\Az}
is as in
7.2
, has the following description. Fix any ‘final time’
T>0
, and let
G_{k,T}^{\Gamma\log\Gamma}
be a
\Gamma\log\Gamma
polymer with weights given in terms of the Aztec weights via

(7.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\rho_{x,y}&=a_{-y,k+x+1}^{[T+k+x]}\\
\kappa_{x,y}&=1/b_{-y,k}^{[T+k-1-x]}.\end{split}

Let
(\Pi(\tau))_{0\leq\tau\leq T}
be the joint polymer path trajectories as in
5.1
. Then

(7.8)

(Y^{\Az}_{\tau}(M_{\tau+k-1}))_{0\leq\tau\leq T}=(\Pi(\tau))_{0\leq\tau\leq T}

in distribution, for any fixed realization of the weights
a_{i,j}^{[n]},b_{i,j}^{[n]}
.

7.2.
Proofs

Section

Proof.

Exactly analogous to the proof of
Lemma

4.2
. It is easiest to see by first gauge-transforming by each
b_{i,j}^{[m]}
at the white vertices of the
(-^{\prime})
-columns, so all nontrivial edge weights are either
b_{i,j}^{[m]}
or
a_{i,j}^{[m]}
for some
i
. Now apply urban renewal (
2.2
) inside each square. As before, the top and bottom of the graph have
1
-valent vertices (this time black ones), so remove these and the edges incident to them. Also, contract the
1
-valent vertices joining those of the new squares which are above one another via
2.3
. Each of the new squares has weights

(7.9)

\frac{1}{a_{i,j}^{[m]}+b_{i,j}^{[m]}},\frac{a_{i,j}^{[m]}}{a_{i,j}^{[m]}+b_{i,j}^{[m]}},\frac{b_{i,j}^{[m]}}{a_{i,j}^{[m]}+b_{i,j}^{[m]}},\frac{1}{a_{i,j}^{[m]}+b_{i,j}^{[m]}}

at its North, East, South and West faces respectively, for some
i
. Gauge transform by
a_{i,j}^{[m]}+b_{i,j}^{[m]}
at the upper white vertex of each such square. After doing this at each square, each one has weights

(7.10)

1,a_{i,j}^{[m-1]},b_{i,j-1}^{[m-1]},1

ordered as before. Contract all
2
-valent white vertices on the sides to single black vertices, and expand each white vertex in each square horizontally. Finally, gauge transform by
1/b_{i,j-1}^{[m-1]}
at each white vertex which is incident to an edge of weight
b_{i,j-1}^{[m-1]}
. The resulting graph is
G^{\prime}
, and the distributional equality statements in
2.2
and
2.3
imply the desired one.
∎

and

and

are as follows.

.

Definition 7.6
.

For any
n\geq\ell\geq 1
, let
\bar{G}^{h-swap}_{n,\ell}
be the graph formed by removing both columns of
2
-valent white vertices from
