Proof of
7.1
.

The proof is exactly analogous to that of
4.10
. First, we may define
Y_{\ell}^{\Az}(M)
for matchings
M
on
G_{n}^{hor}
rather than
G_{n}^{\Az}
, as the
\ell^{th}
column of squares from the left on the former corresponds to the
\ell^{th}
row of
n
squares from the top in the latter after reflection over the diagonal from top-left to bottom-right and vertex-dilation. Then,
Lemma

7.3
allows us to swap the
(+^{\prime})
and
(-^{\prime})
columns to the left and right of the
\ell^{th}
column, while keeping the distribution of edges in the
\ell^{th}
column invariant. This gives a distributional equality between the edges on the
\ell^{th}
column of squares in
G_{n}^{hor}
and the only column of squares in
G^{h-swap}_{n,\ell}
, analogous to
4.4
. Any dimer cover of
G^{h-swap}_{n,\ell}
must have the same configuration of all horizontal edges in the first block of
(-^{\prime})
and second block of
(+^{\prime})
columns;
Lemma

4.5
showed this for
G^{v-swap}_{n,\ell}
, which is the same underlying graph as
G^{h-swap}_{n,\ell}
with different weights. This gives a distributional equality with the column of squares in
\bar{G}^{h-swap}_{n,\ell}
. Dimer covers of
\bar{G}^{h-swap}_{n,\ell}
are in bijection with path configurations
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{p}
of
G^{\Gamma\log\Gamma}_{n-\ell+1,\ell}
: the bijection was given for the same underlying graph and digraph in
Lemma

4.8
.

It remains to check that the parameters of the weights match up. The series of
\ell

(+^{\prime})
-columns in the left half of
\bar{G}^{h-swap}_{n,\ell}
have weights such that the
j^{th}
column (starting from
j=1
at the left) has weights
a_{i,j}^{[n-\ell+j]},1\leq i\leq n-\ell+j
, enumerated top to bottom. On the right half of
\bar{G}^{h-swap}_{n,\ell}
we have a series of
n-\ell+1

(-^{\prime})
-columns, where the
j^{th}
column (starting from
j=1
at the left) has weights
1/b_{i,\ell}^{[n-j+1]},1\leq i\leq n-j+1
. Hence the weights on
G^{\Gamma\log\Gamma}_{n-\ell+1,\ell}
correspond to those on
\bar{G}^{h-swap}_{n,\ell}
via

(7.11)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\rho_{x,y}&\longleftrightarrow a_{-y,\ell+x+1}^{[n-\ell+(\ell+x+1)]}=a_{-y,\ell+x+1}^{[n+x+1]}\\
\kappa_{x,y}&\longleftrightarrow 1/b_{-y,\ell}^{[n-x]}\end{split}

These have distribution

(7.12)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{-y,\ell+x+1}^{[n+x+1]}&\sim\Gamma(\psi_{\ell+x+1}+\theta_{-y},1)\\
b_{-y,\ell}^{[n-x]}&\sim\Gamma(\phi_{x+\ell-n}-\theta_{-y},1).\end{split}

Taking
m=\ell,p=n-\ell+1
, this yields the distributions in
7.1
and completes the proof.

∎

Proof of
7.2
.

Exactly analogous to the proof of
5.1
in
Section

5
, now that we have proven the single-time matching
7.1
.
∎

7.3.
Matching North and South turning points with stationary strict-weak polymer

We will actually only treat the South turning point, as the North is the same by flipping the Aztec diamond around the horizontal axis and interchanging the
a
and
b
variables.

The stationary strict-weak polymer, also known as the Gamma polymer, was introduced in
[
26
]
. We will instead follow the notation of the later work
[
21
]
, partly to keep notation similar to what we had for the stationary log-Gamma polymer, and partly because we will use probabilistic results from
[
21
]
as input. The Burke property given in this section was proven in
[
26
]
, but we will use the formulation in
[
21
]
.

Definition 7.7
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>0
. On every up-right directed edge
(i,j)\to(i+1,j)
or
(i,j)\to(i,j+1)
with
i,j\geq 0
, place an independent random weight with distributions as follows. Boundary vertical edges
e=(0,j)\to(0,j+1)
have weights
\wt(e)=R_{j}\sim\mathrm{Beta}^{-1}(\beta,\alpha)
, while all other vertical edges have weights
1
. Horizontal edges
e=(i,j)\to(i+1,j)
have weights

(7.13)

\wt(e)=Y_{i,j}\sim\begin{cases}\Gamma(\alpha,1)&j\geq 1\\
\Gamma(\alpha+\beta,1)&j=0\end{cases}.

These are known as
stationary strict-weak polymer
weights.

Let
\Pi_{m,n}
be the set of up-right paths with steps
(i,j)\to(i+1,j)
and
(i,j)\to(i,j+1)
, beginning at
(0,0)
and ending at
(m,n)
. Then the associated partition function is

(7.14)

Z^{SW}_{m,n}:=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{m,n}}\prod_{e\in\pi}\wt(e),

where the product is over the weights on all lattice edges in the path. The associated
quenched
polymer measure on
\Pi_{m,n}
is

(7.15)

Q^{SW}_{m,n}(\pi)=\frac{\prod_{e\in\pi}\wt(e)}{Z^{SW}_{m,n}},

a random probability measure depending on the weights. The associated
annealed
polymer measure on
\Pi_{m,n}
is

(7.16)

\mathbb{P}_{m,n}^{SW}(\pi)=\mathbb{E}[Q^{SW}_{m,n}(\pi)].

Proposition 7.4

(Burke property)
.

In the notation of
7.7
,
let

(7.17)

\displaystyle\begin{split}U_{m,n}&=\frac{Z^{SW}_{m,n}}{Z^{SW}_{m-1,n}}\\
V_{m,n}&=\frac{Z^{SW}_{m,n}}{Z^{SW}_{m,n-1}}\end{split}

whenever
m\geq 1,n\geq 0
(for
U_{m,n}
) or
m\geq 0,n\geq 1
(for
V_{m,n}
) so both partition functions are defined. Then

(7.18)

\displaystyle\begin{split}U_{m,n}&\sim\Gamma(\alpha+\beta,1)\\
V_{m,n}&\sim\mathrm{Beta}^{-1}(\beta,\alpha)\end{split}

for every
(m,n)
. Furthermore, associating
U_{m,n}
and
V_{m,n}
to the directed horizontal edge
(m-1,n)\to(m,n)
and vertical edge
(m,n)\to(m,n-1)
respectively, we have that for any down-right path in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
, the
U
and
V
variables associated to its right and down segments are all mutually independent.

Proof.

Checked in
[
26
, Lemma 6.3]
in slightly different coordinates, see
[
21
, Proposition 2.3]
for the statement in our coordinates. Our
\alpha,\beta
correspond to
\theta
and
\mu-\theta
in the notation of
[
21
]
.
∎

We will use the notation
x_{mid}
of
6.3
for strict-weak polymer paths as well as for log-Gamma, since the path set is the same. Finally, we can state the desired matching.

Theorem 7.5
.

Fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha,\beta>0
. Let
M
be a perfect matching of the Gamma-disordered Aztec diamond
G_{n}^{\Az}
with homogeneous parameters

(7.19)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\theta_{i}&\equiv 0\\
\psi_{j}&\equiv\alpha\\
\phi_{j}&\equiv\beta.\end{split}

Let
\pi
be distributed according to the polymer measure
Q^{SW}_{n,n}
of an independent stationary strict-weak polymer with the same parameters
\alpha,\beta
. Then

(7.20)

T^{South}(M)=x_{mid}(\pi)\quad\quad\quad\quad\text{in (annealed) distribution}

where
T^{South}(M)
is as in
1.2
.

Proof.

The proof of
7.5
is essentially the same as that of
1.4
, except that we do not have to carry out the path-complementation bijection of
Lemma

6.4
first. The basic idea is that the partition function ratios along the antidiagonal
y=n-x
of the stationary strict-weak polymer correspond to the variables
\kappa_{i,j}
in the log-Gamma portion of the
\Gamma\log\Gamma
polymer, while the weights above it correspond to the variables
\rho_{i,j}
of the
\Gamma
portion.

The exact analogue of
Lemma

6.5
for the stationary strict-weak polymer follows directly from
7.4
. Namely,

(7.21)

Z^{SW}_{i+1,n-i-1}=\frac{U_{i,n-i}}{V_{i+1,n-i-1}}\cdot Z^{SW}_{i,n-i},

where

(7.22)

\displaystyle\begin{split}U_{i,n-i}&\sim\Gamma(\alpha+\beta,1)\\
V_{i,n-i}&\sim\mathrm{Beta}^{-1}(\beta,\alpha)\end{split}

are independent. Hence

(7.23)

Z^{SW}_{n-i,i}=\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}\frac{V_{n-j,j}}{U_{n-j-1,j+1}}Z^{SW}_{n,0}.

Furthermore, from the ‘in this case’ part of
2.6
,

(7.24)

\frac{V_{n-j,j}}{U_{n-j-1,j+1}}\sim\Gamma^{-1}(\beta,1).

By
7.1
,

(7.25)

Y_{n}^{\Az}(M)=X^{poly}(\pi)\quad\quad\quad\quad\text{in (annealed) distribution}

where
\pi
is a polymer path on
G_{1,n}^{\Gamma\log\Gamma}
. Both sides are singleton sets, and it is clear from comparing
7.2
with
1.2
that
Y_{n}^{\Az}(M)=\{T_{South}(M)+1\}
.

The remainder of the proof is the same as that of
Lemma

6.6
, and we simply give a sketch. The partition function ratios of (
7.24
) are equal in distribution to the weights
\kappa_{0,y}\sim\Gamma^{-1}(\phi_{0})
of
7.1
, since we have set
\phi_{j}\equiv\beta
. Additionally, the weights
1
and
Y_{\ldots}
on the vertical and horizontal edges of
7.7
are equal in distribution to the weights
\rho_{\ldots}\sim\Gamma(\alpha,1)
of
7.1
, since we have set
\psi_{j}\equiv\alpha
. The portions of paths in
\Pi_{n,n}
lying above the antidiagonal
y=n-x
are in bijection with paths on
G^{\Gamma\log\Gamma}_{1,n}
, and this bijection is weight-preserving and takes
X^{poly}(\pi)
to
\{x_{mid}(\pi)+1\}
.
∎

8.
Fluctuations of polymer paths and turning points

Having made exact matchings between the turning points of the Gamma-disordered Aztec diamond and known polymer models in Theorems
1.4
,
6.1
, and
7.5
, in this section we will use known theorems for these polymer models to establish our asymptotics on the turning points. The proofs for the North and South turning points are identical, and also essentially identical to those for the West turning point, while the East turning point has different asymptotics. We analyze the East turning point in
Section

8.1
, analyze the West turning point in
Section

8.2
, and explain the few minor changes to this argument needed for the North and South turning points in
Section

8.3
.

8.1.
East

Theorem 8.1
.

Fix
\alpha,\beta>0
and set parameters
\psi_{j}\equiv\alpha T,\phi_{j}\equiv\beta T,\theta_{i}\equiv 0
in the Aztec diamond weights. Sample a coupled sequence of matchings
M_{1},M_{2},\ldots
according to the shuffling algorithm with these parameters (see
Section

5.1
).

For each
n
, define the continuous time random walk

(8.1)

B_{n}(t)=\frac{T^{East}(M_{\lfloor nt\rfloor})-\frac{\beta}{\alpha+\beta}\lfloor nt\rfloor}{\sqrt{n}}

where
T^{East}
is as in
1.2
. Then for almost every realization of the weights, the law of
B_{n}(t),t\in[0,1]
converges as
n\to\infty
to
\frac{\sqrt{\alpha\beta}}{\alpha+\beta}B_{t},t\in[0,1]
, where
B_{t}
is a standard Brownian motion, and convergence is with respect to the Skorokhod topology. In particular, for almost every realization of the weights,

(8.2)

\frac{T^{East}(M_{n})-\frac{\beta}{\alpha+\beta}\cdot n}{\sqrt{n}}\to\mathcal{N}\left(0,\frac{\alpha\beta}{(\alpha+\beta)^{2}}\right)\quad\quad\quad\quad\text{ in distribution as $n\to\infty$.}

Proof.

By
6.1
,

(8.3)

T^{East}(M_{\lfloor nt\rfloor})=-X_{\lfloor nt\rfloor}-1

in joint distribution, where
X_{\tau},\tau\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is a Beta-RWRE. A quenched invariance principle for almost every environment for the Beta-RWRE is already proven as a consequence of the general result
[
71
, Theorem 1]
, so we will just explain how to check the hypotheses of that result in our specific case. In the notation there (see
[
71
, page 300]
), we have have an iid product-type random environment with

•

d=2

•

\pi_{0,e_{1}+z}=\begin{cases}\frac{a_{1,1}^{[1]}}{a_{1,1}^{[1]}+b_{1,1}^{[1]}}&z=0\\
\frac{b_{1,1}^{[1]}}{a_{1,1}^{[1]}+b_{1,1}^{[1]}}&z=-e_{2}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

•

p(0,z):=\mathbb{E}[\pi_{0,e_{1}+z}]=\begin{cases}\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+\beta}&z=0\\
\frac{\beta}{\alpha+\beta}&z=-e_{2}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

•

v=e_{1}-\frac{\beta}{\alpha+\beta}e_{2}

•

\mathfrak{D}:=\sum_{z\in\{0,-e_{2}\}}(e_{1}+z-v)(e_{1}+z-v)^{T}p(0,z)=\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+\beta}\frac{\beta}{\alpha+\beta}\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}

The Hypothesis (ME) needed for
[
71
, Theorem 1]
is trivially checked, since

(8.4)

\sum_{z}|z|^{2}\mathbb{E}[\pi_{0,e_{1}+z}]=\frac{\beta}{\alpha+\beta}<\infty

and

(8.5)

\mathbb{P}(\sup_{z}\pi_{0,e_{1}+z}<1)=\Pr\left(\max\left\{\frac{a_{1,1}^{[1]}}{a_{1,1}^{[1]}+b_{1,1}^{[1]}},\frac{b_{1,1}^{[1]}}{a_{1,1}^{[1]}+b_{1,1}^{[1]}}\right\}<1\right)=1>0.

This shows Skorokhod convergence. Since a standard Brownian motion is continuous almost surely, we have, for any fixed
t\in[0,1]
, that point evaluation
\pi_{t}:f\mapsto f(t)
is continuous at
B_{t}
in the Skorokhod topology almost surely
[
7
, Thm 12.5]
. Then by the Mapping Theorem
[
7
, Thm 2.7]
we also have that, for any fixed
t\in[0,1]
, the variable
B_{n}(t)
converges in law to
B(t)
. Applying this to
t=1
yields (
8.2
).
∎

8.2.
West

Recall the stationary log-Gamma polymer of
6.2
. By
1.4
, we wish to consider the intersection point
x_{mid}(\pi)
of a path in this polymer with the antidiagonal
x+y=n
. Specifically, we will show in the following two results that (1)
x_{mid}(\pi)
lies within a large enough
n^{2/3}
-size interval with high probability, and (2)
x_{mid}(\pi)
lies outside an
n^{2/3}
-size interval with non-negligible probability. Together, these show that
x_{mid}(\pi)
fluctuates on the scale
n^{2/3}
.

Proposition 8.2

(Tail bounds on the
n^{2/3}
scale)
.

Fix parameters
0<\alpha,\beta<\infty
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
let
\pi_{n}
be a path distributed by the random polymer measure
Q^{stat-\Gamma}_{n,n}
of
6.2
with these parameters, and let
x_{mid}(\pi_{n})
be as in
6.3
. Then there exist constants
b_{0},C
depending on
\alpha,\beta
such that for every
b\geq b_{0}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

(8.6)

\mathbb{P}^{stat-\Gamma}_{n,n}\left(\left\lvert x_{mid}(\pi_{n})-\frac{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha)+\Psi_{1}(\beta)}\cdot n\right\rvert\geq bn^{2/3}\right)\leq\frac{C}{b^{3}}.

Here
\Psi_{1}
is the trigamma function.

Proposition 8.3

(Anticoncentration on the
n^{2/3}
scale)
.

Assume the same setup as
8.2
. Then there exist constants
n_{0},C_{0},C_{1}>0
depending on
\alpha,\beta
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
,

(8.7)

\mathbb{P}^{stat-\Gamma}_{n,n}\left(\left\lvert x_{mid}(\pi_{n})-\frac{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha)+\Psi_{1}(\beta)}\cdot n\right\rvert>C_{1}n^{2/3}\right)\geq C_{0}.

As before,
\Psi_{1}
is the trigamma function.

Remark 8.1
.

It is instructive to consider extreme cases of the macroscopic location
\frac{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha)+\Psi_{1}(\beta)}\cdot n
of the turning points. Since the trigamma function is decreasing, when
\alpha\gg\beta
(so the
a_{i,j}^{[n]}
weights are much larger than the
b_{i,j}^{[n]}
weights, we have
\frac{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha)+\Psi_{1}(\beta)}\approx 1
and the turning point is essentially in the Southwest corner of the Aztec diamond. Similarly, if
\alpha\ll\beta
the turning point is essentially in the Northwest corner. If
\alpha=T\bar{\alpha}
and
\beta=T\bar{\beta}
, then
\Psi_{1}(T\bar{\alpha})\approx\tfrac{1}{T\bar{\alpha}}
for large
T
by (
9.4
), and consequently

(8.8)

\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{\Psi_{1}(T\bar{\beta})}{\Psi_{1}(T\bar{\alpha})+\Psi_{1}(T\bar{\beta})}=\frac{\bar{\alpha}}{\bar{\alpha}+\bar{\beta}}.

This corresponds to the deterministic limit
a_{i,j}^{[n]}=\bar{\alpha},b_{i,j}^{[n]}=\bar{\beta}
of our weights.

The hard work needed for these estimates is contained in
[
74
]
and
[
21
]
, but some translation is needed since those works study a different quantity. They do not study the intersection with the antidiagonal, but rather intersections with certain vertical and horizontal lines which we define now.

Definition 8.1
.

Given a path
\pi\in\Pi_{m,n}
in the notation of
6.2
, and
0\leq l\leq n,0\leq k\leq m
, we define

(8.9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}v_{0}(l)&:=\min\{i:(i,l)\in\pi\}\\
v_{1}(l)&:=\max\{i:(i,l)\in\pi\}\end{split}

and

(8.10)

\displaystyle\begin{split}w_{0}(k)&:=\min\{j:(k,j)\in\pi\}\\
w_{1}(k)&:=\max\{j:(k,j)\in\pi\}.\end{split}

Note that the dependence on
\pi
is implicit in the notation.

k
l
v_{1}(l)
v_{0}(l)
m
w_{0}(k)
w_{1}(k)
n

Figure 34
.
Example path with
v_{0},v_{1},w_{0},w_{1}
illustrated. Reproduced from
[
21
, Figure 3]
.

We will use the following theorem on the fluctuation exponents of these quantities.

Theorem 8.4

(Tail bounds and anticoncentration on the
n^{2/3}
scale)
.

Recall the setup of
6.2
and fix the parameters
\alpha,\beta
. For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let

(8.11)

m_{N}=\left\lfloor\frac{\Psi_{1}(\alpha)}{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}N\right\rfloor

where
\Psi_{1}
is the trigamma function. Then there exist positive constants
b_{0},C,c_{0},c_{1},N_{0}
depending only on the parameters
\alpha,\beta
and on
\tau
, such that for all
b\geq b_{0}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

(8.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{m_{N},N}^{stat-\Gamma}(v_{0}(\lfloor\tau N\rfloor)\leq\tau m_{N}-bN^{2/3}\text{ or }v_{1}(\lfloor\tau N\rfloor)\geq\tau m_{N}+bN^{2/3})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{b^{3}}

(8.13)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{m_{N},N}^{stat-\Gamma}(w_{0}(\lfloor\tau m_{N}\rfloor)\leq\tau N-bN^{2/3}\text{ or }w_{1}(\lfloor\tau m_{N}\rfloor)\geq\tau N+bN^{2/3})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{b^{3}}

and for all
N\geq N_{0}
,

(8.14)

\mathbb{P}_{m_{N},N}^{stat-\Gamma}(v_{1}(\lfloor\tau N\rfloor)\geq\tau m_{N}+c_{1}N^{2/3}\text{ or }w_{1}(\tau m_{N})\geq\tau N+c_{1}N^{2/3})\geq c_{0}.

Furthermore, for any compact set
S\subset[0,1)
, the constants
b_{0},C,c_{0},c_{1},N_{0}
may be chosen uniformly over all
\tau\in S
.

Proof.

This is a special case of the log-Gamma case of
[
21
, Theorem 1.5]
, where we recall that our
\alpha,\beta
correspond to
\theta,\mu-\theta
in their notation. Specifically, one must take the parameters
N,n_{N},m_{N}
in that result to be
\lfloor N/\Psi_{1}(\beta)\rfloor
,
N
and
\lfloor N\Psi_{1}(\alpha)/\Psi_{1}(\beta)\rfloor
in terms of our variables. Note that the fact that our
N
is a constant times the one in
[
21
, Theorem 1.5]
changes the constants
c_{1},b,C
in that result by a constant, but this does not change the result.

One must then verify that our
n_{N}
and
m_{N}
satisfy
[
21
, (1.10)]
. In our notation, this condition translates to the bounds

(8.15)

\left\lvert\left\lfloor\frac{\Psi_{1}(\alpha)}{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}N\right\rfloor-\left\lfloor\frac{N}{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}\right\rfloor\cdot\Var(\log Y_{0,j})\right\rvert\leq\gamma N^{2/3}

and

(8.16)

\left\lvert N-\left\lfloor\frac{N}{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}\right\rfloor\cdot\Var(\log Y_{j,0})\right\rvert\leq\gamma N^{2/3}

holding for all
N
, for some constant
\gamma
. These bounds follow directly from the formula

(8.17)

\Var\log X=\Psi_{1}(\chi)

when
X\sim\Gamma^{-1}(\chi,1)
, and the fact that
Y_{0,j}\sim\Gamma^{-1}(\alpha,1)
and
Y_{j,0}\sim\Gamma^{-1}(\beta,1)
in
6.2
. The quantities we wish to bound by
\gamma N^{2/3}
are actually just
\mathcal{O}(1)
error terms coming from different placement of floor functions in the two terms being compared.
∎

Essentially the same theorem, but without the uniformity in
\tau
(which we need), was proven first in
[
74
, Theorem 2.3]
. We note that
[
21
, Theorem 1.5]
also proves uniformity with respect to the Gamma parameters
\alpha,\beta
, though we do not need this.

To relate
v_{i}
to
x_{mid}
for
8.2
, we use a basic deterministic fact:

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
1\leq n\leq m
and let
\pi\in\Pi_{m,n}
be any path, let
0\leq\ell\leq n
, and let
x_{mid}(\pi)
and
v_{i}(\ell)
be as in
6.3
and
8.1
respectively. Then

(8.18)

|x_{mid}(\pi)-(n-\ell)|\leq\max\{v_{1}(\ell)-(n-\ell),(n-\ell)-v_{0}(\ell)\}

Proof.

The segment between
(v_{0}(\ell),\ell)
and
(v_{1}(\ell),\ell)
either lies to the left of the line
x+y=n
, to the right of the line, or intersects the line. In other words, these three cases correspond to the conditions

(1)

v_{1}(\ell)\leq n-\ell

(2)

v_{0}(\ell)\geq n-\ell

(3)

v_{0}(\ell)\leq n-\ell\leq v_{1}(\ell)

respectively.

In the first case, we additionally have

(8.19)

v_{1}(\ell)\leq x_{mid}(\pi)\leq n-\ell

because the path
\pi
moves only up and right from the point
(v_{1}(\ell),\ell)
so the
x
-coordinate can only increase before it hits the line
x+y=n
. Similarly, in the second case

(8.20)

n-\ell\leq x_{mid}(\pi)\leq v_{0}(\ell).

In the third case, since
\pi
passes horizontally between
(v_{0}(\ell),\ell)
and
(v_{1}(\ell),\ell)
, its intersection with the line
x+y=n
occurs at
(n-\ell,\ell)
, and so
x_{mid}(\pi)=n-\ell
.

In each case, (
8.18
) clearly holds.
∎

Proof of
8.2
.

We note that interchanging
\alpha
with
\beta
and simultaneously interchanging the
x
and
y
axes in
6.2
yields distributionally equal weights, since the boundary weights are interchanged by both transformations. This transformation also replaces

(8.21)

\displaystyle x_{mid}(\pi_{n})

\displaystyle\mapsto n-x_{mid}(\pi_{n})

(8.22)

\displaystyle\frac{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha)+\Psi_{1}(\beta)}

\displaystyle\mapsto 1-\frac{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha)+\Psi_{1}(\beta)}.

Hence we may assume without loss of generality that
\Psi_{1}(\alpha)\geq\Psi_{1}(\beta)
, since the case
\Psi_{1}(\alpha)<\Psi_{1}(\beta)
of
8.2
follows from this case by symmetry.

For each
n
, let
N_{n}
be an integer such that

(8.23)

n=\left\lfloor\frac{\Psi_{1}(\alpha)}{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}N_{n}\right\rfloor.

By our assumption
\Psi_{1}(\alpha)\geq\Psi_{1}(\beta)
, we may always take
N_{n}\leq n
.

Let
\pi_{n}
be as in the statement. We first relate it to a path
\pi_{n}^{\prime}
in a smaller
n\times N_{n}
rectangular box, on which
8.4
applies, see
Figure

35
. Rather than
x_{mid}(\pi_{n})
, we will consider
\min(x_{mid}(\pi_{n}),N_{n})
for now, and deal with the event
\min(x_{mid}(\pi_{n}),N_{n})\neq x_{mid}(\pi_{n})
later.

We next claim that as a deterministic fact, for any path
\pi_{n}
, the function of
\pi_{n}
given by
\min(x_{mid}(\pi_{n}),N_{n})
depends only on the intersection
\pi_{n}\cap([1,n]\times[n-N_{n}+1,n])
. We have that
x_{mid}(\pi_{n})
lies in
[0,N_{n}-1]
if and only if the intersection of
\pi_{n}
with the line
x+y=n
lies inside the box
[0,n]\times[n-N_{n}+1,n]
(see
Figure

35
). In the other case,
x_{mid}(\pi_{n})\geq N_{n}
,
\pi_{n}
must hit the line
x+y=n
at a point outside this box, and hence will enter the box at a point with
x
-coordinate
\geq N_{n}
. Hence
\min(x_{mid}(\pi_{n}),N_{n})
depends only on the part of the path
\pi_{n}\cap([0,n]\times[n-N_{n}+1,n])
lying inside this box. In fact, it depends only on
\pi_{n}\cap([1,n]\times[n-N_{n}+1,n])
, since
\pi_{n}
passes through
(0,n)
if and only if it passes through
(1,n)
but not
(1,n-1)
, and the latter event depends only on
\pi_{n}\cap([1,n]\times[n-N_{n}+1,n])
, proving the claim.

(0,n-N_{n})
(n,0)
(0,0)
(0,n)
(n,n)
(N_{n},n-N_{n})

Figure 35
.
A path
\pi_{n}\sim\mathbb{P}^{stat-\Gamma}_{n,n}
(black). The portion inside the green
n\times N_{n}
rectangle, when completed to a path
\pi_{n}^{\prime}
beginning at
(0,n-N_{n})
by adding a horizontal portion (red, dashed), has distribution
\mathbb{P}^{stat-\Gamma}_{n,N_{n}}
after shifting, by
6.3
. In this case,
\min(x_{mid}(\pi_{n}),N_{n})=x_{mid}(\pi_{n})
. If instead the black path had intersected the blue line below the green rectangle, we would have
\min(x_{mid}(\pi_{n}),N_{n})=N_{n}=x_{mid}(\pi_{n}^{\prime})
.

Let
\pi_{n}^{\prime}
be
\mathbb{P}^{stat-\Gamma}_{n,N_{n}}
-distributed. Similarly,
x_{mid}(\pi_{n}^{\prime})
only depends on
\pi_{n}^{\prime}\cap([1,n]\times[1,N_{n}])
. By the distributional equality of
6.3
,
\pi_{n}\cap([1,n]\times[n-N_{n}+1,n])
and
\pi_{n}^{\prime}\cap([1,n]\times[1,N_{n}])
are equal in distribution up to a vertical coordinate shift by
N_{n}
, which does not affect
x_{mid}
. Hence by the previous paragraph,

(8.24)

\min\{x_{mid}(\pi_{n}),N_{n}\}=x_{mid}(\pi_{n}^{\prime})\quad\quad\quad\quad\text{in (annealed) distribution.}

However,
8.4
will give us control on
x_{mid}(\pi_{n}^{\prime})
. Setting
N=N_{n}
and
m_{N}=n
in
8.4
, there exist positive constants
b_{0},C
depending only on the parameters
\alpha,\beta
and on
\tau
, such that for all
b\geq b_{0}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

(8.25)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{n,N_{n}}^{stat-\Gamma}(v_{0}(\lfloor\tau N_{n}\rfloor)\leq\tau n-bN_{n}^{2/3}\text{ or }v_{1}(\lfloor\tau N_{n}\rfloor)\geq\tau n+bN_{n}^{2/3})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{b^{3}}

By changing
b_{0}
and
C
, (
8.25
) remains true with
\tau n
replaced by
\lceil\tau n\rceil
and
bN_{n}^{2/3}
replaced by
bn^{2/3}
. Hence, setting

(8.26)

\tau=\frac{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha)+\Psi_{1}(\beta)},

we have

(8.27)

\tau\cdot n=N_{n}-\tau\cdot N_{n},

up to irrelevant
1+o_{n}(1)
factors from the floor function in (
8.23
) which we ignore. Hence (
8.25
) implies

(8.28)

\mathbb{P}^{stat-\Gamma}_{n,N_{n}}(\max\{(N_{n}-\lfloor\tau N_{n}\rfloor)-v_{0}(\lfloor\tau N_{n}\rfloor),v_{1}(\lfloor\tau N_{n}\rfloor)-(N_{n}-\lfloor\tau N_{n}\rfloor)\}\geq bn^{2/3})\leq\frac{C}{b^{3}}.

Taking
(m,n)=(n,N_{n})
and
\ell=\lfloor\tau N_{n}\rfloor
in
Lemma

8.5
, we have

(8.29)

\left\lvert x_{mid}(\pi_{n}^{\prime})-(N_{n}-\lfloor\tau N_{n}\rfloor)\right\rvert\leq\max\{(N_{n}-\lfloor\tau N_{n}\rfloor)-v_{0}(\lfloor\tau N_{n}\rfloor),v_{1}(\lfloor\tau N_{n}\rfloor)-(N_{n}-\lfloor\tau N_{n}\rfloor)\},

so

(8.30)

\mathbb{P}^{stat-\Gamma}_{n,N_{n}}(\left\lvert x_{mid}(\pi_{n}^{\prime})-(N_{n}-\lfloor\tau N_{n}\rfloor)\right\rvert\geq bn^{2/3})\leq\frac{C}{b^{3}}

for all
b\geq b_{0}
.

To complete the proof, we must deal with the minimum on the left hand side of (
8.24
). Note that (
8.30
) implies

(8.31)

\mathbb{P}^{stat-\Gamma}_{n,N_{n}}(x_{mid}(\pi_{n}^{\prime})\geq(1-\tau)N_{n}+bn^{2/3})\leq\frac{C}{b^{3}}.

Since
\tau>0
and
n
is a constant times
N_{n}
, (
8.31
) implies that
\mathbb{P}^{stat-\Gamma}_{n,N_{n}}(x_{mid}(\pi_{n}^{\prime})=N_{n})=o_{n}(1)
as
n\to\infty
. Hence by (
8.24
) and (
8.30
),

(8.32)

\mathbb{P}^{stat-\Gamma}_{n,n}(x_{mid}(\pi_{n})\geq(1-\tau)N_{n}+bn^{2/3})\leq\frac{C}{b^{3}}+o_{n}(1).

Note also that by (
8.23
),

(8.33)

(1-\tau)N_{n}=\frac{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha)+\Psi_{1}(\beta)}\cdot n\cdot(1+o_{n}(1)),

where the
1+o_{n}(1)
comes from ignoring the floor function in (
8.23
). After possibly changing
b_{0}
and
C
to dispense with the
1+o_{n}(1)
terms, substituting (
8.33
) into (
8.32
) yields (
8.6
).
∎

For
8.3
, we similarly begin with an elementary deterministic lemma.

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
n\leq m
, let
\pi\in\Pi_{m,n}
, and for any
1\leq\ell\leq n
and
1\leq k\leq n
let
v_{1}(\ell)
and
w_{1}(k)
be as in
8.1
. Then for any such
\pi
, the implications

(8.34)

\displaystyle v_{1}(\ell)\geq n-\ell

\displaystyle\implies x_{mid}(\pi)\geq n-\ell

(8.35)

\displaystyle w_{1}(k)\geq n-k

\displaystyle\implies x_{mid}(\pi)\leq k

hold.

Proof.

If
v_{1}(\ell)\geq n-\ell
, then the point
(v_{1}(\ell),\ell)\in\pi
lies weakly up-right of the line
x+y=n
. Hence the intersection of
\pi
with
x+y=n
occurs at
y
-coordinate
\leq\ell
, because
\pi
is an up-right path. Thus it occurs at
x
-coordinate
\geq n-\ell
, so
x_{mid}(\pi)\geq n-\ell
.

Similarly, if
w_{1}(k)\geq n-k
then the point
(k,w_{1}(k))\in\pi
lies weakly up-right of the line
x+y=n
. Hence the intersection of
\pi
with
x+y=n
occurs at
x
-coordinate
\leq k
.
∎

Proof of
8.3
.

As in the proof of
8.2
, it suffices to consider the case
\Psi_{1}(\alpha)\geq\Psi_{1}(\beta)
. Let
N_{n}
,
\pi_{n}
, and
\pi_{n}^{\prime}
be as in that proof, and recall that

(8.36)

\min\{x_{mid}(\pi_{n}),N_{n}\}=x_{mid}(\pi_{n}^{\prime})\quad\quad\quad\quad\text{in (annealed) distribution.}

For any
C_{1}
, by (
8.23
)

(8.37)

N_{n}>\frac{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha)+\Psi_{1}(\beta)}n+C_{1}n^{2/3}

for all sufficiently large
n
, and together with (
8.36
) this implies

(8.38)

\mathbb{P}^{stat-\Gamma}_{n,n}\left(\left\lvert x_{mid}(\pi_{n})-\frac{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha)+\Psi_{1}(\beta)}\cdot n\right\rvert>C_{1}n^{2/3}\right)\\
=\mathbb{P}^{stat-\Gamma}_{n,N_{n}}\left(\left\lvert x_{mid}(\pi_{n}^{\prime})-\frac{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha)+\Psi_{1}(\beta)}\cdot n\right\rvert>C_{1}n^{2/3}\right)

for all sufficiently large
n
. So it remains to bound the right-hand side of (
8.38
).

Let

(8.39)

\tau_{0}=\frac{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha)+\Psi_{1}(\beta)},

fix some
\delta\in(0,\tau_{0})
, and let
S=[\tau_{0}-\delta,\tau_{0}]
. Then

(8.40)

\tau_{n}:=\tau_{0}-c_{1}\frac{N_{n}^{\frac{2}{3}}}{N_{n}+n}\in S

for all
n
large enough. Setting
N=N_{n}
and
m_{N}=n
in
8.3
, there exist positive constants
c_{1},c_{0},N_{0}
such that

(8.41)

\mathbb{P}_{n,N_{n}}^{stat-\Gamma}\left(v_{1}(\lfloor\tau N_{n}\rfloor)\geq\tau n+c_{1}N_{n}^{2/3}\text{ or }w_{1}(\lfloor\tau n\rfloor)\geq\tau N_{n}+c_{1}n^{2/3}\right)\geq c_{0}.

for all
n\geq N_{0}
and
\tau\in S
.

\tau_{0}
satisfies the elementary relations

(8.42)

\tau_{0}n=(1-\tau_{0})N_{n}\cdot(1+o_{n}(1))

and

(8.43)

\tau_{0}=\frac{N_{n}}{N_{n}+n}\cdot(1+o_{n}(1)),

where as usual,
1+o_{n}(1)
comes from ignoring a floor function. From these a simple computation shows

(8.44)

N_{n}-\lfloor\tau_{n}N_{n}\rfloor=\left(\tau_{0}n+\tau_{0}c_{1}N_{n}^{2/3}\right)\cdot(1+o_{n}(1))

and

(8.45)

N_{n}-\lfloor\tau_{n}n\rfloor=\left(\tau_{0}N_{n}+(1-\tau_{0})\left(\frac{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha)}\right)^{2/3}c_{1}n^{2/3}\right)\cdot(1+o_{n}(1)).

Because
\tau_{0}<1
, (
8.44
) implies that

(8.46)

N_{n}-\lfloor\tau_{n}N_{n}\rfloor<\tau_{0}n+c_{1}N_{n}^{2/3}

for all large enough
n
. Similarly,
1-\tau_{0}\in(0,1)
, and by our assumption
\Psi_{1}(\alpha)\geq\Psi_{1}(\beta)
we have that

(8.47)

\left(\frac{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha)}\right)^{2/3}\in(0,1),

so (
8.45
) implies that

(8.48)

N_{n}-\lfloor\tau_{n}n\rfloor<\tau_{0}N_{n}+c_{1}n^{2/3}

for all large enough
n
. Combining (
8.46
) and (
8.48
) with (
8.41
), we have

(8.49)

\mathbb{P}_{n,N_{n}}^{stat-\Gamma}\left(v_{1}(\lfloor\tau_{n}N_{n}\rfloor)\geq N_{n}-\lfloor\tau_{n}N_{n}\rfloor\text{ or }w_{1}(\lfloor\tau_{n}n\rfloor)\geq\tau_{n}N_{n}-\lfloor\tau_{n}n\rfloor\right)\geq c_{0}

for all sufficiently large
n
. By
Lemma

8.6
, the implications

(8.50)

v_{1}(\lfloor\tau_{n}N_{n}\rfloor)\geq N_{n}-\lfloor\tau_{n}N_{n}\rfloor\quad\quad\implies\quad\quad x_{mid}(\pi_{n}^{\prime})\geq N_{n}-\lfloor\tau_{n}N_{n}\rfloor

and

(8.51)

w_{1}(\lfloor\tau_{n}n\rfloor)\geq\tau_{n}N_{n}-\lfloor\tau_{n}n\rfloor\quad\quad\implies\quad\quad x_{mid}(\pi_{n}^{\prime})\leq\lfloor\tau_{n}n\rfloor

hold for any path
\pi_{n}^{\prime}
. Hence (
8.49
) implies

(8.52)

\mathbb{P}_{n,N_{n}}^{stat-\Gamma}\left(x_{mid}(\pi_{n}^{\prime})\geq N_{n}-\lfloor\tau_{n}N_{n}\rfloor\text{ or }x_{mid}(\pi_{n}^{\prime})\leq\lfloor\tau_{n}n\rfloor\right)\geq c_{0}.

By (
8.44
) and the definition of
N_{n}
,

(8.53)

N_{n}-\lfloor\tau_{n}N_{n}\rfloor=\left(\tau_{0}n+\tau_{0}c_{1}\left(\frac{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha)}\right)^{2/3}n^{2/3}\right)\cdot(1+o_{n}(1)).

By (
8.45
) and
(
8.42
),

(8.54)

\lfloor\tau_{n}n\rfloor=\left(\tau_{0}n-(1-\tau_{0})\left(\frac{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha)}\right)^{2/3}c_{1}n^{2/3}\right)\cdot(1+o_{n}(1)).

Let
C_{1}
be any real number satisfying

(8.55)

0<C_{1}<\min\left\{\tau_{0}c_{1}\left(\frac{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha)}\right)^{2/3},(1-\tau_{0})\left(\frac{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha)}\right)^{2/3}c_{1}\right\},

so that

(8.56)

\tau_{0}n+C_{1}n^{2/3}<\left(\tau_{0}n-(1-\tau_{0})\left(\frac{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha)}\right)^{2/3}c_{1}n^{2/3}\right)\cdot(1+o_{n}(1))

and

(8.57)

\tau_{0}n-C_{1}n^{2/3}>\left(\tau_{0}n-(1-\tau_{0})\left(\frac{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha)}\right)^{2/3}c_{1}n^{2/3}\right)\cdot(1+o_{n}(1))

for all sufficiently large
n
, say
n>n_{0}
where we also take
n_{0}>N_{0}
. Then taking the bound (
8.52
), substituting the asymptotic expressions (
8.53
) and (
8.54
), and bounding these via (
8.56
) and (
8.57
), we have that

(8.58)

\mathbb{P}_{n,N_{n}}^{stat-\Gamma}\left(x_{mid}(\pi_{n}^{\prime})\geq\tau_{0}n+C_{1}n^{2/3}\text{ or }x_{mid}(\pi_{n}^{\prime})\leq\tau_{0}n-C_{1}n^{2/3}\right)\geq c_{0}

for all
n>n_{0}
. After taking
C_{0}=c_{0}
in (
8.58
), recalling the definition of
\tau_{0}
, and replacing
\mathbb{P}^{stat-\Gamma}_{n,N_{n}}
and
\pi_{n}^{\prime}
by
\mathbb{P}^{stat-\Gamma}_{n,n}
and
\pi_{n}
via (
8.38
) (possibly increasing
n_{0}
so that the latter holds), (
8.58
) yields (
8.7
).
∎

8.3.
North and South

Because the North turning point is related to the South turning point by symmetry, we will again just consider the South one. Recall that
\Psi_{1}
is the trigamma function.

Proposition 8.7

(Tail bounds on the
n^{2/3}
scale)
.

Fix parameters
\alpha,\beta>0
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
let
\pi_{n}
be a path distributed by the random polymer measure
Q^{SW}_{n,n}
of
7.7
with these parameters, and let
x_{mid}(\pi_{n})
be as in
6.3
. Then there exist constants
b_{0},C
depending on
\alpha,\beta
such that for every
b\geq b_{0}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

(8.59)

\mathbb{P}^{SW}_{n,n}\left(\left\lvert x_{mid}(\pi_{n})-\frac{\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}\cdot n\right\rvert\geq bn^{2/3}\right)\leq\frac{C}{b^{3}}.

Proposition 8.8

(Anticoncentration on the
n^{2/3}
scale)
.

Assume the same setup as
8.7
. Then there exist constants
n_{0},C_{0},C_{1}>0
depending on
\alpha,\beta
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
,

(8.60)

\mathbb{P}^{SW}_{n,n}\left(\left\lvert x_{mid}(\pi_{n})-\frac{\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}\cdot n\right\rvert>C_{1}n^{2/3}\right)\geq C_{0}.

The only needed new input is the analogue of
8.4
, which we give now.

Theorem 8.9

(Tail bounds and anticoncentration on the
n^{2/3}
scale)
.

Recall the setup of
7.7
and fix the parameters
\alpha,\beta
. For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let

(8.61)

m_{N}=\left\lfloor\frac{\Psi_{1}(\beta)-\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta)}N\right\rfloor

where
\Psi_{1}
is the trigamma function. Then there exist positive constants
b_{0},C,c_{0},c_{1},N_{0}
depending only on the parameters
\alpha,\beta
and on
\tau
, such that for all
b\geq b_{0}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

(8.62)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{m_{N},N}^{SW}(v_{0}(\lfloor\tau N\rfloor)\leq\tau m_{N}-bN^{2/3}\text{ or }v_{1}(\lfloor\tau N\rfloor)\geq\tau m_{N}+bN^{2/3})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{b^{3}}

(8.63)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{m_{N},N}^{SW}(w_{0}(\lfloor\tau m_{N}\rfloor)\leq\tau N-bN^{2/3}\text{ or }w_{1}(\lfloor\tau m_{N}\rfloor)\geq\tau N+bN^{2/3})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{b^{3}}

and for all
N\geq N_{0}
,

(8.64)

\mathbb{P}_{m_{N},N}^{SW}(v_{1}(\lfloor\tau N\rfloor)\geq\tau m_{N}+c_{1}N^{2/3}\text{ or }w_{1}(\tau m_{N})\geq\tau N+c_{1}N^{2/3})\geq c_{0}.

Furthermore, for any compact set
S\subset[0,1)
, the constants
b_{0},C,c_{0},c_{1},N_{0}
may be chosen uniformly over all
\tau\in S
.

Proof.

This is a special case of the strict-weak case of
[
21
, Theorem 1.5]
, where we recall that our
\alpha,\beta
correspond to
\mu,\theta
in their notation, and we set their
\beta
to be
1
. Specifically, one must take the parameters
N,n_{N},m_{N}
in that result to be
\lfloor N/\Psi_{1}(\beta)\rfloor
,
N
and
\lfloor N\Psi_{1}(\alpha)/\Psi_{1}(\beta)\rfloor
in terms of our variables. Note that the fact that our
N
is a constant times the one in
[
21
, Theorem 1.5]
changes the constants
c_{1},b,C
in that result by a constant, but this does not change the result.

One must then verify that our
n_{N}
and
m_{N}
satisfy
[
21
, (1.10)]
. In our notation, this condition translates to the bounds

(8.65)

\left\lvert\left\lfloor\frac{\Psi_{1}(\beta)-\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta)}N\right\rfloor-\left\lfloor\frac{N}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta)}\right\rfloor\cdot\Var(\log R_{j})\right\rvert\leq\gamma N^{2/3}

and

(8.66)

\left\lvert N-\left\lfloor\frac{N}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta)}\right\rfloor\cdot\Var(\log Y_{i,0})\right\rvert\leq\gamma N^{2/3}

holding for all
N
, for some constant
\gamma
. Recalling the distributions of
R_{j}
and
Y_{i,0}
from
7.7
, it is well known that

(8.67)

\displaystyle\Var(\log R_{j})

\displaystyle=\Psi_{1}(\beta)-\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta)

(8.68)

\displaystyle\Var(\log Y_{i,0})

\displaystyle=\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta),

and this proves the above bounds.
∎

Proof of
8.7
.

First assume that
\alpha,\beta
are such that

(8.69)

\Psi_{1}(\beta)-\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta)\geq\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta).

This is the analogue of the condition
\Psi_{1}(\alpha)\geq\Psi_{1}(\beta)
in the proof of
8.2
. The proof is now exactly the same as that of
8.2
after replacing
\Psi_{1}(\alpha)
and
\Psi_{1}(\beta)
(the variances of the logarithms of the boundary weights in the stationary log-Gamma model) by
\Psi_{1}(\beta)-\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta)
and
\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta)
(the variances of the logarithms of the boundary weights in the stationary strict-weak model). The only needed input is
8.9
in place of
8.4
, and the deterministic
Lemma

8.5
which is valid for both polymers.

Unlike the log-Gamma case, where the case
\Psi_{1}(\alpha)\geq\Psi_{1}(\beta)
and its negation could be related by symmetry, the case

(8.70)

\Psi_{1}(\beta)-\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta)<\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta).

cannot be dealt with by symmetry, but it can be dealt with by the same argument. Instead of choosing
N_{n}
by

(8.71)

n=\left\lfloor\frac{\Psi_{1}(\beta)-\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta)}N_{n}\right\rfloor

as we do in the case (
8.69
), we choose
N_{n}
to be an integer such that

(8.72)

n=\left\lfloor\frac{\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\beta)-\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta)}N_{n}\right\rfloor,

and (
8.70
) ensures we may choose
N_{n}\leq n
. Rather than considering the wide short box
[0,n]\times[n-N_{n},n]
inside
[0,n]^{2}
as before, we reflect the picture and consider the tall thin box
[n-N_{n},n]\times[0,n]
. The result
6.3
applies mutatis mutandis to the stationary strict-weak polymer, since its proof just uses the Burke property. So the portion of our polymer path inside
[n-N_{n},n]\times[0,n]
is also distributed as a stationary strict-weak polymer.

Now, the ratio of this box’s dimensions is still
\frac{\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\beta)-\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta)}\cdot(1+o(1))
, which is the right ratio for
8.9
to apply. From there we obtain that the polymer path crosses the line
y=n-x
roughly at its intersection with the line

(8.73)

y-n=\frac{n}{N_{n}}(x-n),

which is the diagonal of the box
[n-N_{n},n]\times[0,n]
, together with appropriate quantitative bounds as before. Solving for the
x
-coordinate of the intersection between these two lines yields

(8.74)

x=\frac{\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta)}{\Psi_{1}(\beta)}\cdot n\cdot(1+o(1))

where the
1+o(1)
comes from the floor function in (
8.72
) when we rewrite
N_{n}
in terms of
n
. The details of establishing the fluctuation results using
8.9
are the same as the case (
8.69
) after reflecting about the line
x=y
.
∎

Proof of
8.8
.

In the case (
8.69
) the proof is identical to that of
8.3
, again after substituting
\Psi_{1}(\alpha)
and
\Psi_{1}(\beta)
in that argument for
\Psi_{1}(\beta)-\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta)
and
\Psi_{1}(\alpha+\beta)
. In the case (
8.70
) we define
N_{n}
by (
8.72
) and make the same argument as before but reflected about the line
y=x
.
∎

8.4.
Proof of
1.3

Proof.

For the West turning point, the result follows by matching the turning point with the stationary log-Gamma polymer midpoint by
1.4
, and then applying
8.2
and
8.3
. For the South turning point, we similarly match with the strict-weak polymer by
7.5
and then apply
8.7
and
8.8
. The North turning point is the same after reflecting the Aztec diamond about the horizontal axis, which interchanges
\alpha
and
\beta
. In principle we have different constants for the bounds for each turning point, but by taking the worst case of the three we have the uniform constants in
1.3
.
∎

9.
Asymptotics of the free energy

Our next goal is to understand the asymptotic behavior of the free energy.

9.1.
Results

Recall from the Introduction that the free energy is defined as

(9.1)

F_{n}=T\log Z_{n}.

The free energy is a central quantity in statistical mechanics, though it is often challenging to compute explicitly. In our model, however, the partition function is given as a product of independent random variables (see
3.4
), which allows us to employ standard probabilistic tools. Below we present our main results; the derivations are provided afterward.

We begin by computing the averaged quenched and annealed free energies. The
anneaeled
free energy is

F_{n}^{a}=T\log\mathbb{E}[Z_{n}].

By Jensen’s inequality, we always have
F_{n}^{a}\geq\mathbb{E}F_{n}
. Since
Z_{n}
is a product of independent random variables, both quantities can be computed explicitly.

Theorem 9.1
.

Consider the averaged quenched and annealed free energies,
\mathbb{E}F_{n}
and
F_{n}^{a}
for the Gamma-disordered Aztec diamond as defined in
1.7
. We have

(9.2)

F_{n}^{a}=T\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\log(\psi_{j}+\phi_{j-k}),

and

(9.3)

\mathbb{E}F_{n}=T\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\Psi_{0}(\psi_{j}+\phi_{j-k}),

where
\Psi_{0}(x)
denotes the digamma function.

The proof is given in Section
9.3
below.

To compare
F_{n}^{a}
and
\mathbb{E}F_{n}
, we use the following classical inequality for the digamma function:

\frac{1}{2x}<\log x-\Psi_{0}(x)<\frac{1}{x},\quad x>0.

This shows that when all parameters
\psi_{j}+\phi_{j-k}
are large, the annealed and quenched free energies are close. However, when these parameters are close to zero, the difference between
F_{n}^{a}
and
\mathbb{E}F_{n}
becomes significant. To track this in a uniform way we scale
\psi_{j}
and
\phi_{j-n}
parameters by
T
:

\psi_{j}=\bar{\psi}_{j}T,\qquad\phi_{j-n}=\bar{\phi}_{j-n}T.

With this scaling, we obtain the following result.

Corollary 9.2
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\bar{\psi}_{j},\bar{\phi}_{j},T>0
. Consider the averaged quenched and annealed free energies,
\mathbb{E}F_{n}
and
F_{n}^{a}
for the Gamma-disordered Aztec diamond as defined in
1.7
with
\psi_{j}=T\bar{\psi}_{j}
and
\phi_{j-n}=T\bar{\phi}_{j-n}.

Then

\frac{1}{2n^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\frac{1}{\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}}<\frac{1}{n^{2}}\left(F_{n}^{a}-\mathbb{E}[F_{n}]\right)<\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\frac{1}{\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}}.

We conclude with a result on the fluctuations of the free energy.

Theorem 9.3
.

In the same setting as
9.2
, as
n\to\infty
we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{F_{n}-\mathbb{E}[F_{n}]}{\sqrt{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{k}T^{2}\Psi_{1}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}))}}<s\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{s}e^{-x^{2}/2}\,dx+\mathcal{O}(n^{-1}),

where the error term is uniform for
T\in(0,\infty)
, and for all sequences
\{\bar{\psi}_{j}\},\{\bar{\phi}_{k}\}
contained in compact subsets of
(0,\infty)
. Here,
\Psi_{1}(x)=\Psi_{0}^{\prime}(x)
denotes the trigamma function.

The uniformity of the error term with respect to
T\in(0,\infty)
implies, in particular, that
T
may vary arbitrarily with
n
without affecting the nature of the fluctuations. Consequently, the fluctuations remain Gaussian in all regimes; there is no scaling limit of
T
with
n
that leads to non-Gaussian behavior.

Finally, we recall that the trigamma function
\Psi_{1}
is strictly positive and satisfies the bounds

(9.4)

\frac{1}{x}+\frac{1}{2x^{2}}\leq\Psi_{1}(x)\leq\frac{1}{x}+\frac{1}{x^{2}},\quad x>0.

These estimates imply that the variance of
F_{n}
behaves asymptotically as
\sim n^{2}
for small
T>0
, and as
\sim n^{2}T
when both
n
and
T
are large.

9.2.
Preliminaries on Gamma random variables and their logarithms

We begin with some basic observations about logarithms of Gamma-distributed random variables. First, note that if
X\sim\Gamma(\xi,1)
, then

(9.5)

\mathbb{E}[X]=\xi.

Next we recall that the characteristic function
\mathbb{E}[X^{it}]
of
\log X
is given by

\frac{\Gamma(\xi+it)}{\Gamma(\xi)},

and hence the cumulants of
\log X
are

(9.6)

\kappa_{k}=\Psi_{k-1}(\xi),\quad k\geq 2,

where
\Psi_{k}=\frac{d^{k+1}}{dx^{k+1}}\log\Gamma(x)
denotes the polygamma function. Moreover, the following asymptotic expansions hold
[
62
, Chapter 5]
:

(9.7)

\Psi_{k}(\xi)=\frac{(-1)^{k+1}k!}{\xi^{k+1}}+\mathcal{O}(1),\quad\xi\downarrow 0,

and

(9.8)

\Psi_{k}(\xi)=\frac{(-1)^{k+1}k!}{\xi^{k}}+\mathcal{O}(\xi^{-k-1}),\quad\xi\to\infty.

These expressions show that the variance of
\log X
is of order
\sim 1/\xi^{2}
for small
\xi
, and
\sim 1/\xi
for large
\xi
. After appropriate rescaling, we obtain the following. We do not actually use it in this paper (for the desired Gaussianity we make a stronger argument which involves the rate of convergence), but it is worth recording.

Proposition 9.4
.

Let
X\sim\Gamma(\xi,1)
. Then, as
\xi\to\infty
,

(9.9)

\frac{\log X-\Psi_{0}(\xi)}{\sqrt{\Psi_{1}(\xi)}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1),

and as
\xi\downarrow 0
,

(9.10)

\frac{\log X-\Psi_{0}(\xi)}{\sqrt{\Psi_{1}(\xi)}}-1\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}Z\sim-\mathrm{Exp}(1),

in distribution.

Proof.

The cumulants
\tilde{\kappa}_{k}
of

\frac{\log X-\Psi_{0}(\xi)}{\sqrt{\Psi_{1}(\xi)}}

are given by

\tilde{\kappa}_{1}=0,\qquad\tilde{\kappa}_{2}=1,\qquad\tilde{\kappa}_{k}=\frac{\Psi_{k-1}(\xi)}{(\Psi_{1}(\xi))^{k/2}}\quad\text{for }k\geq 3.

From (
9.8
), it follows that
\tilde{\kappa}_{k}\to 0
as
\xi\to\infty
for all
k\geq 3
. Hence, the rescaled logarithm of a Gamma distribution converges in distribution to a standard normal distribution, proving (
9.9
).

Similarly, from (
9.7
), we find that as
\xi\downarrow 0
, the cumulants satisfy

\tilde{\kappa}_{k}\to(-1)^{k}(k-1)!\quad\text{for }k\geq 2,

which are the cumulants of the negative of an exponential distribution with parameter 1. Therefore, after shifting the mean appropriately, we obtain convergence to
-\mathrm{Exp}(1)
, establishing (
9.10
).
∎

9.3.
Proofs of Theorem
9.1
and
9.3

We recall that by Corollary
3.4
we have

Z_{n}=\prod_{k=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{k}X_{j,k},

where
X_{j,k}
, for
j=1,\ldots,k
and
k=1,\ldots,n
are independent random variables with distribution

X_{j,k}\sim\Gamma(\psi_{j}+\phi_{j-k},1).

The independence makes it easy to compute the the expectation and fluctuations of the free energy. Let us start with the mean and then compute the fluctuations.

Proof of Theorem
9.1
.

Using the independence of the random variables and equation (
9.5
), we obtain

\mathbb{E}[Z_{n}]=\prod_{k=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{k}(\psi_{j}+\phi_{j-k}).

Taking the logarithm yields equation (
9.2
).

To compute the averaged quenched free energy, we first take the logarithm and then the expectation. Applying equation (
9.6
), we find

\mathbb{E}[\log Z_{n}]=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\Psi_{0}(\psi_{j}+\phi_{j-k}),

which establishes equation (
9.3
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
9.3
.

It is clear from the independence of the random variables that a Central Limit Theorem holds for any fixed choice of parameters. However, to obtain a uniform convergence, it is useful to apply the Berry–Esseen theorem. Recall that this theorem states that for independent (but not necessarily identically distributed) random variables
X_{j}
, for
j=1,\ldots,N
, we have

\left|\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{N}(X_{j}-\mathbb{E}X_{j})}{\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sigma_{j}^{2}\right)^{1/2}}<s\right)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{s}e^{-x^{2}/2}\,dx\right|\leq C\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}[|X_{j}-\mathbb{E}X_{j}|^{3}]}{\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sigma_{j}^{2}\right)^{3/2}},

where
C
is a universal constant and
\sigma_{j}^{2}=\operatorname{Var}(X_{j})
.

Using the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we have

\mathbb{E}[|X_{j}-\mathbb{E}X_{j}|^{3}]\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[|X_{j}-\mathbb{E}X_{j}|^{2}]}\cdot\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[|X_{j}-\mathbb{E}X_{j}|^{4}]}.

Applying Cauchy–Schwarz again to the sum, we obtain

\left|\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{N}(X_{j}-\mathbb{E}X_{j})}{\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sigma_{j}^{2}\right)^{1/2}}<s\right)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{s}e^{-x^{2}/2}\,dx\right|\leq C\frac{\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}[|X_{j}-\mathbb{E}X_{j}|^{4}]\right)^{1/2}}{\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sigma_{j}^{2}}.

Next, observe that

\mathbb{E}[|X_{j}-\mathbb{E}X_{j}|^{4}]=\kappa_{4,j}+3\kappa_{2,j}^{2},

where
\kappa_{k,j}
denotes the
k
th cumulant of
X_{j}
. This is particularly helpful in our setting, as the cumulants are expressed in terms of polygamma functions (see equation (
9.6
)).

Applying this to our case (include the factor
T
) yields:

\left|\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{F_{n}-\mathbb{E}F_{n}}{\sqrt{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{k}T^{2}\Psi_{1}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}))}}<s\right)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{s}e^{-x^{2}/2}\,dx\right|\\
\leq C\frac{\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{k}T^{4}\Psi_{3}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}))+3T^{2}\Psi_{1}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}))^{2}\right)^{1/2}}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{k}T^{2}\Psi_{1}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}))}.

We have also used equation (
9.6
) to compute both second and fourth moments (i.e., cumulants). We can bound the expression as follows:

\left|\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{F_{n}-\mathbb{E}F_{n}}{\sqrt{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{k}T^{2}\Psi_{1}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}))}}<s\right)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{s}e^{-x^{2}/2}\,dx\right|\\
\leq\frac{C\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{n(n+1)}}\cdot\frac{\max_{j,k}\sqrt{T^{4}\Psi_{3}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}))+3T^{2}\Psi_{1}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}))^{2}}}{\min_{j,k}T^{2}\Psi_{1}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}))}.

Now note that from (
9.7
) we find

T^{4}\Psi_{3}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}))+3T^{2}\Psi_{1}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}))^{2}\sim 1,

and

T^{2}\Psi_{1}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k})\sim 1,

both as
T\downarrow 0.
Hence

(9.11)

\frac{\max_{j,k}\sqrt{T^{4}\Psi_{3}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}))+3T^{2}\Psi_{1}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}))^{2}}}{\min_{j,k}T^{2}\Psi_{1}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}))}\sim 1,

as
T\downarrow 0
.

Similarly, from (
9.8
) we find

T^{4}\Psi_{3}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}))+3T^{2}\Psi_{1}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}))^{2}\sim T,

and

T^{2}\Psi_{1}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k})\sim T,

both as
T\to\infty
. Hence,

(9.12)

\frac{\max_{j,k}\sqrt{T^{4}\Psi_{3}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}))+3T^{2}\Psi_{1}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}))^{2}}}{\min_{j,k}T^{2}\Psi_{1}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}))}\sim 1,

as
T\to\infty
. Finally, by continuity, the fact that
\Psi_{1}(x)>0
for
x>0
, (
9.11
) and (
9.12
) we find that there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that

c_{1}<\frac{\max_{j,k}\sqrt{T^{4}\Psi_{3}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}))+3T^{2}\Psi_{1}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}))^{2}}}{\min_{j,k}T^{2}\Psi_{1}(T(\bar{\psi}_{j}+\bar{\phi}_{j-k}))}<c_{2},

for all
T\in(0,\infty)
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

The theorem follows directly from Theorem
9.1
and Corollary
9.2
by setting
\bar{\psi}_{j}=\bar{\alpha}
and
\bar{\phi}_{j-n}=\bar{\beta}
.
∎

Appendix A
LGV paths

An important strategy for studying the Aztec diamond dimer model is by using its bijection to non-intersecting paths on a directed graph, and utilizing a theorem due to Lindström-Gessel-Viennot
[
37
,
56
]
and Eynard-Mehta
[
35
]
to study the correlation functions. In fact, it was shown recently
[
22
]
that the shuffling algorithm is related to commutation rules for the transition matrices for the non-intersecting paths. The reader familiar with
[
22
]
may have noticed the resemblance between switching
(+)
- and
(-)
-columns, as done in
Section

4
, and these commutation rules. The purpose of this appendix is to outline how these points of view are indeed equivalent (as one expects).

A.1.
Non-intersecting paths

We start by introducing the model of non-intersecting paths on a directed graph, beginning with defining the latter.

The graph
G_{LGV}^{n}=(V_{LGV}^{n},E_{LGV}^{n})
is defined as follows. The vertex set is

V_{LGV}^{n}=\left\{(i,j)\mid i\in\{0,1,\ldots,2n\},\ j\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}.

The directed edges are given by the following rules, for all
i=0,1,\ldots,n-1
and all
j\in\mathbb{Z}
:

•

From each vertex
(2i,j)
, there are directed edges to
(2i+1,j)
and
(2i+1,j-1)
.

•

From each vertex
(2i+1,j)
, there are directed edges to
(2i+2,j)
and
(2i+1,j-1)
.

(0,-1)
(2n,-n-1)
(0,-2)
(2n,-n-2)
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
(0,-N)
(2n,-N-n)

Figure 36
.
Illustration of the non-intersecting paths on the graph
G_{LGV}^{n}
(here
n=4
).

The graph can be interpreted as an alternating arrangement of two types of infinite vertical columns. The type I columns connect vertices to their two right neighbors, one horizontally across and one diagonally down.
The type II columns send arrows horizontally to the neighbors on the right, and also downward to the neighbors directly below.

\beta_{1}
1-\beta_{1}
\beta_{2}
1-\beta_{2}
\beta_{3}
1-\beta_{3}
\beta_{4}
1-\beta_{4}
\beta_{5}
\gamma_{1}
1
\gamma_{2}
1
\gamma_{3}
1
\gamma_{4}
1
\gamma_{5}

Figure 37
.
Part of a type I column (left) and part of a type II column (right).

For
N\geq n
we then consider collections of paths
\{\pi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
on this directed graph such that

•

Each path
\pi_{j}
starts at
(0,-j)
and ends at
(2n,-j-n)
.

•

The paths are mutually disjoint, i.e. they have no vertices in common.

The last is the non-intersecting condition.

The connection between the matchings of the Aztec diamond and these non-intersecting paths has been discussed at length in various places in the literature. It is important to note that, despite the fact that all are based on the same principle, there are different conventions in the literature. We will briefly discuss here the viewpoint of
[
22
,
6
]
, and refer to these papers for further details.

Instead of considering just one Aztec diamond of size
n
, we will consider a region that consists of gluing three individual parts as shown in Figure
38
: two Aztec diamond graphs, one of size
n
and one of size
n-1
, with a simple corridor of vertical length
N-n
in between. We place the graph such that the top-left black vertex has coordinate
(\frac{1}{4},-\frac{5}{4})
and the top-left white vertex has coordinate
(\frac{3}{4},-\frac{3}{4})
.

We claim that there is a bijection between all matching of this larger graph and non-intersecting paths on the graph
G_{LGV}^{n}
described above. This is done by drawing red path on the Northwest, Southeast and Southwest dominoes as indicated in Figure
39
. By removing the dominoes and augmenting the paths with trivial horizontal parts as indicated in the picture on the right, we obtain precisely a collection of non-intersecting paths on
G_{LGV}^{n}
with the starting and endpoint described above.

An important feature of the larger (slightly odd-looking) bipartite graph is that a matching cannot have an edge that has one vertex in a corridor and one in one of the Aztec diamonds. More precisely, each matching decouples into two matchings of the individual Aztec diamond graphs and a frozen configuration in the corridor in between.

For the paths this means that each path
\pi_{j}
has to pass through the point

(2n-2j+1,\,-n-1)\,,\qquad j=1,\dots,n.

Moreover, there is an explicit bijection between the portions of the paths
17
17

17

This portion by itself are referred to in the literature as the DR paths
[
43
]
. They start from one side of the Aztec, end at a neighboring side and never intersect.
lying above the line

y=-n-1

and dimer configurations of the Aztec diamond of size
n
. That the matching is frozen in the corridor implies that segments of the paths between the horizontal lines

y=-n-1\quad\text{and}\quad y=-N-1

are deterministic zig–zag paths.

Figure 38
.
The leftmost picture is the bipartite graph that consists of two Aztec diamond graphs, one of size 3 and another of size 2, and a corridor of vertical length 2 between them. Every dimer configuration necessarily decouples into a dimer cover of the three separate regions.

(0,-1)
(0,-N)
(2n,-n-1)
(2n,-N-n)

Figure 39
.
By drawing paths on the dominoes in the dimer matching in Figure
38
we obtain non-intersecting paths. By augmenting these paths with trivial horizontal lines, we obtain configuration of non-intersecting paths. This augmentation by horizontal lines corresponds to the vertex-dilation to go from
G^{\Az}_{n}
to
G^{vert}_{n}
in
Section

4
.

A.2.
The LGV theorem

The next step is to define a probability measure on the collection of non-intersecting paths. Before we do that, we first introduce some notation.

Let
S=(S_{i,j})_{i,j\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the shift matrix

S_{i,j}=\begin{cases}1,&j=i-1,\\
0&j\neq i-1.\end{cases}

For
\bm{a}=\{a_{j}\}_{j=\infty}^{\infty}
we define
D(\bm{a})
as the
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
diagonal matrix with diagonal entries given by
a_{j}
, but in reversed order:

D(\bm{a})=\mathop{{\mathrm{diag}}}(\ldots,a_{2},a_{1},a_{0},a_{-1},a_{-2},\ldots).

In other words,
(D(\bm{a}))_{jk}=a_{-k}
if
j=k
and
(D(\bm{a}))_{jk}=0
otherwise.
We also define infinite matrices
\phi(\bm{\beta})
and
\psi(\bm{\gamma})
as follows:

\phi(\bm{\beta})=D(\bm{\beta})+(I-D(\bm{\beta}))S,\quad\text{and}\qquad\psi(\bm{\gamma})=\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}S^{j}\right)D(\bm{\gamma}).

These matrices encode the weighting of the two different type columns that were used to construct the graph
G_{LGV}^{n}
.

We use these matrices to define weights on the edges of the graph
G_{LGV}^{n}
as follows. For
i=1,\ldots,n
let
\bm{\beta}_{i}^{[n]}=\{{\beta}_{i,j}^{[n]}\}_{j=-\infty}^{\infty}
and
\bm{\gamma}_{i+1}^{[n]}=\{\gamma_{i+1,j}^{[n]}\}_{j=-\infty}^{\infty}
be two families of sequences of positive numbers, and set

\phi_{i}=\phi(\bm{\beta}_{i}^{[n]}),\quad\text{and }\psi_{i}=\psi(\bm{\gamma}_{i+1}^{[n]}).

Then we define edge weights

w((2(i-1),j)\to(2i-1,k))=\left(\phi_{i}\right)_{j,k},

and

w((2i-1,j)\to(2i,k))=\left(\psi_{i}\right)_{j,k}

for
i=1,\ldots,n
.
Using these weights, we then define a probability measure on the set of all non-intersecting paths by defining the probability of a collection
\{\pi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
as the product of the weights of all edges:

\mathbb{P}\left(\{\pi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}\right)=\frac{1}{Z_{n,N}}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\prod_{e\in\pi_{j}}w(e),

where
Z_{n,N}
is a normalizing constant.

Remark A.1
.

The slightly odd convention that the sequences of
\gamma_{j}
’s start with
\gamma_{2}
and end with
\gamma_{n+1}
is done to match the conventions that we made earlier in the paper. Indeed, by choosing

(A.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\gamma_{i,j}^{[n]}&=a_{i,j}^{[n]}+b_{i,j}^{[n]}\\
\beta_{i,j}^{[n]}&=\frac{a_{i,j}^{[n]}}{a_{i,j}^{[n]}+b_{i,j}^{[n]}},\end{split}

the mapping from non-intersecting paths to matchings of the Aztec diamond with weights
a^{[n]}_{i,j}
and
b_{i,j}^{[n]}
outlined in
Figure

39
is weight-preserving (up to an overall factor that does not change the probability measure).

A.3.
Vertical slices

For each
\ell\in\{0,\ldots,2n\}
and
j=1,\ldots,N
the
\pi_{j}
has at least one (but possibly more) vertex with horizontal coordinate
\ell
, and we define
\pi_{j}(\ell
) as the vertical coordinate of the
highest
vertex with horizontal coordinate
\ell
. We will be interested in the joint law of the positions
\pi_{j}(2\ell-1)
for
j=1,\ldots,N
, at given odd horizontal coordinate
2\ell-1
for some
\ell\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
.

Before describing the law we first define, for
\ell=1,2,\ldots,n
, the matrices,

\Phi_{\ell}=\phi_{1}\psi_{1}\phi_{2}\psi_{2}\cdots\phi_{\ell-1}\psi_{\ell-1}\phi_{\ell}=\left(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell-1}\phi_{j}\psi_{j}\right)\phi_{\ell},

and

\Psi_{\ell}=\psi_{\ell}\phi_{\ell+1}\psi_{\ell+1}\phi_{\ell+2}\psi_{\ell+2}\cdots\phi_{n}\psi_{n}\phi_{\ell}=\psi_{\ell}\prod_{j=\ell+1}^{n}\phi_{j}\psi_{j}.

Then the celebrated theorem by Lindström-Gessel-Viennot (the LGV Theorem)
[
37
,
56
]
tells us that the law of the
\pi_{j}(2\ell-1)
is given by a product of determinants:

(A.2)

\mathbb{P}(\pi_{j}(2\ell-1)=x_{j},j=1,\ldots,N)=\frac{1}{Z_{\ell,n,N}}\det((\Phi_{\ell})_{-i,x_{j}})_{i,j=1}^{N}\det((\Psi_{\ell})_{x_{j},-j-n})_{i,j=1}^{N}

where
Z_{\ell,n,N}
is a normalizing constant.

A.4.
Reversing the order of the columns

Now the important point is that one can reverse the order in the product in the definition of
\Phi_{\ell}
and
\Psi_{\ell}
based on the following lemma, which is the analogue of Lemma
4.2

Lemma A.1
.

Let
\bm{\beta}=\{\beta_{j}\}_{j=-\infty}^{\infty},\bm{\gamma}=\{\gamma_{j}\}_{j=-\infty}^{\infty}
be two infinite sequences. Then

\phi(\bm{\beta})\psi(\bm{\gamma})=\psi(\hat{\bm{\gamma}})\phi(\hat{\bm{\beta}}),

where
\hat{\beta},\hat{\gamma}
are defined by

(A.3)

\begin{cases}\hat{\gamma}_{j}&=\beta_{j}\gamma_{j}+(1-\beta_{j+1})\gamma_{j+1}\\
\hat{\beta}_{j}&=\frac{\beta_{j}\gamma_{j}}{\beta_{j}\gamma_{j}+(1-\beta_{j+1})\gamma_{j+1}},\end{cases}

for
j\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

The verification is a straightforward matrix multiplication. We will show that

(A.4)

\psi(\hat{\bm{\gamma}})^{-1}\phi(\bm{\beta})=\phi(\hat{\bm{\beta})}\psi(\bm{\gamma})^{-1}.

First note that

(A.5)

\psi(\hat{\bm{\gamma}})^{-1}\phi(\bm{\beta})=D(\hat{\bm{\gamma}})^{-1}(I-S)(D(\bm{\beta})+(I-D(\bm{\beta}))S)\\
=D(\hat{\bm{\gamma}})^{-1}D(\bm{\beta})-D(\hat{\bm{\gamma}})^{-1}SD(\bm{\beta})+D(\hat{\bm{\gamma}})^{-1}(I-D(\bm{\beta}))S-D(\hat{\bm{\gamma}})^{-1}S(I-D(\bm{\beta}))S,

and

(A.6)

\phi(\hat{\bm{\beta}})\psi(\bm{\gamma})^{-1}=(D(\hat{\bm{\beta}})+(I-D(\hat{\bm{\beta}}))S)D(\bm{\gamma})^{-1}(I-S)\\
=D(\hat{\bm{\beta}})D(\bm{\gamma})^{-1}-D(\hat{\bm{\beta}})D(\bm{\gamma})^{-1}S+(I-D(\hat{\bm{\beta}}))SD(\bm{\gamma})^{-1}-(I-D(\hat{\bm{\beta}}))SD(\bm{\gamma})^{-1}S.

Now using

SD(\bm{\beta})=D(\sigma(\bm{\beta})S,

where
\sigma(\bm{\beta})_{j}=\beta_{j+1}
, we can write the right sides of (
A.5
) and (
A.6
) as quadratic polynomials in
S
, and after comparing coefficients
we find that (
A.4
) is equivalent to the system

\begin{cases}D(\hat{\bm{\gamma}})^{-1}D(\bm{\beta})=D(\hat{\bm{\beta}})D(\bm{\gamma})^{-1}\\
-D(\hat{\bm{\gamma}})^{-1}D(\sigma(\bm{\beta}))+D(\hat{\bm{\gamma}})^{-1}(I-D(\bm{\beta}))=-D(\hat{\bm{\beta}})D(\bm{\gamma})^{-1}+(I-D(\hat{\bm{\beta}}))D(\sigma(\bm{\gamma}))^{-1}\\
D(\hat{\bm{\gamma}})^{-1}(I-D(\sigma(\bm{\beta})))=(I-D(\hat{\bm{\beta}}))D(\sigma(\bm{\gamma}))^{-1}.\end{cases}

These three equations can be reduced to two, since the second equation is the difference between the third and first equations. From the latter we obtain (where multiplication between two vectors is taken componentwise)

(A.7)

\begin{cases}\hat{\bm{\gamma}}^{-1}\bm{\beta}=\hat{\bm{\beta}}\bm{\gamma}^{-1},\\
\hat{\gamma}^{-1}(1-\sigma(\bm{\beta}))=(1-\hat{\bm{\beta}})\sigma(\bm{\gamma})^{-1},\end{cases}

which we can rewrite as

(A.8)

\begin{cases}\bm{\beta}\bm{\gamma}=\hat{\bm{\beta}}\hat{\bm{\gamma}},\\
(1-\sigma(\bm{\beta}))\sigma(\bm{\gamma})=(1-\hat{\bm{\beta}})\hat{\bm{\gamma}}.\end{cases}

Substituting the first into the second gives

\begin{cases}\bm{\beta}\bm{\gamma}=\hat{\bm{\beta}}\hat{\bm{\gamma}},\\
(1-\sigma(\bm{\beta}))\sigma(\bm{\gamma})+\bm{\beta}\bm{\gamma}=\hat{\bm{\gamma}}.\end{cases}

From here it is easy to see that
\hat{\bm{\gamma}}
and
\hat{\bm{\beta}}
are as in (
A.3
), which finishes the proof.
∎

By iteratively applying this lemma, we see that we can rewrite
\Phi_{\ell}
and
\Psi_{\ell}
as in the following lemma.

Lemma A.2
.

We can write
\Phi_{\ell}
and
\Psi_{\ell}
as

(A.9)

\Phi_{\ell}=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell-1}\psi(\bm{\gamma}_{1}^{[n-i]})\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\phi(\bm{\beta}_{i}^{[n-\ell+i]})

and

(A.10)

\Psi_{\ell}=\prod_{i=\ell}^{n}\psi(\bm{\gamma}_{\ell+1}^{[n+\ell-i]})\prod_{i=\ell+1}^{n}\phi(\bm{\beta}_{i}^{[i-1]})

Here the weights
\bm{\beta}_{i}^{[k]}
and
\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{[k]}
are defined as
18
18

18

Note that not all the updated parameters appear in (
A.9
) and (
A.10
). In particular, note that only
\bm{\gamma}_{1}^{[k]}
appears in
\Phi_{\ell}
, and no other
\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{[k]}
. Similarly, only
\bm{\gamma}_{\ell+1}^{[k]}
appears in
\Psi_{\ell}
.

(A.11)

\begin{cases}\gamma_{i,j}^{[k-1]}&=\beta_{i,j}^{[k]}\gamma_{i+1,j}^{[k]}+(1-\beta_{i,j+1}^{[k]})\gamma_{i+1,j+1}^{[k]}\\
\beta_{i,j}^{[k-1]}&=\frac{\beta_{i,j}^{[k]}\gamma_{i+1,j}^{[k]}}{\beta_{i,j}^{[k]}\gamma_{i+1,j}^{[k]}+(1-\beta_{i,j+1}^{[k]})\gamma_{i+1,j+1}^{[k]}}\end{cases}.

Proof.

By Lemma
A.1
and (
A.11
) we have

\phi(\bm{\beta}_{i}^{[k]})\psi(\bm{\gamma}_{i+1}^{[k]})=\psi(\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{[k-1]})\phi(\bm{\beta}_{i}^{[k-1]}).

By applying this rule iteratively (in arbitrary order) we can reorder the product in the definition of
\Phi_{\ell}
and
\Psi_{\ell}
such that all
\psi
’s come first followed by all the
\phi
’s. The result is then as stated in the formula.
∎

The reordering in (
A.9
) and (
A.10
) is not just algebraic, but also has an combinatorial interpretation of switching a type I with a type II column. This leads us to define the graph
\tilde{G}_{LGV}^{n,\ell}=(\tilde{V}_{LGV}^{n,\ell},\tilde{E}_{LGV}^{n,\ell})
as follows. We start by gluing
\ell-1
columns of type II, followed by
\ell
columns of type I, then
n-\ell+1
columns of type II, and, finally,
n-\ell
columns of type I. See also Figure
40
.
The weights
\tilde{w}(e)
that we will put on the edges are determined as follows:

\tilde{w}((i,j)\to(i+1,k))=\left(\psi(\gamma_{1}^{[n-i-1]})\right)_{j,k}

for
i=0,\ldots,\ell-2
, and

\tilde{w}((\ell+i,j)\to(\ell+i+1,k))=\left(\phi(\beta_{i+1}^{[n-\ell+j+1]})\right)_{j,k}

for
i=0,\ldots,\ell-1
,
and

\tilde{w}((2\ell+i,j)\to(2\ell+i+1,k))=\left(\psi(\gamma_{\ell+1}^{[n-i]})\right)_{j,k}

for
i=0,\ldots,n-\ell
, and

\tilde{w}((n+\ell+i,j)\to(n+\ell+i+1,k))=\left(\phi(\beta_{\ell+i+1}^{[\ell+i]})\right)_{j,k}

for
i=0,\ldots,n-\ell-1
. The
\tilde{G}_{LGV}^{n,\ell}
is the analogue of the graph
G^{v-swap}_{n,\ell}
.

(0,-1)
(2n,-n-1)
(0,-2)
(2n,-n-2)
(0,-n+\ell-1)
(2n,-2n+\ell-1)
(0,-n+\ell-2)
(2n,-2n+\ell-2)
\vdots
\vdots
(0,-N)
(2n,-N-n)

Figure 40
.
Illustration of the non-intersecting paths on the graph
\tilde{G}_{LGV}^{n,\ell}
(here
\ell=3
and
n=5
). The graph
\tilde{G}_{LGV}^{n,\ell}
is the analogue of
G^{v-swap}_{n,\ell}
.

We consider again non-intersecting paths
\{\tilde{\pi}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
starting at
(0,-j-n)
and ending at
(2n,-j)
for
j=1,\ldots,N
, but now on the graph
\tilde{G}_{LGV}^{n,\ell}
. The following is the equivalent of Proposition
4.4
.

Proposition A.3
.

Fix
\ell\in\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\}
. Then the joint of law of
\{\pi_{j}(2\ell-1)\}_{j=1}^{N}
and the joint law of
\{\tilde{\pi}_{j}(2\ell-1)\}_{j=1}^{N}
are the same.

Proof.

This is a triviality since the LGV Theorem states that both laws are given by (
A.2
) (but based on different factorizations of
\Phi_{\ell}
and
\Psi_{\ell}
.)
∎

A.5.
Identifying and removing frozen parts

An important feature of the graph
G^{v\text{-}swap}_{n,\ell}
was that every matching was frozen in the first
\ell-1
and last
n-\ell
columns, as stated in Lemma
4.5
.
Similarly, we show that for every configuration of non-intersecting paths on
\tilde{G}_{LGV}^{n,\ell}
, the top paths are frozen in the first
\ell-1
columns and the last
n-\ell
columns. In other words, these paths must necessarily be straight horizontal lines until we arrive at the type I columns. Whereas Lemma
4.5
followed from the fact that there were
1
-valent vertices, in the case of non-intersecting paths this is due to a blocking principle given by the following inequality relation on the type II columns:

(A.12)

\tilde{\pi}_{j}(k)\geq\tilde{\pi}_{j+1}(k-1)+1,

for
j=1,\ldots,N-1
and

k=1,\ldots,\ell-1
, and
k=2\ell,\ldots,n+\ell
.

The following is then the equivalent of
Lemma

4.5
.

Lemma A.4
.

Let
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
N\geq n
, and consider a configuration of non-intersecting path
\{\tilde{\pi}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
on the graph
\tilde{G}_{LGV}^{n,\ell}
.
For
j=1,\ldots,N-n+1
, the path
\tilde{\pi}_{j}
consists of a straight horizontal line from
(0,-j)
to
(\ell-1,-j)
, then a path from
(\ell-1,-j)
to
(n+\ell,-j-\ell)
, and then a straight diagonal line from
(n+\ell,-j-\ell)
to
(2n,-j-n)
.

Proof.

We first sketch the idea. By the blocking principle (
A.12
) for the type II columns, only the
\ell-1
paths in the bottom can move (in the first step only the bottommost path can jump, and if it does then it gives space for the next path to move in the second step, etc.). The top
N-\ell+1
paths have to be frozen in these first columns and, consequently, the path
\tilde{\pi}_{j}
cannot reach a position below
-j-\ell
after passing through the next
\ell
columns of type I for
1\leq j\leq N-\ell+1
. Then again by the fact that type II columns have the blocking principle (
A.12
), the top paths are restricted by these positions at the vertical slice
2\ell-1
giving a lower bound for their positions at slice
n+\ell
. This lower bound is tight enough to ensure that the only way to end up at the endpoint at slice
2n
is that this lower bound at slice
n+\ell
is attained and that the paths continue diagonally down towards their final destinations. The proof below formalizes this argument.

From (
A.12
) we see that, for
j=1,\ldots,N-\ell+1
and
k=1,\ldots,\ell-1
,

\tilde{\pi}_{j}(k)\geq\tilde{\pi}_{j+k}(0)+k=-j,

where we used the initial condition in the last step. But since paths only jump down and start at
\tilde{\pi}_{j}(0)=-j
, we also have
\tilde{\pi}_{j}(k)\leq-j
. We thus find

\tilde{\pi}_{j}(k)=-j,

for all
j=1,\ldots,N-\ell+1
and
k=1,\ldots,\ell-1
. The top
N-\ell+1
paths are therefore straight lines in the first
\ell-1
columns.

Now we show that the last
n-\ell
steps in the paths
\tilde{\pi}_{j}
with
j=1,\ldots,n-\ell+1
have to be diagonal straight lines, which is somewhat harder to see from the illustrations.
We first note that from the first half of this proof, we also find

(A.13)

\tilde{\pi}_{j}(2\ell-1)\geq-j-\ell,

for
j=1\ldots,N-\ell+1
, since the paths can step at most
1
down at each step between
\ell-1
and
2\ell-1
.
Now, note from (
A.12
) that

\tilde{\pi}_{j}(2\ell-1+k)\geq\tilde{\pi}_{j+k}(2\ell-1)+k,

for
j=1,\ldots,N-k
and
k=1,\ldots,n-\ell+1
. Combining the last two inequalities, we find

\tilde{\pi}_{j}(2\ell-1+k)\geq-j-\ell,

for
j=1,\ldots,N-n+1
and
k=1,\ldots,n-\ell+1
.
Finally, since the last
n-\ell
steps of the paths can go down at most
1
, and since the paths end at
(2n,-j-n)
we must have

\tilde{\pi}_{j}(n+\ell)\leq-j-\ell.

for all
j=1\ldots,N
. Therefore

(A.14)

\tilde{\pi}_{j}(n+\ell)=-j-\ell.

for
j=1,\ldots,N-n+1
, and the only way these paths can end up from here at
(2n,-j-n)
is by diagonal straight lines. ∎

We are not done yet. There are further parts of the path configurations that are frozen and this will be important for us. We start with the following lemma, which says that the middle paths are all frozen, not only in the first
\ell-1
columns of type II, but also in the subsequent collection of type I columns. Note that this only holds for sufficiently large
N
, and we therefore upgrade our assumption and take
19
19

19

Assuming
N\geq 2n
is sufficient for all upcoming claims, but part of these claims will hold under weaker bounds on
N
. Since this is however not relevant for our purposes, we will make the overall assumption
N\geq 2n
.

N\geq 2n
.

Lemma A.5
.

Let
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and take
N\geq 2n
.
The positions
\tilde{\pi}_{n-\ell+1+j}(k)
for
j=n-\ell+2,n-\ell+3,\ldots,N-n+1
and
k\in\{0,\ldots,2\ell-1\}
are fully frozen. More precisely, up to the vertical line with horizontal coordinate
2\ell-1
the path
\tilde{\pi}_{j}
consists of a straight horizontal line from
(0,-j)
to
(\ell-1,-j)
, then a straight diagonal line to
(2\ell-1,-j+\ell)
, for
j=n-\ell+2,n-\ell+3,\ldots,N-n+1
.

Proof.

Note that we already know from the previous lemma that, for
j\leq N-n+1
, the paths have horizontal straight parts from
(0,-j)
to
(\ell-1,-j)
. It remains to show that they continue as diagonal straight lines, if we further assume
j\geq n-\ell+2
.

We first consider the location of
\tilde{\pi}_{n-\ell+2}(2\ell-1).
First note that by (
A.13
) we have
\tilde{\pi}_{n-\ell+2}(2\ell-1)\geq-n-2
. On the other hand, combining this inequality repeatedly with (
A.12
) we find

(A.15)

-n-2\leq\tilde{\pi}_{n-\ell+2}(2\ell-1)\leq\tilde{\pi}_{n-\ell+1}(2\ell)-1\leq\tilde{\pi}_{1}(n+\ell)-n+\ell-1=-n-2,

where we used (
A.14
) in the last step. Therefore, we have
\tilde{\pi}_{n-\ell+2}(2\ell)=-n-2.
This means in particular that
\tilde{\pi}_{n-\ell+2}
from
(\ell-1,-n+\ell-2)
has to continue as diagonal straight line to
(2\ell-1,-n-2)
.

By the non-intersecting condition we then also see that all
\tilde{\pi}_{j}
with
j=n-\ell+2,\ldots,N-n+1
have to be straight diagonal lines. This finishes the proof.
∎

Corollary A.6
.

Let
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and take
N\geq 2n
. The paths
\tilde{\pi}_{j}
, with
j=n-\ell+1,\ldots,N-2n+\ell-1
are fully frozen and given by a straight horizontal paths in type II columns and straight diagonal lines in type I columns.

Proof.

For the first
\ell-1
type II columns and first
\ell
type I columns this is (part of) Lemma
A.5
. But this means also that, by the blocking principle (
A.12
) and the fact that the middle paths have consecutive locations at slice
2\ell-1
, the middle paths with index
j=n-\ell+1,\ldots,N-2n+\ell
, must be frozen in the next
n-\ell+1
type II columns. It also implies that the only way to end up at their final destinations is by taking straight diagonal lines in the remain
n-\ell
columns of type I.
∎

We have thus proven that the collection of paths
\tilde{\pi}_{j}
can be divided in a top, middle and bottom block. The middle block is fully frozen and the top and bottom have randomness. But the fact that the middle block is frozen also implies that the top and bottom blocks are independent.

We also need the following lemma describing frozen points that the top paths have to go through.

Lemma A.7
.

For
j=1,\ldots,n-\ell+1
, the path
\tilde{\pi}_{j}
has to pass through the point

(n+\ell+1-j,-\ell-j)
.

Proof.

Combining
\tilde{\pi}_{n-\ell+2}(2\ell-1)=-n-2
from (
A.15
) with (
A.12
) yields

\tilde{\pi}_{n-\ell+2-k}(2\ell+k-1)\geq-n-2+k,

for
k=0,\ldots,n-\ell+1
. On the other hand, applying (
A.12
) starting from
\pi_{1}(n+\ell)=-1-\ell
, gives

\tilde{\pi}_{n-\ell+2-k}(2\ell+k-1)\leq-n-2+k.

for
k=0,\ldots,n-\ell+1
. Combining the two inequalities gives an equality, and after inserting
k=n-\ell+2-j
we find the statement.
∎

Remark A.2
.

We find it useful to think of the limit
N\to\infty
. Then we will have an infinite number of paths but for
j\geq n-\ell+2
the path but the only non-frozen parts of the paths lie fully inside the region that is cut out by the red point in Figure
40
. This limit should be taken with some care, and this is why we formulate the results for finite
N
.

A.6.
Relation to polymer measure

Lemmas
A.5
and
A.7
tell us that the part of the paths inside region of the graph that is cut out by the frozen locations indicated by the red dots in Figure
40
is independent from the parts of the paths outside this region. Since this region contains precisely the paths that we are interested in, and in particular the locations
\tilde{\pi}_{j}(2\ell-1)
, we eliminate all other parts from the graph. What remains is a graph, denoted by
tG^{LGV}_{n,\ell}
, consisting of (parts of)
\ell
columns of type I and then (parts of)
n-\ell+1
columns of type II. We will place this graph such that the top-left coordinate is
(-\ell,-1)
and then consider
n-\ell+1
non-intersecting paths
\hat{\pi}_{j}
that start at
(-\ell,-j)
and end at
(n-\ell+1-j,-\ell-j)
. See also Figure
41
.

Summarizing, we have obtained the following proposition.

Proposition A.8
.

Let
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
N\geq 2n-\ell+1
. Then the laws of
\{\pi(2\ell-1)\}_{j=1}^{n-\ell+1}
and
\{\hat{\pi}_{j}(\ell)\}_{j=1}^{n-\ell+1}
are the same.

This is the path translation of
4.10
.

Note that each of the paths has to end with a final horizontal edge. By removing these edges, which does not change the probability measure elsewhere, we obtain the same hybrid polymer models on
G^{\beta\Gamma}_{\ldots}
from
Section

4
.

A.7.
Concluding remarks

The above is an alternative construction to relate dimer configuration of the Aztec diamond to
\beta
-
\Gamma
polymer, that fits with existing recent literature connecting spider moves to matrix refactorizations. One downside of this construction is the introduction of auxilliary paths and having to choose
N
large enough (often one takes
N\to\infty
). The column swaps in
Section

4
do not require additional paths, and we have therefore chosen to keep that as our main construction in this paper.

Figure 41
.
The left picture shows the non-frozen parts of paths
\tilde{\pi}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\pi}_{n-\ell+1}
on
\tilde{G}^{LGV}_{n,\ell}
when
n=5,\ell=3
. Note that, after removing the
1
-valent edges on the right, this graph is exactly the underlying graph
G^{\beta\Gamma}_{3,3}
from
4.5
shown on the right, and the weights will also have the same distribution if we make the choice (
A.1
).

Appendix B
Deterministic limit to Fock’s weights

We recall from (
1.30
) that in the limit
T\to\infty
the random weights for the Gamma disordered Aztec diamond with general parameters become deterministic and are given by

(B.1)

a_{i,j}^{[n]}=\psi_{j}+\theta_{i},\qquad b_{i,j}^{[n]}=\phi_{j-n}-\theta_{i}.

The purpose of this appendix is to show that these weights are a special case of Fock’s weights in the genus 0 case
[
13
]
. They also appear in the context of critical weights for isoradial graphs
[
50
]
. Note that if
\theta_{i}=0
, then these are exactly the general Schur process weights on the Aztec diamond; one can also get this family by taking all
\psi_{j}\equiv\psi
and
\phi_{j}\equiv\phi
but leaving the
\theta_{i}
distinct. We thank Cédric Boutillier for helpful comments related to this appendix.

\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{2}
\alpha_{3}
\beta_{1}
\beta_{2}
\beta_{3}
\gamma_{3}
\gamma_{2}
\gamma_{1}
\delta_{3}
\delta_{2}
\delta_{1}
x
y
weight
=|x-y|

Figure 42
.
A rail yard graph and the corresponding edge weights given in
B.1
.

Definition B.1
.

Fix sequences
(\alpha_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
,
(\gamma_{j})_{j=1}^{n}
,
(\beta_{k})_{k=1}^{n}
,
(\delta_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{n}
of real numbers such that

\alpha_{i}<\gamma_{j}<\beta_{k}<\delta_{\ell}\qquad\text{for all }1\leq i,j,k,\ell\leq n.

We draw the tracks of the rail yard graph on the Aztec diamond as in Figure
42
.
There are vertical and horizontal tracks, alternating in direction.
We label the horizontal tracks directed to the left by
\alpha_{j}
and those directed to the right by
\beta_{j}
.
The vertical tracks directed upward and downward are labeled
\gamma_{i}
and
\delta_{i}
, respectively.
Note that the intersection point of any horizontal and vertical track lies on an edge.
We then define the weight of each edge to be the absolute value of the difference of the labels of the two intersecting tracks, as indicated in Figure
42
.

We note that the parameters
\psi_{j},\phi_{j},\theta_{i}
in (
B.1
) can be changed by taking

(B.2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\phi_{j}&\mapsto\phi_{j}+c\\
\psi_{j}&\mapsto\psi_{j}-c\\
\theta_{i}&\mapsto\theta_{i}+c\end{split}

without affecting the edge weights. Since one must have
\phi_{j}>\theta_{i}
for each
i,j
for positivity already, this means that without loss of generality one may take

(B.3)

\phi_{j}>\theta_{i}>\psi_{j}\quad\quad\quad\text{ for all }i,j\in\mathbb{Z}.

Proposition B.1
.

Fix
n
and let
\mathbb{P}_{\infty}
be the dimer measure on the Aztec diamond
G_{n}^{\Az}
with weights as in (
B.1
) for parameters satisfying (
B.3
). Let
\mathbb{P}_{\delta}
be the dimer measure on the same Aztec diamond defined by
B.1
with
\delta_{j}=\delta
,
\gamma_{i}=\theta_{i}
,
\alpha_{j}=-\psi_{j}
and
\beta_{j}=\phi_{j-n}
, for any real
\delta
large enough to satisfy the condition of
B.1
. Then

\lim_{\delta\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\delta}=\mathbb{P}_{\infty}

as functions on the finite set
\mathcal{M}
of perfect matchings.

Proof.

It is well known
20
20

20

One way to see this is that any two dimer configurations can be obtained from one another by a sequence of moves where each move replaces the edges around a given face of the graph by their complement. The change in the weight of the dimer configuration for each flip corresponds to multiplying or dividing by the corresponding face weight.
that the dimer measure is determined by the face weights. Hence, we need only show that the face weights defined by the Fock weights converge to the face weights defined by the weights (
B.1
). Now let us define these face weights.

Note that there are two types of faces for the Aztec diamond: the even faces, that have black vertices at the left and right, and the odd faces, that have the black vertices on top and bottom. For the faces, we associate weights
F
according to

(B.4)

x

z
w
F
F=\frac{xw}{yz}

for the even faces, and

(B.5)

x
y
z
w
F
F=\frac{yz}{xw}

for the odd faces.

For the weights yielding
\mathbb{P}_{\infty}
, we get face weights

(B.6)

a_{i,j}^{[n]}
1
b_{i,j}^{[n]}
1
F
F=\frac{a_{i,j}^{[n]}}{b_{i,j}^{[n]}}=\frac{\psi_{j}+\theta_{i}}{\phi_{j-n}-\theta_{i}}

The corresponding face weights for
\mathbb{P}_{\delta}
are given by

(B.8)

\gamma_{i}-\alpha_{j}
\delta_{i}-\alpha_{j}
\beta_{j}-\gamma_{i}
\delta_{i}-\beta_{j}
F
F=\frac{(\gamma_{i}-\alpha_{j})(\delta_{i}-\beta_{j})}{(\delta_{i}-\alpha_{j})(\beta_{j}-\gamma_{i})}

Under our identification
\delta_{i}=\delta
,
\gamma_{i}=\theta_{i}
,
\alpha_{j}=-\psi_{j}
and
\beta_{j}=\phi_{j-n}
, as
\delta\to\infty
the weight in (
B.8
) converges to the one in (
B.6
), and similarly (
B.9
) converges to (
B.7
). This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark B.1
.

In the literature, the parameters
\alpha_{j},\beta_{j},\gamma_{j},\delta_{j}
are more commonly taken to lie on the unit circle rather than on the real line. However, there is a remarkable principle: if one applies any Möbius transformation to these parameters, one obtains a new weight that is
gauge equivalent
to the original one. Consequently, we may apply a Möbius transformation that maps the unit circle to the real line. See also
[
13
, Remark 8]
and
[
49
, Section 5]
.
